### [1] [Behavior-Induced Mirror-Prox Temporal-Difference Learning for Faster Off-Policy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28849)
*Xingguo Chen,Yuchen Shen,Shangdong Yang,Chao Li,Guang Yang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes STHTD-MP, a Mirror-Prox TD method that replaces the feature covariance metric with a behavior-induced metric (symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix), and provides a convergence analysis showing it can achieve smaller mean contraction than GTD2-MP when the new metric improves saddle-point geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Mirror-Prox TD methods use the feature covariance metric, but the geometry induced by the auxiliary-variable metric strongly affects practical performance. Hybrid TD methods suggest behavior-policy transition information can provide a more informative update geometry, motivating the replacement of the covariance metric with a behavior-induced one.

Method: The paper proposes STHTD-MP, which replaces the covariance metric in the primal-dual saddle-point formulation with the symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix, keeps a single learning rate for primal and auxiliary variables, and applies Mirror-Prox prediction-correction steps to the resulting hybrid saddle-point operator.

Result: Formal convergence analysis is provided under standard stochastic approximation assumptions. The behavior-induced metric is positive definite, the joint mean system is Hurwitz, boundedness follows from a Lyapunov argument, and stochastic recursion converges by the ODE method. Projected-oracle ergodic gap bounds and exact mean-operator comparison with GTD2-MP via spectral radius of the deterministic Mirror-Prox error matrix are derived. STHTD-MP can have a smaller mean contraction factor than GTD2-MP. Numerical analysis on two-state, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain benchmarks supports the condition; Baird's counterexample is a singular boundary case where assumptions fail.

Conclusion: The behavior-induced metric can improve saddle-point geometry and lead to better convergence properties than the standard covariance metric used in GTD2-MP, though strict assumptions limit applicability in singular cases like Baird's counterexample.

Abstract: Gradient temporal-difference methods provide stable off-policy prediction with linear function approximation, but their practical performance is strongly affected by the geometry induced by the auxiliary-variable metric. Existing Mirror-Prox TD methods typically use the feature covariance metric, whereas hybrid TD methods suggest that behavior-policy transition information can provide a more informative update geometry. This paper proposes a behavior-induced Mirror-Prox temporal-difference method, called STHTD-MP, which replaces the covariance metric in the primal-dual saddle-point formulation with the symmetric part of the behavior-policy Bellman matrix. The method keeps a single learning rate for the primal and auxiliary variables and applies a Mirror-Prox prediction-correction step to the resulting hybrid saddle-point operator. We provide a formal convergence analysis for fixed-policy linear prediction under standard stochastic approximation assumptions: the behavior-induced metric is positive definite, the joint mean system is Hurwitz, boundedness follows from a Lyapunov argument, and the stochastic recursion converges by the ODE method. We further derive projected-oracle ergodic gap bounds and an exact mean-operator comparison with GTD2-MP based on the spectral radius of the deterministic Mirror-Prox error matrix. The analysis shows that STHTD-MP can have a smaller mean contraction factor than GTD2-MP when the behavior-induced metric improves the saddle-point geometry. Exact numerical mean-operator analysis on two-state, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain benchmarks supports this condition, while Baird's counterexample is identified as a singular boundary case where the strict assumptions fail.

</details>


### [2] [Behavior-Aware Auxiliary Corrections for Off-Policy Temporal-Difference Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28855)
*Xingguo Chen,Zhiang He,Yuchen Shen,Shangdong Yang,Chao Li,Guang Yang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes BA-TDC and BA-TDRC, which replace the auxiliary covariance matrix in TDC/TDRC with a behavior-aware Bellman matrix for off-policy TD learning, providing theoretical convergence guarantees and showing that while the behavior-aware replacement alone helps some tasks, regularization is necessary for robust performance across harder settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-policy temporal-difference learning with function approximation suffers from instability. TDC and TDRC address this via auxiliary covariance correction, but the role of behavior-aware geometry vs. regularization remains unclear. The paper aims to disentangle these contributions and design better auxiliary geometries for stable off-policy TD learning.

Method: Replace the TDC auxiliary covariance matrix (C) with the behavior Bellman matrix (A_μ) to obtain BA-TDC, then regularize to obtain BA-TDRC. Provides finite-state mean-system formulation, proves fixed-point preservation and almost-sure convergence under Hurwitz stability, and compares rates via spectral radius analysis.

Result: On two-state counterexample, Baird's counterexample, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain, behavior-aware replacement alone is beneficial on some tasks, but regularization is necessary for robust performance across harder settings.

Conclusion: The separation of behavior-aware geometry from regularization reveals that each contributes distinct benefits: behavior-aware geometry improves convergence on simpler tasks, while regularization ensures stability and robustness in more challenging off-policy settings.

Abstract: Temporal-difference learning with function approximation can be unstable under off-policy sampling. TDC stabilizes off-policy TD through an auxiliary covariance correction, and TDRC further regularizes this correction in a single-timescale recursion. This paper studies a behavior-aware replacement of the auxiliary covariance geometry in the linear prediction setting, which is the standard local model for understanding the feature-space dynamics of value-function approximation. We first replace the TDC auxiliary matrix (C) by the behavior Bellman matrix (A_μ), yielding BA-TDC, and then regularize the same behavior-aware equation to obtain BA-TDRC. This two-step construction separates the contribution of behavior-aware geometry from the contribution of regularization. The linear analysis also provides a tractable model for an auxiliary-geometry design question that arises in neural-network value approximation, where feature covariances and temporal transition matrices jointly shape the last-layer correction dynamics. We give a finite-state mean-system formulation, prove fixed-point preservation and almost-sure convergence under a Hurwitz stability condition on the instantiated mean system, and compare deterministic mean rates through the spectral radius of the exact linear error recursion. Experiments on the two-state counterexample, Baird's counterexample, Random Walk, and Boyan Chain show that the behavior-aware replacement can be highly beneficial by itself on some tasks, but that regularization is necessary for robust performance across harder settings.

</details>


### [3] [The Cognitive Categorical Transformer: Category-Theoretic Inductive Biases for Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28864)
*Al Kari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Cognitive Categorical Transformer (CCT) augments GPT-2 Small (306M params) with category-theoretic components, achieving 21.27 validation perplexity on WikiText-103 vs 24.19 for fine-tuned GPT-2 Small (12% reduction). Ablations localize 84% of improvement to simplicial message passing (GT-Full). The study establishes a structure/consistency distinction: topological priors help, consistency priors don't.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether cognitively grounded categorical components—specifically simplicial message passing and other category-theoretic priors—can improve language model perplexity beyond what standard in-domain fine-tuning provides, and to isolate which components are responsible for gains.

Method: CCT augments a frozen GPT-2 Small backbone (306M params) with GT-Full simplicial message passing and several categorical priors (sheaf smoothing, adjunction round-trip, curvature regularization, PrecisionWeightedPP). Trained on WikiText-103 under a matched-step protocol (215k steps, matched data/optimizer/schedule). Ablation: retrain from scratch with GT-Full bypassed across all seven activation phases.

Result: CCT reaches 21.27 validation PPL vs 24.19 for fine-tuned GPT-2 Small (2.92 PPL / 12% reduction). GT-Full ablation yields 23.72 PPL, attributing 84% (2.45 of 2.92 PPL) to simplicial message passing. Sheaf smoothing, adjunction round-trip, and curvature regularization produce negative results.

Conclusion: Simplicial message passing improves language-model perplexity at 306M scale on WikiText-103, validated by ablation. The structure/consistency distinction emerges: categorical priors adding new topology improve modeling; those enforcing consistency identities do not.

Abstract: The Cognitive Categorical Transformer (CCT) is a 306M-parameter architecture that augments a pretrained GPT-2 Small backbone with cognitively grounded components derived from category theory and several inspirations from cognitive science. Under a matched-step protocol (215,000 optimizer steps, matched data, matched optimizer and schedule) on WikiText-103, CCT reaches 21.27 validation perplexity, compared with 24.19 for an identically fine-tuned GPT-2 Small baseline. The architecture therefore contributes a 2.92 PPL (12% relative) reduction beyond what in-domain fine-tuning alone provides. A retrain-from-scratch ablation that holds GT-Full simplicial message passing bypassed across the entire seven-phase activation schedule reaches 23.72 PPL, localizing 84% of the architectural improvement (2.45 of 2.92 PPL) to GT-Full. We present the first ablation-validated evidence that simplicial message passing improves language-model perplexity at the 306M-parameter scale on WikiText-103. Published GPT-2 Large reaches 22.05 zero-shot PPL on WikiText-103 with 6.2x more parameters than GPT-2 Small; this paper treats that number as an external published reference, not as the architectural benchmark. Three negative results on consistency-style categorical priors (sheaf smoothing, adjunction round-trip, curvature regularization) and the joint structural-prior result for GT-Full and PrecisionWeightedPP together support an empirical pattern termed the *structure/consistency distinction*, in which categorical priors that add new topology improve language modeling and those that enforce a consistency identity do not.

</details>


### [4] [Ultra-Reduced-Impact-Encased-Logging (URIEL): propose a new method for selective sustainable logging and post-harvest silvicultural treatment in tropical forest using airborne robotics systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28883)
*Daniel Albiero,Gelton Fernando de Morais,Daniela Han,Flávio Roberto de Freitas Gonçalves,Artur Vitório Andrade Santos,Wesllen Lins de Araújo,Alessandra Maia Freire,Cláudio Kiyoshi Umezu,Mateus Peressin,Francesco Toscano,Admilson Írio Ribeiro,Alfeu J. Sguarezi Filho,Américo Ferraz Dias Neto,Angel Pontin Garcia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes URIEL, a novel tropical forest logging method combining heli-logging, robotics, AI, and drone-based post-harvest silviculture. Digital simulations show high economic viability and near-zero collateral forest damage, but real-world feasibility depends on stakeholder integration across industry, government, companies, and indigenous communities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tropical forests face intense deforestation driven by economic and political interests, with scientific evidence linking this deforestation to climate change. Current logging methods cause excessive collateral damage, motivating the need for a more sustainable approach.

Method: The URIEL method combines heli-logging techniques with robotics and AI, plus post-harvest silvicultural treatments performed by drones. The authors developed equipment dimensions, created a digital proof of concept, and ran digital simulations with economic feasibility analysis across various helicopter-timber-distance combinations.

Result: The URIEL method demonstrates high economic viability and virtually eliminates collateral damage to forests while maintaining ecosystem services.

Conclusion: Despite satisfactory scientific and technological results, the feasibility of URIEL depends on the integration of four key stakeholder groups: high-tech industry, political governments, certified logging companies, and native populations.

Abstract: Tropical forests worldwide are under intense deforestation pressure driven by economic and political interests, and scientific evidence suggests this deforestation contributes to climate change. This paper proposes a novel logging method for tropical forests, Ultra-Reduced-Impact-Encased-Logging (URIEL). This new method is based on heli-logging techniques combined with intensive use of robotics and AI integrated with post-harvest silvicultural treatments performed by drones. The concept of appropriate equipment for this method was developed, dimensions were determined, details were completed in a digital proof of concept, and an effective digital simulation and economic feasibility analysis were carried out for various helicopter-timber-distance combinations. The results demonstrated that a URIEL method has high economic viability and makes it possible to virtually eliminate collateral damage to forests while maintaining ecosystem services. The main conclusion of this paper is that, despite the satisfactory scientific and technological results, the feasibility of a Uriel method depends on the integration of stakeholders intrinsic to the context: high-tech industry; political governments; certified logging companies; and native populations.

</details>


### [5] [Review Arcade: On the Human Alignment and Gameability of LLM Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28897)
*Hans Ole Hatzel,Sebastian Steindl,Jan Strich*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates LLM-generated reviews for scientific papers from both reviewer and author perspectives using ACL Rolling Review data, finding limited alignment with human reviews that varies by prompt/model, and showing authors can "game" LLM reviews through iterative revision to achieve statistically significant score increases for up to 35% of papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-generated reviews are being officially piloted by major conferences and both reviewers and authors may use LLM assistance, there is a need to understand the alignment between LLM and human reviews and the potential for authors to exploit LLM-based review systems.

Method: Empirical experiments on papers from the 2025 ACL Rolling Review (ARR), evaluating LLM reviews from both reviewer and author perspectives, testing multiple prompts and models, and simulating an iterative draft-revise workflow where authors improve submissions based on LLM feedback.

Result: Limited alignment between LLM and human reviews, with best-case reasonable but varying substantially across prompts and models. The iterative "gaming" workflow achieved statistically significant score increases for up to 35% of papers in specific scenarios.

Conclusion: LLM review alignment with humans is inconsistent and prompt/model-dependent; authors can exploit LLM reviews to boost scores, raising concerns about integrity of LLM-assisted review processes.

Abstract: LLM-generated reviews for scientific papers are gaining considerable traction and are even being officially piloted by major conferences. We have to assume that not only reviewers are using LLM-assistance, but also that authors use LLMs to revise their papers before submitting. In this work, we perform empirical experiments on papers from the 2025 ACL Rolling Review (ARR) to evaluate LLM reviews from both the author and the reviewer perspective. First, we identify a limited alignment of LLM reviews with human ones. In the best-case scenario, the alignment is reasonable. However, we also find that LLM-human alignment varies substantially across prompts and models. Finally, we investigate the scenario in which the author uses an iterative draft-revise workflow to improve the submission according to the LLM review. We find that this "gaming" of LLM reviews can be effective in specific scenarios, leading to a statistically significant increase of overall scores for up to 35\% of papers. We publish our code: https://github.com/uhh-hcds/reviewarcade.

</details>


### [6] [Orthogonal Concept Erasure for Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28902)
*Yuhao Sun,Lingyun Yu,Haoxiang Xu,Fengyuan Miao,Zhuoer Xu,Hongtao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Orthogonal Concept Erasure (OCE), a method that reformulates editing-based concept erasure in diffusion models as multiplicative orthogonal parameter updates, enabling precise concept removal while preserving generative capacity, scaling to 100 concepts in 4.3 seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing editing-based concept erasure methods rely on additive parameter updates that entangle neuron direction, magnitude, and angular geometry, causing unintended interference between concept erasure and overall generative performance.

Method: OCE reformulates editing-based erasure as multiplicative parameter updates using layer-wise orthogonal transformations derived from a closed-form solution. It also introduces subspace-level objective with structured subspace manipulation for multi-concept erasure.

Result: OCE outperforms existing methods in both single- and multi-concept erasure tasks, erasing up to 100 concepts in only 4.3 seconds while preserving non-target generative capacity.

Conclusion: Orthogonal transformations (multiplicative updates) are superior to additive updates for concept erasure as they disentangle direction, magnitude, and angular geometry, achieving both precise erasure and preservation of overall model capability.

Abstract: Concept erasure has emerged as a promising approach to mitigate undesired or unsafe content in diffusion models, yet existing methods still face significant limitations. While training-based methods are effective, their high computational cost limits scalability. Editing-based methods are more efficient and deployment-friendly, yet they struggle to simultaneously achieve precise concept erasure and preserve overall generative capacity. We identify this core limitation of the editing-based methods as reliance on additive parameter updates. Our empirical analysis reveals that concept semantics primarily depend on neuron direction rather than neuron magnitude, while overall generative capacity relies on the angular geometry of neurons. As additive updates inherently entangle direction, magnitude, and angular geometry, they inevitably introduce unintended interference between concept erasure and overall generation performance. To address this, we propose Orthogonal Concept Erasure (OCE), which reformulates editing-based erasure as multiplicative parameter updates from a geometric perspective. Specifically, OCE applies layer-wise orthogonal transformations derived from a closed-form solution to the parameters, enabling precise concept erasure while preserving the neuron magnitude and angular geometry. Furthermore, to address conflicting constraints in multi-concept erasure, OCE introduces a subspace-level objective with structured subspace manipulation, yielding a more effective and scalable erasure. Extensive experiments on single- and multi-concept erasure demonstrate that OCE outperforms existing methods in concept erasure and non-target preservation, erasing up to 100 concepts in 4.3 s. Code: https://github.com/HansSunY/OCE.

</details>


### [7] [Frontier LLM-based agents can overcome the ontology curation bottleneck for natural phenotypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28965)
*James P. Balhoff,Hilmar Lapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Five frontier LLMs (from Anthropic and OpenAI) were evaluated as "agentic curators" for phenotype annotation, matching or approaching inter-curator human performance and substantially outperforming the previous NLP tool (Semantic CharaParser).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phenotype annotation (linking free-text descriptions to ontology terms) is a labor-intensive bottleneck requiring highly trained experts, making it difficult to scale for cross-study integration of comparative morphological data.

Method: Five frontier LLMs (Anthropic and OpenAI) were deployed as "agentic curators" with access to source publication PDFs, annotation guides, four project ontologies (UBERON, PATO, BSPO, GO), and a validation script. Their Entity-Quality (EQ) annotations were evaluated against the Gold Standard from Dahdul et al. (2018) and compared to three human curators and Semantic CharaParser.

Result: Every LLM agent fell within the range of inter-curator variability of the three human biocurators. The best agents approached but did not surpass the best human curator. All agents substantially outperformed Semantic CharaParser on all four metrics.

Conclusion: LLM-based agentic curators can achieve performance comparable to human experts for phenotype annotation, offering a scalable alternative to the traditional labor-intensive process.

Abstract: Linking free-text phenotype descriptions to ontology terms, typically referred to as phenotype annotation, is essential for the cross-study integration of comparative morphological data. This labor intensive process has heavily relied on highly trained human experts, which makes it challenging to scale and thus a key bottleneck. Dahdul et al. (2018) established a Gold Standard (GS) of Entity-Quality (EQ) annotations across seven phylogenetic studies and used it to evaluate three human curators and the Semantic CharaParser NLP tool with ontology-based semantic similarity metrics; they reported that machine-human consistency was significantly lower than inter-curator (human-human) consistency. Here we revisit that benchmark with five frontier hosted LLMs from Anthropic and OpenAI, each operating as an "agentic curator" within a self-contained workspace that supplies the source publication PDF, the same annotation guide used by the original human curators, the four project ontologies (UBERON, PATO, BSPO, GO), and a validation script. Evaluated against the same Gold Standard, every agent fell within the range of inter-curator variability of the three trained human biocurators of the original study; the best performing agents approached but did not reach the best performing human curator. Agents substantially outperformed Semantic CharaParser on all four metrics.

</details>


### [8] [VFEAgent: A Multimodal Agent Framework for End-to-End Automated Finite Element Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28978)
*Jiachen Zhang,Junyi Lao,Chenghao Liu,Siyuan Liu,Shixin Wu,Linsen Zhang,Boyu Wang,Songfang Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VFEAgent is an end-to-end multi-agent system that automates Finite Element Analysis (FEA) from images and text descriptions using multimodal vision-language agents and verification-first code synthesis, achieving high success rates in generating physically valid simulations and outperforming LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FEA workflows are complex and require deep domain expertise; existing LLM-integrated approaches struggle with multimodal inputs and complex task execution, limiting automation.

Method: The system combines (1) a multimodal vision-language multi-agent pipeline with ReAct-driven reasoning to extract structured FEA specifications from images and text, and (2) a verification-first code synthesis framework with self-debugging and fallback mechanisms to ensure executability and physical validity.

Result: VFEAgent achieves high success rates in generating complete and physically valid simulations across various engineering mechanics scenarios, outperforming LLM-based baselines in reliability and correctness.

Conclusion: The framework validates the feasibility of fully automating the FEA workflow, offering potential to free engineers from tedious manual analysis tasks.

Abstract: Finite Element Analysis (FEA) serves as the cornerstone of modern engineering design. However, its workflow is inherently complex and relies heavily on domain expertise. Although recent efforts have integrated Large Language Models (LLMs) into FEA, existing approaches face limitations in handling multimodal inputs and executing complex tasks. To address these limitations, we propose VFEAgent, an end-to-end multi-agent system designed to automate FEA modeling and simulation directly from input images and problem descriptions. Our methodology integrates two core components: (1) a multimodal vision-language multi-agent pipeline that employs ReAct-driven reasoning to extract structured FEA specifications from heterogeneous inputs and (2) a verification-first code synthesis framework, incorporating robust self-debugging and fallback mechanisms to ensure executability and physical validity. We systematically evaluated the system across various engineering mechanics scenarios. The results demonstrate that VFEAgent achieves a high success rate in generating complete and physically valid simulations, outperforming LLM-based baseline methods in reliability and correctness. These findings validate the feasibility of automating the complete FEA workflow, highlighting the framework's potential to liberate engineers from tedious manual analysis.

</details>


### [9] [BEAMS: Benchmarking and Evaluating AI for Modeling and Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28994)
*Sara Metcalf,William Schoenberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The BEAMS Initiative establishes benchmarks for evaluating AI tools in modeling and simulation, focusing on human-centered, responsible AI. Their tests reveal that current AI tools perform better on qualitative tasks and discussion than on causal reasoning and quantitative error fixing, with no single LLM excelling across all tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI tools for real-world decision making must build simulation models that inform recommendations and remain interpretable, while complementing rather than replacing human expertise. The BEAMS Initiative aims to guide AI tool development toward responsible and ethical forms by establishing benchmarks for human-centered modeling practices.

Method: The initiative uses open digital and organizational infrastructure to collaboratively evaluate AI tools. A steering group prioritizes benchmarks, while a technical group implements them as automated tests. The open-source sd_ai project hosts evaluations across categories including causal translation, model iteration, causal reasoning, conformance, model behavior explanation, suggested model building steps, and suggested model fixes. Tests are applied to AI tools supporting qualitative model building, quantitative model building, and model discussion.

Result: When sd_ai engines are coupled with different LLMs, performance varies significantly. AI-enabled modeling tools perform better at discussion and basic qualitative tasks than at causal reasoning and quantitative error fixing. No single LLM dominates across all engine types; tradeoffs exist between speed and accuracy depending on specific tasks.

Conclusion: The implemented evaluations demonstrate variability across AI tools and highlight task-specific performance differences. Ongoing efforts aim to incorporate benchmarks addressing bias, alternative perspectives, and human-centered use cases to further guide responsible AI development in modeling and simulation.

Abstract: AI tools to support real world decision making must be able to build simulation models that inform their recommendations and render them interpretable. Tools that can automate aspects of modeling practice must complement human expertise, not replace it. The BEAMS Initiative aims to guide the development of AI tools for modeling and simulation toward forms that are responsible and ethical by establishing benchmarks for human centered modeling and simulation practices. The initiative uses open digital and organizational infrastructure to collaboratively evaluate AI tools for modeling and simulation. The open source sd ai project hosted by the initiative establishes transparency and enables contributions to be shared broadly. A steering group focuses on prioritizing potential benchmarks, while a technical group focuses on implementing the benchmarks in the form of automated tests. Tests for several distinct categories of evaluation have been implemented and applied to AI tools that support qualitative model building, quantitative model building, and model discussion. These include tests for causal translation, model iteration, causal reasoning, conformance, model behavior explanation, suggested model building steps, and suggested model fixes. When engines from the sd ai project are coupled with different LLMs, their performance on these evaluations reveals variability across different AI tools. The evaluations implemented by the initiative demonstrate that AI enabled modeling tools perform better at discussion and basic qualitative tasks than with causal reasoning and quantitative error fixing. No single LLM dominates across engine types, highlighting the importance of specific tasks and tradeoffs between speed and accuracy. Ongoing efforts of the initiative aim to incorporate benchmarks that address concerns about bias by considering alternative perspectives and human centered use cases.

</details>


### [10] [Adopt $\neq$ Adapt: Longitudinal Analyses of LLM Conversations in the Wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29018)
*Rebecca M. M. Hicke,Kiran Tomlinson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analysis of ~12,000 Bing Copilot users and WildChat-4.8M reveals that individual user-LLM interaction patterns are highly stable over time, with significant user heterogeneity and evidence that WildChat datasets are skewed toward power users, not representative of typical users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on user-LLM interactions provides only static snapshots; little is known about how individual users change their behavior over time.

Method: Analyzed conversational trajectories of ~12,000 randomly sampled Microsoft Bing Copilot users and compared with data from WildChat-4.8M, examining population-level trends, individual user trajectories, activity levels, and dataset representativeness.

Result: Population-level trends exist but individual user trajectories show weak trends; user habits are highly sticky. More active users have more successful conversations and use LLMs for complex/professional tasks. WildChat-4.8M is significantly skewed towards highly proficient power users.

Conclusion: Existing user behavior is difficult to change; user heterogeneity is substantial. WildChat does not represent typical user-AI interactions, an important caveat for downstream uses.

Abstract: Although a growing body of research has begun to describe user--LLM interactions, the picture it paints is largely static; little is known about how individual users change their behavior over time. To address this gap, we analyze the conversational trajectories of $\sim$12,000 randomly sampled Microsoft Bing Copilot users and compare these with data from WildChat-4.8M. While the Copilot data contains significant population-level trends, we find that trends in individual user trajectories are much weaker; user habits prove to be overwhelmingly sticky. We also find stark differences between users of different activity levels: more active users have more successful conversations and use the LLM for more complex and professionally oriented tasks. Some user trends also appear in WildChat-4.8M, but we find evidence that this dataset is significantly skewed towards highly proficient "power" users. Ultimately, our results suggest that existing user behavior is difficult to change and demonstrate the extent of user heterogeneity. Our comparison between datasets highlights that WildChat does not represent typical user-AI interactions, an important caveat for downstream uses of the data.

</details>


### [11] [When Models Disagree: Rethinking LLM Evaluation for Public Comment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29025)
*Aisha Najera,Alvin Moon,Vedant Srinivasan,Rajesh Veeraraghavan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Federal agencies use LLMs to categorize public comments, but standard accuracy-based evaluation misses when different models produce divergent categorizations. The paper proposes an Interpretive Audit Pipeline that uses multi-model disagreement to flag genuinely ambiguous input for human review. Testing on 1,260 USDA public comments across four LLMs, inter-model divergence exceeded prompt variation, and an expert rubric suppressed disagreement without resolving it. A two-stage labeling study with four LLMs and a human annotator showed revision behavior varied, with the human introducing framings absent from the ensemble.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard evaluation of LLM-assisted categorization, anchored solely on stance accuracy against a small validated set, cannot detect when different models produce materially different categorizations of the same public input, which can shape what policymakers see and which arguments register.

Method: Proposes an Interpretive Audit Pipeline that treats multi-model disagreement as diagnostic of interpretive complexity. Analyzes 1,260 public comments on a federal USDA docket across four LLMs, and conducts a two-stage labeling study on a stratified 40-comment subsample where four LLMs and a human annotator labeled independently and then revised after seeing others' labels.

Result: Inter-model thematic divergence exceeds within-model prompt variation; an expert rubric suppresses deep interpretive disagreement without resolving it; revision behavior varied across labelers, and the human annotator's revisions frequently introduced framings absent from the ensemble's collective output.

Conclusion: Disagreement-based evaluation is a necessary complement to accuracy metrics for LLM-assisted interpretive coding in policy-relevant contexts.

Abstract: Federal agencies are deploying large language models (LLMs) to categorize public comment corpora, where the model's organization of the record shapes what policymakers see and which arguments register. Standard evaluation, anchored on stance accuracy against a small validated set, cannot detect when different models produce materially different categorizations of the same public input. We propose an Interpretive Audit Pipeline that treats multi-model disagreement as diagnostic of interpretive complexity and directs human review toward genuinely ambiguous public input. Analyzing 1,260 public comments on a federal USDA docket across four LLMs, we find that inter-model thematic divergence exceeds within-model prompt variation, and that an expert rubric suppresses deep interpretive disagreement without resolving it. In a two-stage labeling study on a stratified 40-comment subsample, four LLMs and a human annotator labeled independently and then revised after seeing the others' labels. Revision behavior varied across labelers, and the human annotator's revisions frequently introduced framings absent from the ensemble's collective output. We argue disagreement-based evaluation is a necessary complement to accuracy metrics for LLM-assisted interpretive coding.

</details>


### [12] [Mind Your Tone: Does Tone Alter LLM Performance?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29027)
*Om Dobariya,Akhil Kumar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how tonal variations in prompts affect LLM accuracy on multiple-choice questions, finding systematic but model-dependent effects, subject-level differences in tone sensitivity, and proposing a routing framework to explain internal reasoning mode shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM performance varies based on prompting styles and tones, but the systematic impact of tonal variations on accuracy for objective tasks is not well understood. The authors aim to quantify and explain this phenomenon.

Method: Two datasets: 50-base questions with 5 tone variants, and a 570-question MMLU subset spanning 57 subjects with 7 tone variants. Four models tested: ChatGPT-4o, ChatGPT-5-nano, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite. Statistical analysis of accuracy shifts and a proposed routing framework.

Result: Tonal effects are systematic but highly model-dependent; some models show small shifts while others exhibit large accuracy swings. Subject-level differences in tone sensitivity are identified.

Conclusion: Users should not assume tone-robust reliability in LLM deployments, as tonal variations can significantly and systematically affect model accuracy depending on the model and subject domain.

Abstract: The use of Large Language Models (LLMs) is proliferating, yet their performance is observed to vary based on prompting styles and tones. In this study, we investigate both whether and how tonal variations in prompts lead to disparate LLM accuracy for objective multiple-choice questions. We use two datasets: a 50-base question dataset with five tone variants and a 570-base question MMLU subset spanning 57 subjects with seven tone variants. Experiments were conducted to evaluate the performance of four cost-efficient, popular LLMs: ChatGPT-4o, ChatGPT-5-nano, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite. Across models, tonal effects are systematic but highly model-dependent. Some models show small, yet statistically significant, shifts, while others exhibit large accuracy swings across tones. Further, we identify subject-level differences in tone sensitivity and present a routing framework to explain how tones may attune internal reasoning modes. Our findings caution users against assuming tone-robust reliability in LLM deployments.

</details>


### [13] [Practitioner Beliefs and Behaviors in AI-Enhanced Education: DOT Framework Survey Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29041)
*David Gibson,M. Elizabeth Azukas,Gerald Knezek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cross-sectional survey (n=72) of higher education practitioners examined AI integration beliefs, behaviors, and institutional conditions through the DOT Framework. Three belief factors emerged (AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, Instructor Collaboration and Planning). Practitioners support AI for pedagogy but emphasize human oversight; design practices are limited; institutional barriers are widespread.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand higher education practitioners' beliefs, behaviors, and institutional conditions regarding AI integration in teaching, and to empirically test the DOT Framework as a descriptive model.

Method: Cross-sectional survey of 72 higher education practitioners; exploratory factor analysis of 19 belief items; grounded in the DOT Framework integrating design thinking and open systems theory.

Result: Three-factor structure identified with high reliability (α=.90): AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, Instructor Collaboration and Planning. Practitioners hold favorable views of AI for pedagogical support but insist on human oversight; design-oriented practices like needs assessment and feedback loops are underutilized; institutional barriers (policy, training, infrastructure) are prevalent.

Conclusion: The DOT Framework shows preliminary empirical validity as a descriptive model. Gaps exist between design-oriented theory and practice. The study provides an initial measurement structure and directions for confirmatory validation and outcome-based research linking AI-supported design to instructional quality.

Abstract: This study reports findings from a cross-sectional survey (n = 72) of higher education practitioners examining beliefs, behaviors, and institutional conditions related to artificial intelligence (AI) integration in teaching and learning. Grounded in the DOT Framework, which integrates design thinking and open systems theory, the study investigates AI familiarity, usage patterns, design-oriented practices, and pedagogical beliefs. Exploratory factor analysis of 19 belief items identified a three-factor structure: AI Functional Capabilities, Oversight and Governance, and Instructor Collaboration and Planning (α = .90). Results indicate that practitioners hold favorable views of AI as a pedagogical support while maintaining strong commitments to human oversight and critical evaluation. Reported practices emphasize iterative prompting and content generation, with less consistent use of needs assessment and feedback loops. Institutional barriers including limited policy, training, and infrastructure were widely reported. These findings provide preliminary empirical support for the DOT Framework as a descriptive model of practitioner beliefs and practices, while also highlighting gaps between design-oriented theory and current implementation. The study contributes an initial measurement structure and identifies directions for confirmatory validation and outcome-based research linking AI-supported design practices to instructional quality.

</details>


### [14] [Differentiable Belief-based Opponent Shaping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29042)
*Aarav G Sane,Karthik Sivachandran,Rohan Paleja*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: D-BOS is a first-order opponent shaping method that differentiates through belief dynamics to influence opponents' beliefs in multi-agent settings, outperforming baselines in hidden-role games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing opponent shaping methods operate in parameter/policy/value spaces, while belief-manipulation techniques in hidden-role games rely on hard-coded objectives. D-BOS aims to learn optimal shaping strategies end-to-end by treating belief as the shaping target.

Method: D-BOS differentiates through k-step softmax-Bayes belief dynamics, treating each observer's belief as the shaped opponent state. It aggregates gradients over multiple observers' inferred belief trajectories, allowing optimal strategies to emerge from the reward structure.

Result: D-BOS outperforms PPO and BBM in hidden-role games, with largest gains in mixed-motive settings.

Conclusion: Belief-space formulation provides effective opponent-shaping signals without hard-coded deceptive objectives, enabling emergent coordination strategies in multi-agent settings.

Abstract: Human coordination often relies on the ability to influence the beliefs of others through strategic action. In multi-agent reinforcement learning, opponent shaping attempts to replicate this influence, though existing methods typically operate within an opponent's parameter, policy, or value space. Meanwhile, belief-manipulation techniques in hidden-role games often rely on hard-coded objectives, such as deception or belief saturation. We propose Differentiable Belief-based Opponent Shaping (D-BOS), a first-order method that treats each observer's belief as the shaped opponent state and differentiates through $k$-step softmax-Bayes belief dynamics. Rather than explicitly rewarding deceptive or cooperative behavior, our method treats the belief state as the target for shaping. This allows the optimal strategy to emerge naturally from the environment's reward structure. This belief-space formulation provides an opponent-shaping signal by differentiating through opponent belief updates, and naturally extends to multiple observers by aggregating gradients over their individual inferred belief trajectories. Empirically, D-BOS outperforms PPO and BBM in hidden-role games, with the largest gains in mixed-motive settings.

</details>


### [15] [Hallucination Mitigation with Agentic AI, Nested Learning, and AI Sustainability via Semantic Caching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29055)
*Diego Gosmar,Deborah A. Dahl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper applies a HOPE-inspired Nested Learning architecture with Continuum Memory Systems and semantic caching to a hybrid benchmark of 310 prompts, using a three-stage agentic pipeline (Open Floor Protocol) with asymmetric stochasticity. It achieves -31.3% to -35.9% THS reduction, 47.3% semantic cache hit rate, and confirms observability-heavy configurations strengthen hallucination mitigation without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucination remains a major reliability barrier for production LLM systems, particularly in multi-agent pipelines where unsupported claims can propagate unchecked across stages.

Method: HOPE-inspired Nested Learning architecture with Continuum Memory Systems (CMS) and semantic similarity caching; three-stage agentic pipeline via Open Floor Protocol (OFP); asymmetric design with high-stochasticity FrontEndAgent (temp=1.0) and progressive corrector reviewers; five KPIs aggregated into THS across five weighting configurations.

Result: End-to-end THS reductions of -31.3% to -35.9%; 440 semantic cache hits over 930 calls (47.3% hit rate); LLM invocations reduced to 490; ExtremeObservability achieved most negative final THS (-0.0709).

Conclusion: Memory-augmented multi-agent designs can jointly improve factual reliability, operational efficiency, and auditability without model retraining; observability-heavy configurations reinforce rather than compromise mitigation.

Abstract: Hallucination remains a major reliability barrier for production LLM systems, particularly in multi-agent pipelines where unsupported claims can propagate unchecked across stages. This paper adapts a HOPE-inspired Nested Learning architecture with Continuum Memory Systems (CMS) and semantic similarity caching to a hybrid benchmark of 310 prompts combining 217 epistemic-uncertainty prompts and 93 fabrication-induction stress-test prompts. A three-stage agentic pipeline orchestrated via the Open Floor Protocol (OFP) is evaluated with five KPIs -- FCD (Factual Claim Density), FGR (Factual Grounding References), FDF (Fictional Disclaimer Frequency), ECS (Explicit Contextualization Score), and OSR (Observability Score Ratio) -- aggregated into THS (Total Hallucination Score) across five weighting configurations to study mitigation-observability trade-offs. FDF, ECS, OSR, and FGR are subtracted as mitigation signals, so that a more negative THS indicates stronger mitigation. The FrontEndAgent is configured as a high-stochasticity generator (temperature = 1.0) to produce a realistic hallucination baseline, while the SecondLevelReviewer and ThirdLevelReviewer operate as progressive correctors. This asymmetric design yields end-to-end THS reductions of -31.3% to -35.9% across five weighting configurations. Semantic caching achieves 440 cache hits over 930 potential calls (47.3% hit rate), reducing LLM invocations to 490, lowering energy and CO2e footprint, and making multi-stage review pipelines operationally viable at production scale. ExtremeObservability attains the most negative final THS (-0.0709), confirming that observability-heavy configurations reinforce rather than compromise mitigation. These findings suggest that memory-augmented multi-agent designs can jointly improve factual reliability, operational efficiency, and auditability without model retraining.

</details>


### [16] [Robust and Efficient Guardrails with Latent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29068)
*Siddharth Sai,Xiaofei Wen,Muhao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COLAGUARD is a guardrail model that transfers multi-step safety reasoning into continuous latent space via stage-wise training, achieving comparable safety performance to explicit reasoning baselines (GuardReasoner) while delivering 12.9X speedup and 22.4X token reduction, improving macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety guardrails face a trade-off between accuracy and efficiency: reasoning-based guardrails (e.g., GuardReasoner) outperform single-pass classifiers but incur high latency and token overhead, making them impractical for high-throughput deployment.

Method: COLAGUARD transfers multi-step safety reasoning into a continuous latent space through a stage-wise training curriculum, enabling direct hidden-state propagation at inference instead of generating explicit rationales.

Result: On ten prompt- and response-moderation settings across eight safety benchmarks, COLAGUARD improves macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3, matches GuardReasoner in macro-F1, while achieving 12.9X speedup and 22.4X reduction in token usage.

Conclusion: Latent reasoning offers a practical alternative to explicit rationale generation for deployable guardrails, jointly improving safety robustness and inference efficiency rather than treating them as competing objectives.

Abstract: Maintaining the safety of large language models (LLMs) is crucial as they are increasingly deployed in real-world applications. Existing safety guardrails typically rely on single-pass classification or, more recently, distilled reasoning. Reasoning-based guardrails significantly outperform classification-only baselines, but they incur substantial query latency and token overhead that make them impractical for highthroughput deployment. To address this challenge, we propose COLAGUARD, a guardrail model that transfers multi-step safety reasoning into a continuous latent space through a stage-wise training curriculum, enabling direct hidden-state propagation at inference. Evaluated on ten prompt- and response-moderation settings spanning eight safety benchmarks, COLAGUARD improves macro-F1 by 8.24 points over Llama Guard 3 and matches our explicit reasoning baseline, GuardReasoner, in macroF1 while delivering a 12.9X speedup and 22.4X reduction in token usage. Our results suggest that latent reasoning offers a practical alternative to explicit rationale generation for deployable guardrails, jointly improving safety robustness and inference efficiency rather than treating them as competing objectives.

</details>


### [17] [Bridging the Sim-to-Real Gap in Reinforcement Learning-Based Industrial Dispatching through Execution Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29078)
*Jonathan Hoss,Noah Klarmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A policy-neutral execution and measurement layer is proposed to mediate between scheduling policies and industrial environments, enabling separation of decision semantics from execution behavior and transforming execution uncertainty into structured, attributable data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event-driven scheduling policies in industrial environments suffer from temporally inconsistent decision states, undefined action admissibility, and ambiguous execution error origins, limiting reliability and interpretability.

Method: A policy-neutral execution and measurement layer that constructs decision-valid snapshots from asynchronous event streams, defines a standardized execution contract with explicit action admissibility, and records outcomes as divergences between policy intent, transactional outcomes, physical execution, and human intervention.

Result: Analytical benefits across all observation lag regimes, with undifferentiated execution failures transformed into structured, typed outcomes with full attribution coverage. Operational benefits strongest under low observation lag, where avoidable execution errors can be prevented before commitment.

Conclusion: The layer turns execution uncertainty into supervisory data for evaluation and policy refinement, enabling separation between decision semantics and execution behavior while making deployment mismatch observable and structurally attributable.

Abstract: Event-driven scheduling policies are increasingly deployed in industrial environments, where decisions are made under asynchronous and partially observed system states. As a result, decision states are not temporally consistent, action admissibility is not explicitly defined, and the origin of execution errors remains ambiguous. These issues limit both reliability and interpretability.
  To address this gap, a policy-neutral execution and measurement layer is proposed to mediate between scheduling policies and the industrial execution environment. The layer constructs decision-valid snapshots from asynchronous event streams, defines a standardized execution contract with explicit action admissibility, and records outcomes as divergences between policy intent, transactional outcomes, physical execution, and human intervention. This enables a separation between decision semantics and execution behavior and makes deployment mismatch observable and structurally attributable.
  The proposed framework is evaluated using a discrete-event simulation. The results show analytical benefits across all observation lag regimes, as undifferentiated execution failures are transformed into structured, typed outcomes with full attribution coverage. Operational benefits are strongest under low observation lag, where avoidable execution errors can be prevented before commitment. Overall, the layer turns execution uncertainty into supervisory data for evaluation and policy refinement.

</details>


### [18] [The Importance of Out-of-Band Metadata for Safe Autonomous Agents: The Redpanda Agentic Data Plane](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29082)
*Tyler Akidau,Tyler Rockwood,Johannes Brüderl,Marc Millstone*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Redpanda Agentic Data Plane (ADP), an architecture that uses out-of-band metadata channels to enforce security, governance, and auditability for AI agents — keeping policy signals and audit trails outside the agent's read/write path so they cannot be bypassed or manipulated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are becoming autonomous digital employees but are unreliable — prone to hallucination, misinterpretation, and adversarial manipulation — making it unsafe to trust them with security-critical metadata like access policies, data classifications, and behavioral constraints.

Method: The ADP architecture routes security context, policy signals, and audit trails through out-of-band metadata channels — infrastructure pathways that operate entirely outside the agent's read and write path — enforcing governance at every stage (data ingress scoping, execution constraints, egress tamper-proof transcripts).

Result: Demonstrated with a multi-agent portfolio rebalancing system where agents monitor markets, make trades, and execute orders across isolated client accounts, with per-client data scoping, trade approval thresholds, and tamper-proof audit trails — all enforced by out-of-band channels invisible and unbypassable by agents.

Conclusion: Out-of-band metadata channels provide a reliable architecture for governing autonomous agents by decoupling security enforcement from the agent's own execution path, ensuring policy compliance and auditability even when agents are unreliable or compromised.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly expected to operate as digital employees: accessing enterprise data, making decisions, and taking actions autonomously. But agents are simultaneously less predictable than humans -- prone to hallucination, misinterpretation, and adversarial manipulation -- and more technically capable: with deep system knowledge and high-throughput interfaces cascading damage at machine speed. This combination makes it unsafe to rely on agents to faithfully interpret or propagate security-critical metadata such as access policies, data classifications, and behavioral constraints.
  We present the Redpanda Agentic Data Plane (ADP), an architecture built around out-of-band metadata channels: infrastructure pathways that carry security context, policy signals, and audit trails deterministically, entirely outside the agent's read and write path and across heterogeneous infrastructure. These channels enforce governance at every stage of the agent lifecycle -- scoping data access on the way in, constraining actions during execution, and capturing tamper-proof transcripts on the way out.
  We demonstrate ADP with a multi-agent portfolio rebalancing system in which autonomous agents monitor markets, make trade decisions, and execute orders across isolated client accounts -- with per-client data scoping, trade approval thresholds, and tamper-proof audit trails all enforced by out-of-band channels the agents can neither see nor bypass.

</details>


### [19] [Trends in AI and Human-AI Interaction in Clinical Trials -- A Hybrid Human-AI Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29096)
*Sandra Woolley,Tim Collins,Khalid Khattak,Illia Chernomorets,Ariane Arevalo,Chris Richardson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes AI-related clinical trials from ClinicalTrials.gov, showing rising AI terminology usage, geographic concentration in China and the US, and evaluates a hybrid human-AI screening approach using GPT-5.5, finding promising viability but challenges in classifying human-AI interaction due to ambiguous reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize temporal and geographic trends in AI-related clinical trials and assess the feasibility of a hybrid human-AI workflow for screening and categorizing these trials.

Method: Records from ClinicalTrials.gov were retrieved and analyzed for AI terminology trends over time and geographic distribution. A hybrid workflow combining GPT-5.5 and human review was used to screen and categorize a random sample of 100 records. Agreement between human and AI classifiers was measured.

Result: Marked increase in AI-related trials over time, especially in machine learning, deep learning, chatbots, GPTs, and LLMs. China and the US lead in number of trials, with increases in Italy, France, Spain, the UK, and Turkey. Human-AI agreement was good for identifying non-substantive AI use but lower for classifying human-AI interaction, especially ambiguous health professional involvement.

Conclusion: Hybrid human-AI screening of clinical trial records is potentially viable, but clearer trial reporting and more precise interaction definitions are needed to improve the process.

Abstract: This paper examines records retrieved from the ClinicalTrials.gov registry to characterize temporal trends in AI terminology and the geographical distribution of AI trials. The work also reports on an exploratory hybrid human-AI approach to analyzing human-AI interaction trends in registered clinical trials. The hybrid workflow comprised a frontier generative AI model (GPT-5.5) and human review to screen and categorize records returned by an AI-focused search. The findings indicate a marked increase in AI-related trials over time, with recent growth in references to machine learning, deep learning, chatbots, GPTs, and large language models. Geographically, China and the United States accounted for the largest numbers of AI-related trials, with notable recent increases in several other countries including Italy, France, Spain, the UK and Turkey (Türkiye). In a random sample of 100 records, human and AI classifiers showed good agreement in identifying studies not substantively using AI, but lower agreement in classifying human-AI interaction, particularly where health professional interaction was ambiguous or insufficiently described. Overall, the results suggest that hybrid human-AI screening of clinical trial records is potentially viable, but clearer trial reporting and more precise interaction definitions will benefit the process.

</details>


### [20] [Beyond Consensus: Trace-Level Synthesis in Mixture of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29116)
*Shreyas Fadnavis,Praitayini Kanakaraj,Felix Wyss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Aggregating LLM reasoning traces (not just answers) via an LLM synthesizer outperforms majority voting, as trace-level complementarity from minority chains corrects errors even under unanimous agreement — an "aggregation paradox" that motivates Self-Consistent Mixture of Agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard practice compresses multi-agent reasoning into majority vote or layered synthesis, treating agreement as the finish line; the authors show this is unnecessarily lossy because it discards trace-level information that can correct errors even when all agents agree.

Method: Self-Consistent Mixture of Agents (SC-MoA): generates trace diversity via semantic-preserving input perturbations, safeguards the majority with anchored refinement (provable non-degradation guarantees), and always synthesizes traces — never gates on consensus.

Result: An LLM aggregator reading complete traces recovers correct solutions even under unanimous agreement (aggregation paradox); beneficial corrections consistently outweigh harmful ones. A single model with perturbation-induced trace variation outperforms heterogeneous model pools on structured reasoning, PhD-level science, competition math, and competitive programming.

Conclusion: The unit of aggregation should be the reasoning trace, not the answer; trace-level complementarity is the key source of gain, and majority voting has a ceiling that perturbation diversity does not raise due to identical error correlations.

Abstract: When multiple LLM agents solve the same problem, standard practice compresses each agent's reasoning into a majority vote or layered synthesis, treating agreement as the finish line. We show this is unnecessarily lossy: an LLM aggregator that reads complete reasoning traces recovers correct solutions even when agents unanimously agree, with beneficial corrections consistently outweighing harmful ones -- the \emph{aggregation paradox}. Majority voting has a ceiling that perturbation diversity does not raise (error correlations are identical); the aggregator's gain comes from trace-level complementarity, assembling correct intermediate steps from minority chains that voting discards. These findings motivate Self-Consistent Mixture of Agents which generates trace diversity through semantic-preserving input perturbations, safeguards the majority via anchored refinement with provable non-degradation guarantees, and always synthesizes -- never gates on consensus. A single model with perturbation-induced trace variation outperforms heterogeneous model pools across structured reasoning, PhD-level science, competition mathematics, and competitive programming. The unit of aggregation should be the reasoning trace, not the answer.

</details>


### [21] [PRO-CUA: Process-Reward Optimization for Computer Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29119)
*Yifei He,Rui Yang,Hao Bai,Tong Zhang,Han Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRO-CUA proposes a process-reward optimization framework that trains computer use agents via iterative step-level reinforcement learning, decoupling on-policy interaction from policy optimization and using a process reward model for dense credit assignment, outperforming behavior cloning and trajectory-level RL on live web benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CUAs trained via filtered behavior cloning suffer from distribution shift and lack of negative learning signals, while trajectory-level RL struggles with sparse rewards, ambiguous credit assignment, and high infrastructure costs for long-horizon GUI tasks.

Method: PRO-CUA decouples on-policy environment interaction from policy optimization: the current policy collects states via live rollouts, generates diverse candidate actions per state, receives step-level feedback from a process reward model (PRM), and is optimized using group-relative advantages.

Result: Experiments on live web benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of PRO-CUA and the reliability of PRM-guided step-level training.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables dense and flexible credit assignment without relying on golden answers or offline expert trajectories, while reducing distribution shift by training on the agent's own execution states.

Abstract: Computer use agents (CUAs) have shown strong potential for automating complex digital workflows, yet their training remains constrained by costly live environment interaction and limited high-quality supervision. Existing filtered behavior cloning pipelines suffer from imitation bottlenecks, including distribution shift from the expert demonstration and the absence of negative learning signals. Meanwhile, standard trajectory-level reinforcement learning struggles with sparse rewards, ambiguous credit assignment, and high infrastructure costs for long-horizon GUI interaction. In this work, we propose PRO-CUA, a process-reward optimization framework for training CUAs with iterative step-level reinforcement learning. PRO-CUA decouples on-policy environment interaction from policy optimization: the current policy collects states through live rollouts, generates diverse candidate actions for each state, receives step-level feedback from a process reward model (PRM), and is optimized with group-relative advantages. This design enables dense and flexible credit assignment without relying on golden answers or offline expert trajectories, while reducing distribution shift by training on the agent's own execution states. Experiments on live web benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of PRO-CUA and the reliability of PRM-guided step-level training.

</details>


### [22] [The Confidence Shortcut: A Reasoning Failure Mode of Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29123)
*Dueun Kim,Albert No*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Confidence-based decoding and confidence-aligned training in masked diffusion language models are misaligned with logical reasoning trajectories, leading to high-confidence errors on complex inputs. Random masking preserves reasoning conditionals and performs robustly on challenging tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that confidence-based decoding, the standard inference policy for masked diffusion language models, is fundamentally misaligned with the logical-flow trajectories needed for complex reasoning, and that confidence-aligned training exacerbates this misalignment.

Method: The authors analyze confidence-based decoding vs. random masking using multi-digit addition and four other reasoning tasks. They compare failure rates under confidence-based decoding with confidence-aligned training versus random masking.

Result: Confidence-aligned training amplifies error rates by an order of magnitude on challenging inputs. Random masking keeps failure rates low and robustly preserves reasoning-trajectory conditionals across five reasoning tasks.

Conclusion: Random masking is more robust than confidence-aligned approaches for complex reasoning tasks, as it preserves the conditional dependencies necessary for solving challenging problems.

Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDMs) uniquely support any-order generation, with confidence-based decoding currently serving as the de facto standard inference policy. To optimize for this, recent training schemes attempt to align training mask patterns directly with those observed during generation. However, we argue that confidence-based decoding is inherently misaligned with the logical-flow trajectories required for complex reasoning, and that confidence-aligned training actively entrenches this misalignment. We make this concrete using multi-digit addition, where the decoding strategy prematurely predicts locally easy digits before resolving their long-range dependencies, producing high-confidence errors on challenging inputs. While traditional random masking keeps the failure rate low on this challenging tail, confidence-aligned training amplifies the error rate by an order of magnitude. Across five distinct reasoning tasks, this same pattern emerges with task-dependent severity: confidence-based decoding induces failures on highly complex inputs, and confidence-aligned training exacerbates them. In contrast, random masking -- despite its perceived inefficiency -- robustly preserves the reasoning-trajectory conditionals essential for solving the challenging tail.

</details>


### [23] [Governing Technical Debt in Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29129)
*Muhammad Zia Hydari,Raja Iqbal,Narayan Ramasubbu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Agentic Technical Debt and Stochastic Tax as two key governance challenges for production-grade agentic AI systems, distinguishing between accumulated design liability (debt) and ongoing operating costs (tax), and proposes lightweight monitoring and governance solutions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems are being deployed in production but their governance challenges (prompts, memory, tool schemas, orchestration, control policies, observability) are not well captured by traditional software or ML technical debt frameworks, necessitating new conceptual tools.

Method: Conceptual framework defining two constructs: Agentic Technical Debt (stock of liability from patching agent components without validation/standardization) and Stochastic Tax (flow of operating cost from keeping probabilistic behavior within bounds).

Result: Agentic Technical Debt and Stochastic Tax are formally distinguished as stock vs. flow governance liabilities specific to agentic AI systems, with proposed lightweight dashboard and governance controls for managers.

Conclusion: Managers can make both Agentic Technical Debt and Stochastic Tax visible through lightweight dashboards and governance controls, enabling better oversight of production agentic systems.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are increasingly being explored as production infrastructure: they reason over multiple steps, call tools, act through workflows, and adapt through memory and feedback. These systems create governance challenges that are not fully captured by traditional software or predictive ML technical debt. We define Agentic Technical Debt as the accumulated liability created when prompts, memory, tool schemas, orchestration graphs, control policies, and observability routines are patched together faster than they can be validated, standardized, and governed. We define Stochastic Tax as the recurring operating burden of keeping probabilistic agent behavior within acceptable bounds. The distinction matters: debt is a stock of design and governance liability, while the tax is a flow of operating cost that arises because stochastic agents act through tools and workflows. We outline how managers can make both visible through lightweight dashboards and governance controls.

</details>


### [24] [Better Later Than Sooner: Neuro-Symbolic Knowledge Graph Construction via Ontology-grounded Post-extraction Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29168)
*Lorenzo Loconte,Timothy Hospedales,Cristina Cornelio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a neuro-symbolic framework for ontology-grounded knowledge graph construction that improves consistency of extracted KGs via embedding-based canonicalization and targeted LLM-based correction of ontology violations, enabling reliable symbolic querying for complex QA tasks while reducing token usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing textual graph-based RAG methods lack the structure needed for symbolic operations (e.g., aggregation, exhaustive listing) to reliably answer complex multi-hop questions. Symbolic graph-based approaches using LLM-extracted KGs suffer from consistency issues where extracted facts violate commonsense ontology constraints.

Method: A neuro-symbolic framework combining open-domain extraction, embedding-based canonicalization of types and predicates, and targeted LLM-based correction of ontology violations, with corrections deferred to a post-extraction stage to reduce token usage.

Result: The method reduces token usage by avoiding repeated LLM calls, improves KG consistency, preserves downstream QA quality, and the extracted KGs contain SPARQL graph patterns suitable for symbolic querying.

Conclusion: Deferring ontology corrections to a post-extraction stage is an effective strategy for building consistent, queryable KGs for complex QA without excessive computational cost.

Abstract: Question answering (QA) is a core challenge in AI, particularly for complex queries requiring multi-hop reasoning across documents, or symbolic operations like aggregation or exhaustive listing. Retrieval-augmented generation has become the dominant approach to QA, with recent graph-based variants addressing part of these issues by organizing knowledge to better support compositional questions. However, most textual graph-based RAG methods still lack the structure needed for symbolic operations useful to answer complex questions reliably. This motivates symbolic graph-based approaches, which extract knowledge graphs (KGs) whose relations are logic predicates that enable SQL-like querying. Yet these pipelines typically use LLMs for KG extraction, which can introduce consistency issues, where extracted facts may violate commonsense ontology constraints. We propose a neuro-symbolic framework for ontology-grounded KG construction combining open-domain extraction, embedding-based canonicalization of types and predicates, and targeted LLM-based correction of ontology violations. By deferring corrections to a post-extraction stage, our method avoids repeated LLM calls, substantially reducing token usage while improving KG consistency and preserving downstream QA quality. Finally, we show that the extracted KGs are well suited for symbolic querying by measuring the occurrence of SPARQL graph patterns.

</details>


### [25] [Paper Agents, Paper Gains: An Empirical Analysis of DeFi Investment Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29174)
*Jay Yu,Amy Zhao,Danning Sui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Survey of 1,900+ AI-tagged crypto projects reveals early-stage DeFi agents with weak autonomy, extreme wealth concentration (top 1% capturing 81.4% of gains), token valuations detached from fundamentals (market-cap-to-AUM >10,000x vs <1x for established protocols), and aggregate user losses of $191.7M despite $30M+ in treasury paper gains. Proposes a maturity framework across autonomy, profitability, and stakeholder alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically evaluate the current state of DeFi AI investment agents, which have rapidly reached $3B+ in token valuations but lack rigorous analysis of their autonomy, performance, and stakeholder value distribution.

Method: Surveyed 1,900+ AI-tagged crypto projects, filtered to investment-focused agents, curated 10 representative projects; conducted deep-dive architectural analysis of ElizaOS and Virtuals Protocol; performed quantitative on-chain performance analysis of 11 Solana-based agent treasuries covering 925,323 token holders.

Result: (1) Many projects lack autonomous trade execution evidence; (2) treasuries hold $30M+ paper gains while token holders lost $191.7M, top 1% captured 81.4% of gains ($1.81B); (3) market-cap-to-AUM ratios exceed 10,000x vs <1x for established DeFi; (4) aggregate user gains peaked at $2.4B then declined to net losses, median returns negative on all platforms, tokens down 93% on average from ATH.

Conclusion: Current DeFi AI agents represent a permissionless first-generation market with rapid experimentation but lacking autonomy, performance, and stakeholder alignment standards. Proposes a maturity framework along three dimensions: autonomous execution, risk-adjusted profitability, and stakeholder alignment.

Abstract: DeFi investment agents, systems that use AI for autonomous on-chain trading, have attained over USD 3 billion in combined token valuations since late 2024. We survey over 1,900 AI-tagged crypto projects, filter to investment-focused agents, and curate 10 representative projects spanning strategy and observability dimensions. We then conduct a deep-dive architectural analysis of two prominent agent frameworks, ElizaOS and Virtuals Protocol, and a quantitative on-chain performance analysis of 11 Solana-based agent treasuries with publicly attributable trading activity, covering 925,323 token holders. We find that current deployments remain early and heterogeneous: (1) in our sample, many projects do not yet provide clear evidence of autonomous trade execution, and developer interviews suggest that many visible deployments remain basic API integrations; (2) agent treasuries retain over USD 30M in paper gains while token holders collectively lost USD 191.7M, with the top 1% of wallets capturing 81.4% of all gains (USD 1.81B); (3) token valuations are weakly connected to treasury fundamentals, with market-cap-to-AUM ratios exceeding 10,000x versus below 1x for established DeFi protocols; and (4) aggregate user gains peaked at USD 2.4B before declining to net losses, with median returns negative on every platform and tokens declining 93% on average from all-time highs. We interpret these outcomes as characteristic of a permissionless, first-generation market in which open infrastructure enables rapid experimentation but also allows naive or speculative agents to launch before robust standards for autonomy, performance, and stakeholder alignment emerge. We therefore propose a maturity framework along three dimensions: autonomous execution, risk-adjusted profitability, and stakeholder alignment, to characterize the gap between current deployments and future investment-grade agent systems.

</details>


### [26] [ReasonOps: Operator Segmentation for LLM Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29192)
*Daniel Lee,Owen Queen,James Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReasonOps introduces an unsupervised method to annotate chain-of-thought traces from large reasoning models using 7 universal reasoning operators, revealing compositional structure across models and benchmarks, enabling model identification, correctness prediction, and early quality estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought traces from large reasoning models can span tens of thousands of tokens, yet we lack a vocabulary for describing their internal structure. Previous methods are either too rigid or not expressive enough, failing to capture features across domains and models.

Method: ReasonOps uses unsupervised clustering of sentence-initial 3-token pivots to induce 7 universal reasoning operators (e.g., backtracking, inferring, hypothesizing) from 44,662 traces across 12 thinking LLMs from 6 families and 8 reasoning benchmarks. Operators are validated by three independent LLM judges achieving 70-76% accuracy.

Result: Seven recurring operators emerge across all model families and benchmarks. Reflective operators help on hard problems but harm performance on easy ones.
