Operator sequences form distinctive "reasoning fingerprints" — a classifier recovers source model with high macro-AUC. Structural operator features predict answer correctness (WP-AUC) and enable early quality estimation at 50% trace length.

Conclusion: ReasonOps provides an unsupervised, annotation-free pipeline for deep structural insights into LLM reasoning traces, with strong downstream results in model identification and correctness prediction.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought traces from large reasoning models can span tens of thousands of tokens, yet we lack a vocabulary for describing their internal structure. Previous methods developed to analyze chain-of-thought traces are either too rigid or not expressive enough, failing to capture features across domains and models. To remedy this, we develop ReasonOps, an unsupervised, expressive method for annotating chain-of-thought traces, providing succinct universal operators. Using ReasonOps, we analyze 44,662 traces from 12 thinking LLMs spanning 6 families across 8 reasoning benchmarks and discover that they share a common compositional structure: 7 recurring reasoning operators -- discourse-level moves such as backtracking, inferring, and hypothesizing -- that emerge from unsupervised clustering of sentence-initial 3-token pivots. These operators appear across every model family and benchmark domain, confirmed by three independent LLM judges who classify held-out samples at 70 -76% accuracy. We analyze the structure of operators on easy vs. hard problems, revealing that reflective operators are more helpful on hard problems and harm performance on easy problems. Operator sequences are highly model-identifying: a classifier trained on operator distributions alone recovers the source model with macro-AUC, revealing that each model family has a distinctive reasoning fingerprint. Structural operator features predict within-problem answer correctness well above baselines. Classifiers built on these operators reach WP-AUC and on AIME specifically. ReasonOps further enables early quality estimation well before the trace completes: we predict at WP-AUC for only 50% of the trace. The ReasonOps pipeline is unsupervised and annotation-free, enabling deep insights into LLM reasoning traces as well as strong downstream results on model identification and correctness prediction.

</details>


### [27] [GTA: Generating Long-Horizon Tasks for Web Agents at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29218)
*Tenghao Huang,Kung-Hsiang Huang,Prafulla Kumar Choubey,Yilun Zhou,Muhao Chen,Jonathan May,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GTA, a scalable framework for automatically generating realistic, multi-hop web-agent tasks with executable trajectories by integrating crawling, retrieval-based seeding, in-context generation, and automated quality control. The framework decouples crawling from generation, grounds tasks in site graphs, and provides dense supervision. Applied to 50+ websites with multilingual and multi-hop coverage, the resulting benchmark reveals a significant human-agent performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for web agents are limited by manual construction with coarse start-goal annotations and no intermediate trajectories, while automatic generation efforts remain expensive, biased, and shallow. This prevents reliable training and evaluation of agents that must generalize to realistic, multi-hop, cross-page tasks.

Method: GTA integrates four components: (1) crawling to collect website structures, (2) retrieval-based seeding to identify meaningful task starting points, (3) in-context generation to create multi-hop tasks and executable trajectories, and (4) automated quality control via deterministic replays and systematic validation. Crawling is decoupled from generation for efficiency, and tasks are grounded in site graphs to enforce compositionality.

Result: The pipeline was instantiated on over 50 websites spanning e-commerce, government, forums, and news with multilingual and multi-hop coverage. The resulting benchmark reveals a significant human-agent performance gap and enables detailed diagnostics.

Conclusion: The paper contributes: (i) formalizing multi-hop web-agent task generation, (ii) proposing an efficient and validated pipeline for automatic data creation, and (iii) releasing a dynamic benchmark with reproducible evaluation.

Abstract: Web agents, which couple language models with browsing and tool-use capabilities, show promise as open web assistants. Yet progress is increasingly limited by the lack of scalable, process-level supervision. Existing benchmarks are largely manually constructed, providing only coarse start-goal annotations without intermediate trajectories, while recent automatic generation efforts remain expensive, biased, and shallow. These limitations prevent reliable training and evaluation of agents that must generalize to realistic, multi-hop, cross-page tasks. We introduce a scalable framework, GTA, that integrates crawling, retrieval-based seeding, in-context generation, and automated quality control to produce realistic tasks paired with executable trajectories. This design decouples crawling from generation for greater efficiency, grounds tasks in the site graph to enforce compositionality, and ensures dense supervision through deterministic replays and systematic validation. We instantiate the pipeline on over 50 websites covering e-commerce, government, forums, and news, with multilingual and multi-hop coverage. The resulting benchmark reveals a significant human-agent performance gap and enables detailed diagnostics. Our contributions are three-fold: (i) formalizing multi-hop web-agent task generation, (ii) proposing an efficient and validated pipeline for automatic data creation, and (iii) releasing a dynamic benchmark with reproducible evaluation.

</details>


### [28] [BenchTrace: A Benchmark for Testing Reflection Ability and Controlled Evolution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29225)
*Jiahao Huang,Fei Cheng,Junfeng Jiang,Zefan Yu,Akiko Aizawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BenchTrace is a benchmark for evaluating self-evolution ability in LLM agents, comprising reflection and evolution evaluations across 1,821 episodes. Experiments show Qwen3-32B and GPT-4.1 fall below 30% pass rate on reflection evaluation, diagnose reflection bottlenecks, and reveal forgetting and negative transfer in evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation of self-evolving agents is limited: it only measures task scores (not reflection quality) and relies on agents' own runs, lacking mechanisms to target specific failure patterns.

Method: BenchTrace includes (1) a snapshot-reflection dataset of 1,821 annotated episodes across six tasks, (2) Reflection Evaluation via targeted QA on failure identification, (3) Evolution Evaluation via controlled self-evolution simulation measuring failure avoidance, and (4) a new metric called failure avoidance rate (FAR).

Result: Qwen3-32B and GPT-4.1 both fall below 30% end-to-end pass rate on reflection evaluation, with diagnosis as the main bottleneck. Self-evolution methods improve FAR over baselines, but agents forget early lessons as noise accumulates and fail to generalize reflections across contexts, causing negative transfer.

Conclusion: Only fully correct reflections strongly correlate with higher FAR. BenchTrace exposes concrete limits of current self-evolution approaches and provides a controlled, model-agnostic evaluation framework.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents improve over time by reflecting on past failures, but existing evaluation is limited in two ways: it measures only task scores, leaving reflection quality unknown, and it relies on agents' own episode runs, offering no mechanism to target specific failure patterns. We present \textbf{BenchTrace}, a benchmark for evaluating self-evolution ability in LLM agents. BenchTrace is built on a snapshot-reflection dataset of 1,821 annotated episodes spanning six diverse tasks, and comprises a \textbf{Reflection Evaluation} that probes failure identification through targeted QA tasks, and an \textbf{Evolution Evaluation} that tests whether past failure experience translates into avoidance behavior in a controlled self-evolution simulation. Building on BenchTrace, we propose \textbf{failure avoidance rate (FAR)}, a new evaluation metric measuring the fraction of test cases in which the agent successfully avoids the target failure instance. Experiments with Qwen3-32B and GPT-4.1 reveal that both models fall below a 30\% end-to-end pass rate on reflection evaluation, with diagnosis as the primary bottleneck. Evolution evaluation shows that self-evolution methods generally improve FAR over the non-evolving baseline, but agents forget early lessons as noise episodes accumulate, and agents fail to generalize their reflections beyond the specific context, causing negative transfer across task contexts. Our correlation analysis further reveals that only a fully correct reflection is strongly associated with higher FAR. BenchTrace exposes concrete limits of current self-evolution approaches and provides a controlled, model-agnostic framework for targeted evaluation.

</details>


### [29] [Tailoring the Curriculum: Student-Centered Reasoning Distillation via Dynamic Data-Model Compatibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29229)
*Jiahao Huang,Fei Cheng,Junfeng Jiang,Akiko Aizawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Data-Model Compatibility (DMC) metric to assess dataset suitability for reasoning distillation, showing strong correlation with performance and improved results when used for static or dynamic data selection across multiple student models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning distillation's success depends on how well training data align with the student model, but no existing metric explicitly assesses this compatibility. The authors aim to quantify dataset-student alignment to improve distillation outcomes.

Method: The authors propose DMC, a metric that jointly considers data quality, relative difficulty, and student capability. They validate it by (1) measuring its correlation with reasoning distillation performance and (2) using it as a criterion for data selection, including dynamic selection during training.

Result: DMC exhibits a strong correlation with reasoning distillation performance; using DMC as the data selection criterion improves distillation results; dynamic dataset selection based on DMC during training further enhances performance, and these findings hold across multiple student models and tasks.

Conclusion: DMC is an effective metric for evaluating and selecting training data for reasoning distillation, and its dynamic application during training yields additional gains.

Abstract: Reasoning distillation transfers complex reasoning abilities from large language models (LLMs) to smaller ones, yet its success depends on how well the training data align with the student model. This paper introduces the Data-Model Compatibility (DMC) metric, which can be used to assess the suitability of a dataset for reasoning distillation on a student model. DMC provides an assessment by jointly considering data quality, relative difficulty, and student capability. We validated the effectiveness of DMC from two perspectives: (1) DMC exhibits a strong correlation with reasoning distillation performance; and (2) using DMC as the criterion for data selection leads to improved reasoning distillation performance. Both findings are consistently demonstrated across multiple student models and tasks. Moreover, since the DMC of each dataset dynamically changes during training, our experiments demonstrate that dynamically selecting datasets based on DMC can further enhance performance.

</details>


### [30] [Planning with the Views via Scene Self-Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29563)
*Kangrui Wang,Linjie Li,Zhengyuan Yang,Shiqi Chen,Zihan Wang,Li Fei-Fei,Jiajun Wu,Leonidas Guibas,Lijuan Wang,Manling Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLMs struggle with multi-turn view planning in 3D; the proposed iterative framework with self-exploration and view graph distillation boosts Qwen2.5-VL-7B from 2.5% to 47.8%, surpassing GPT-5.4 Pro and Gemini 3.1 Pro.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLMs lack the ability to compose view transformations across multi-turn plans for goal-directed viewpoint navigation in 3D space.

Method: ViewSuite (a 3D point-cloud environment on ScanNet scenes) for evaluation; an iterative framework alternating self-exploration (collecting all trajectories) with view graph distillation (converting trajectories into a compact graph for supervised tasks).

Result: Qwen2.5-VL-7B improved from 2.5% to 47.8% on interactive view planning, surpassing GPT-5.4 Pro (18.5%) and Gemini 3.1 Pro (21.4%).

Conclusion: Self-exploration combined with view graph distillation effectively overcomes the planning gap and sparse reward problem, offering a promising direction for VLMs that actively reason and plan in 3D space.

Abstract: Can VLMs predict how each camera move changes the view, and plan many such moves ahead? We call this capability view planning, requiring (1)understanding how a single action transforms the view, and (2)composing many such transformations across multi-turn plans to identify a target view. We probe both abilities in our proposed ViewSuite, a 3D point-cloud environment on real ScanNet scenes. Across 13 frontier VLMs, a critical planning gap emerges: they possess basic view-action knowledge but fail to compose it across multi-turn plans, with the gap widening as viewpoint distance grows. To close this gap, we propose an iterative framework that alternates self-exploration with view graph distillation. The key insight is that all exploration trajectories, regardless of their outcome, collectively form a view graph that compactly captures how viewpoints connect across a scene. Distilling this graph into diverse supervised tasks reshapes the policy distribution and overcomes the sparse rewards that stall pure RL. This improves Qwen2.5-VL-7B from 2.5% to 47.8% on interactive view planning, surpassing GPT-5.4 Pro (18.5%) and Gemini 3.1 Pro (21.4%). Self-exploration emerges as a promising path toward VLMs that can actively reason and plan in 3D space.

</details>


### [31] [Rethinking Literature Search Evaluation: Deep Research Helps, and Human Citation Lists Are Not a Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29234)
*Gaurav Sahu,Laurent Charlin,Christopher Pal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study showing that a Deep Research pipeline with breadth-first bibliography expansion achieves over 80% recall on literature search (vs. below 20% for API-only), while revealing that human reference lists are imperfect ground truth—only 51% of human citations are moderately relevant, and humans are 2.5x more likely to cite direct collaborators—arguing for multi-axis evaluation of search quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve large-scale literature search from both the retrieval pipeline perspective and the evaluation perspective, questioning whether human reference lists are reliable ground truth.

Method: Implemented a Deep Research pipeline that processes the full query paper and expands retrieved results breadth-first along bibliographies; used neutral LLM-as-a-judge to evaluate human citation relevance; analyzed co-authorship graphs on OpenAlex.

Result: Deep Research pipeline raised recall from below 20% to above 80% on RollingEval-Jun25; only 51% of human citations judged moderately relevant or higher vs. 86-88% for best AI re-rankers; humans are 2.5x more likely to cite direct collaborators.

Conclusion: Single-axis literature-search evaluation is insufficient; recall, topical relevance, ranked-list diversity, and co-authorship-distance diagnostic should be reported jointly as complementary quality measures.

Abstract: We study large-scale literature search from two complementary angles: improving the retrieval pipeline, and stress-testing the human reference list as an evaluation target. First, we implement a Deep Research pipeline that processes the full query paper and expands the retrieved results breadth-first along their bibliographies, and show that it substantially outperforms vanilla API-only search, raising recall on RollingEval-Jun25 (a 250-paper literature-search benchmark) from below 20% to above 80%. Second, we use a neutral LLM-as-a-judge to determine if human references are sound ground truth for the task. We find significant limitations: only 51% of human citations are judged moderately relevant or higher, against 86--88% for the strongest AI-based re-rankers. We study this gap on the OpenAlex co-authorship graph, finding that humans are 2.5x more likely than the best AI re-rankers to cite a direct collaborator. Together, our results argue against single-axis literature-search evaluation: recall, topical-relevance scoring, ranked-list diversity, and a co-authorship-distance diagnostic each measure complementary properties of citation quality and should be reported jointly.

</details>


### [32] [Surfacing Isolated Learners with Outcome-Independent Mediation of Feedback between Teachers and Students Using AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29240)
*Junsoo Park,Youssef Medhat,Htet Phyo Wai,Ploy Thajchayapong,Ashok K. Goel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an interpretable decision layer that ranks course topics requiring attention by combining three pre-outcome signals (learning difficulty prevalence, student-instructor disagreement, unresolved teacher concerns), achieving strong alignment with instructor priorities and identifying at-risk learners earlier than single-signal baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-augmented classrooms generate rich feedback before graded outcomes are available, but these signals are difficult to translate into timely instructional decisions. The authors aim to bridge this gap without relying on grades or post-hoc labels.

Method: A transparent mechanism integrates three signals: (1) student learning difficulty prevalence, (2) disagreement between learner self-reports and observed difficulties, and (3) unresolved teacher concerns. It outputs ranked topic priorities with per-topic decision records. Evaluated in a graduate CS course (n=5 instructor interviews; n=279 survey responses) using overlap, Spearman correlation, and AUC comparisons.

Result: Top-5 topic overlap of 3/5 with instructor concerns; Spearman ρ=0.80 with instructor priorities and ρ=0.46 (p=.048) with student-reported difficulty. Multi-signal integration achieved AUC=0.96 vs. 0.91 for gap prevalence alone. Behavioral signals (reflective thinking, help-seeking, self-efficacy) aligned with learning-related constructs.

Conclusion: Transparent coordination mechanisms may support human-AI co-agency when feedback is incomplete, though results are preliminary and warrant further investigation.

Abstract: AI-augmented classrooms generate rich teacher and student feedback before graded outcomes become available, yet these signals can be difficult to translate into timely instructional decisions. We propose an interpretable decision layer: a transparent mechanism that ranks course topics requiring attention without using grades or post-hoc outcome labels. The approach combines three signals: student learning difficulty prevalence, disagreement between learner self-reports and observed difficulties, and unresolved teacher concerns. The output is a ranked set of topic priorities with per-topic decision records explaining each ranking. In one graduate CS course offering ($n=5$ instructor interviews; $n=279$ survey responses), prioritized topics aligned with instructor concerns (top-5 overlap 3/5; Spearman $ρ=0.80$) and student-reported topic difficulty ($ρ=0.46$, $p=.048$). Multi-signal integration also surfaced learners not identified through individual signal sources alone (AUC $=0.96$ vs. $0.91$ for gap prevalence alone). Reflective thinking, help-seeking, and self-efficacy provided additional evidence that student behavioral signals align with learning-related constructs. While preliminary, these findings suggest that transparent coordination mechanisms may help support human-AI co-agency when feedback is incomplete.

</details>


### [33] [DenseSteer: Steering Small Language Models towards Dense Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29247)
*Yang Ouyang,Shuhang Lin,Jung-Eun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DenseSteer is a training-free inference-time framework that improves small LLMs' reasoning by steering internal representations toward denser reasoning patterns (fewer steps, more information per step), yielding accuracy gains without increasing token-level NLL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models (≤3B parameters) significantly underperform on multi-step reasoning tasks compared to larger LLMs; the paper aims to bridge this gap by identifying and leveraging the Dense Reasoning property.

Method: Empirically analyzed Qwen-2.5 models on math reasoning benchmarks to identify the Dense Reasoning property (fewer steps, higher information density per step). Proposed DenseSteer, a training-free inference-time steering framework that modulates internal representations of small models toward dense reasoning patterns.

Result: Consistent accuracy improvements on small models without increasing token-level Negative Log-Likelihood.

Conclusion: Dense reasoning is an effective structural approach to mathematical problem solving for small models, and DenseSteer provides a practical, training-free method to elicit this capability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning abilities, while smaller models (<= 3B parameters) significantly underperform on multi-step reasoning tasks. Based on empirical analyses of the Qwen-2.5 model family on math reasoning benchmarks, we find that more proficient reasoning is associated with fewer reasoning steps but higher information density per step, a property we term Dense Reasoning. Motivated by this observation, we propose DenseSteer, a training-free inference-time steering framework that enhances small-model reasoning by modulating internal representations toward dense reasoning patterns. Experiments show that our method yields consistent accuracy improvements without increasing token-level Negative Log-Likelihood, highlighting dense reasoning as an effective structural approach to mathematical problem solving.

</details>


### [34] [Provably Secure Agent Guardrail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29251)
*Benlong Wu,Weiming Zhang,Kejiang Chen,Han Fang,Nenghai Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a Proof-Constrained Action (ePCA) framework that uses neural-symbolic isolation and formal first-order logic verification to achieve deterministic security guarantees for AI agents, achieving zero attack success and false positive rates in tested scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing defense mechanisms (semantic guardrails, probabilistic LLM adjudicators) lack deterministic security lower bounds and fail against complex semantic symbol decoupling attacks, creating a fundamental crisis as LLMs become agents with expansive execution privileges.

Method: Introduces an executable Proof-Constrained Action (ePCA) framework with a neural symbolic isolation architecture that forces agents to losslessly formalize intentions into first-order logical mathematical constraints before performing physical operations, abandoning semantic trust in natural language.

Result: Achieves zero attack success rate and zero false positive rate across macroscopic and microscopic two-dimensional dynamic adversarial systems, with extremely low computational latency.

Conclusion: Provides a conditional formal foundation under explicit system assumptions and an engineering paradigm for constructing the underlying defense foundation for future intelligent systems.

Abstract: As large language models transition from bounded generative engines to agents with expansive execution privileges, AI going out of control precipitates a fundamental crisis in artificial intelligence security. Existing defense architectures heavily rely on empirical semantic guardrails and probabilistic large model adjudicators, mechanisms that fail to provide deterministic security lower bounds when facing complex semantic symbol decoupling attacks. To overcome this empirical semantic guardrail dilemma, this paper proposes a new security paradigm for agents based on the fundamental limitations of logical reasoning. Based on this paradigm, we further introduce an executable Proof-Constrained Action (ePCA) framework with a neural symbolic isolation architecture. This framework abandons semantic trust in natural language, forcing agents to losslessly formalize their intentions into first-order logical mathematical constraints before performing physical operations. Empirical evaluations of macroscopic and microscopic two-dimensional dynamic adversarial systems demonstrate that our formal verification mechanism achieves zero attack success rate and zero false positive rate across the evaluated scenarios, with extremely low computational latency. This research provides a conditional formal foundation under explicit system assumptions and an engineering paradigm for constructing the underlying defense foundation for future intelligent systems.

</details>


### [35] [OpenClawBench: Benchmarking Process-side Anomalies in Real-world Agent Execution Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29253)
*Yibing Liu,Yangze Liu,Xiaolong Yin,Bin Wang,Chong Zhang,Hao Yin,Zhongyi Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenClawBench is a large-scale dataset (31,264 annotated trajectories) revealing the Outcome-Process Gap in agent executions — where agents pass task oracles yet exhibit process-side anomalies (e.g., unresolved ambiguity, unsafe writes). FullTax provides structured anomaly supervision (binary labels, evidence, localization, severity, taxonomy). Among 31,135 oracle-passing executions, 2,904 were process-anomalous. A LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma 3 12B detector achieves binary F1=0.729.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world agent executions can hide process anomalies even when tasks succeed (Outcome-Process Gap). Existing success-only evaluation misses these subtle failures, motivating the need for systematic measurement and supervision of process-side anomalies.

Method: Built from BFCL-driven OpenClaw sessions from 6 source models, yielding 31,264 annotated trajectories. FullTax converts aligned trajectories into structured anomaly supervision with binary labels, evidence, onset/span localization, severity, recoverability, and a 5-class taxonomy. A LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma 3 12B detector is trained on high-confidence FullTax supervised pool.

Result: Among 31,135 oracle-passing executions, 2,904 (9.3%) were labeled process-anomalous. The LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma 3 12B detector achieves binary F1=0.729 on the cleaner-labels held-out test split.

Conclusion: Success-only evaluation misses a concrete class of process-side failures. OpenClawBench enables auditable, reusable supervision for studying, diagnosing, and monitoring runtime agent reliability.

Abstract: Task success can hide process anomalies in real-world agent executions. An agent may pass the final task oracle while still accumulating unresolved ambiguity, unsafe external writes, ignored errors, weakly grounded commitments, or capability-boundary overcommitment. We study this mismatch as the Outcome-Process Gap and introduce OpenClawBench, a large-scale dataset for measuring and supervising process-side anomalies in real agent execution processes. OpenClawBench is built from BFCL-driven OpenClaw sessions produced by 6 source models and contains 31,264 annotated trajectories. It aligns task-oracle outcomes with structured process evidence. FullTax converts the aligned trajectories into structured anomaly supervision: binary labels, supporting evidence, onset/span localization, severity, recoverability, and a 5-class anomaly taxonomy. Using OpenClawBench, we make the Outcome-Process Gap measurable. Among 31,135 oracle-passing executions, 2,904 are still labeled process-anomalous under FullTax. These results show that success-only evaluation misses a concrete class of process-side failures in real agent executions. A LoRA-fine-tuned Gemma 3 12B detector trained on the high-confidence FullTax supervised pool reaches binary F1=0.729 on the cleaner-labels held-out test split. Together, OpenClawBench turns real agent execution logs into auditable and reusable supervision for studying, diagnosing, and operationally monitoring runtime agent reliability.

</details>


### [36] [When and How Human Curation Backfires: Preference Alignment under Multi-Model Self-Consuming Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29267)
*Yang Zhang,Xiukun Wei,Xueru Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies self-consuming training (training on synthetic data from prior model iterations) in multi-model settings, finding that while human curation helps alignment in isolated models, cross-model interactions can dampen or invert this effect, degrading long-term alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work on self-consuming training focused on single, isolated models consuming their own outputs with human curation. In practice, models interact and train on outputs from other models, so the authors aim to understand the dynamics of self-consuming training in the multi-model regime.

Method: The authors formalize a framework for interacting self-consuming models, characterize convergence conditions of the dynamical system, and analyze how human curation of one model affects its own alignment (self-influence) and propagates to other models (cross-influence).

Result: Unlike isolated settings where human curation always enhances model alignment, cross-model interactions can dampen or even invert this effect, ultimately degrading long-term alignment.

Conclusion: Multi-model self-consuming training introduces complex dynamics where human curation benefits can be negated or reversed through cross-model influence, highlighting risks in realistic training environments where models interact.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly trained on synthetic data generated by prior model iterations rather than exclusively on real data. This self-consuming training paradigm can lead to model collapse, divergence, or bias amplification. Recent work (Ferbach et al., 2024) shows that incorporating human curation into the loop can steer a self-consuming model toward human-aligned behavior, but these analyses focus on a single, isolated model that solely consumes its own outputs. In practice, however, models often interact and train on input-output pairs produced by other models. This paper studies self-consuming training in the multi-model regime. We first formalize a framework for interacting self-consuming models and characterize when the resulting dynamical system converges to a stable point. We then examine how human curation of one model affects its own alignment (self-influence) and how such effects propagate to other models (cross-influence). Unlike isolated settings where human curation always enhances model alignment, we show that cross-model interactions can dampen or even invert this effect, ultimately degrading long-term alignment.

</details>


### [37] [Indexing the Unreadable: LLM-Native Recursive Construction and Search of Service Taxonomies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29270)
*Wei Zheng,Yang Yan,Yiyang Shao,Jinyang Li,Zeze Chang,Yukuang Jia,Qiming Mao,Chihyung Wang,Jingbin Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A2X is an LLM-native progressive-disclosure scheme for service discovery in the Internet of Agents, which organizes services into a hierarchical taxonomy and walks it layer by layer at query time. This decouples effective-context scarcity from registry size, reduces token consumption, and improves retrieval accuracy — achieving 6.2-point Hit Rate gain at one-ninth the prompt-token cost compared to full-context dumping, and over 20-point Hit Rate improvement over the state-of-the-art open-source embedding-based baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face a structural mismatch with the Internet of Agents (IoA) regime where effective context is a scarce resource that does not scale with the number of services. Concatenating thousands of service descriptions overflows the context window and causes the Lost-in-the-Middle phenomenon.

Method: Proposes A2X (Agent-to-Anything service discovery), an LLM-driven pipeline that automatically organizes registered services into a hierarchical taxonomy and walks it layer by layer at query time, ensuring each LLM call sees only a small candidate set relevant to the user query.

Result: A2X achieves a 6.2-point Hit Rate gain at one-ninth the prompt-token cost compared to full-context dumping, and improves Hit Rate by more than 20 points compared to the state-of-the-art open-source embedding-based baseline.

Conclusion: The proposed progressive-disclosure scheme decouples effective-context scarcity from registry size, significantly reducing token consumption while improving retrieval accuracy, enabling scalable service discovery for the IoA era.

Abstract: The era of the Internet of Agents (IoA) is taking shape: LLM agents are expected to fulfill user goals by orchestrating fast-growing populations of Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, Agent-to-Agent (A2A) endpoints, reusable skills, and other LLM-callable services. Yet LLMs face a structural mismatch with this regime: effective context is a scarce resource that does not scale with the number of services. Concatenating thousands of service descriptions into a prompt overflows the context window, and even when the window is large enough, models systematically under-attend to information in the middle of long inputs, the well-documented Lost-in-the-Middle phenomenon. This is fundamentally a question of context management for service discovery. To address this, we propose an LLM-native progressive-disclosure scheme and its concrete instantiation, A2X (Agent-to-Anything service discovery): an LLM-driven pipeline that automatically organizes the registered services into a hierarchical taxonomy and walks it layer by layer at query time, so that every LLM call sees only a small candidate set highly relevant to the user query. This decouples effective-context scarcity from registry size and significantly reduces token consumption while improving retrieval accuracy. Compared to full-context dumping, A2X achieves a 6.2-point Hit Rate gain at one-ninth the prompt-token cost; compared to the state-of-the-art open-source embedding-based baseline, A2X improves Hit Rate by more than 20 points.

</details>


### [38] [CoHyDE: Iterative Co-Training of LLM Rewriter & Dense Encoder for Tool Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29271)
*Vaishali Senthil,Ashutosh Hathidara,Sebastian Schreiber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoHyDE is an iterative co-training procedure that jointly optimizes a dense encoder and an LLM rewriter for tool retrieval, outperforming both contrastive fine-tuning and HyDE by up to +8 pp NDCG@5, especially on vague queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool retrieval over large API catalogs suffers from a vocabulary gap between colloquial user queries and technical API descriptions. Existing approaches (contrastive encoder fine-tuning and HyDE-style query expansion) address this problem from opposite ends but fail in complementary ways: fine-tuned encoders fail on mismatched surface forms, while HyDE generates catalog-unaware descriptions that degrade well-formed query retrieval.

Method: CoHyDE iteratively trains a dense encoder (via InfoNCE) and an LLM rewriter (via DPO preference alignment) as a co-evolving system. The rewriter generates catalog-style hypothetical descriptions; the encoder is retrained on them. Both components are warm-started on the tool catalog before the iterative loop begins.

Result: On a ~10k tool subset of ToolBench, three rounds of CoHyDE improve over the best single-component baseline by +2.5 pp NDCG@5 on standard queries and +6.3 pp on held-out vague queries, with gains up to +8 pp on the hardest vague tier. Ablations confirm co-training is essential; using either component alone loses up to -8 pp on vague queries.

Conclusion: CoHyDE demonstrates that co-evolving the encoder and rewriter jointly is the key to bridging the vocabulary gap in tool retrieval, achieving robustness on both well-formed and underspecified queries.

Abstract: Tool retrieval over large API catalogs is a core bottleneck for LLM agents: user queries arrive in colloquial, often underspecified language, while the catalog uses technical API vocabulary that no fixed encoder can bridge on its own. The two dominant training approaches, contrastive encoder fine-tuning and HyDE-style query expansion with a frozen LLM, address this problem from opposite ends and fail in complementary directions: the fine-tuned encoder excels when the query's surface form already matches the catalog but collapses when it does not, while zero-shot HyDE is more robust to underspecified queries yet generates catalog-unaware hypothetical descriptions that degrade retrieval when queries are well-formed. We introduce CoHyDE, an iterative procedure that trains the dense encoder and the LLM rewriter as a single co-evolving system: the encoder is retrained with InfoNCE on catalog-style hypothetical descriptions produced by the rewriter, and the rewriter is preference-aligned via DPO against the encoder's retrieval scores, with both sides warm-started on the tool catalog before the loop begins. On a ~10k tool subset of the ToolBench catalog, three rounds of CoHyDE improve over the strongest single-component baseline by +2.5 pp NDCG@5 on standard queries and +6.3 pp on held-out vague queries, with gains as large as +8 pp on the hardest vague tier. Ablations confirm that co-training is the key ingredient: using either component in isolation fails to match CoHyDE on both well-formed and vague queries, with losses of up to -8 pp on vague queries.

</details>


### [39] [Diagnosing Harmful Continuation in Answer-Correct Long-CoT Training Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29288)
*Chen He,Yuhao Wu,Lei Wang,Wenxuan Zhang,Fumin Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Answer-correct long chain-of-thought traces often contain harmful post-conclusion continuation that degrades SFT outcomes. Removing it improves training, and the phenomenon is characterized by an uncertainty-geometry mismatch. A lightweight boundary proxy (HCC) is proposed to cut such continuation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite using answer-correct long-CoT traces for SFT, different traces can yield markedly different fine-tuning outcomes. The paper investigates whether certain portions of these traces (post-conclusion continuations) are detrimental to training.

Method: A delete-only editor removes post-conclusion continuation (suffix after the answer is sufficiently supported). SFT is compared on original vs. processed traces. The removed continuation is further analyzed via uncertainty and hidden-state progress measures, leading to HCC—a lightweight boundary proxy.

Result: Removing editor-identified post-conclusion continuation improves SFT outcomes, confirming it is harmful. The removed continuation shows persistent local uncertainty and weakened terminal-directional progress, forming an uncertainty-geometry mismatch.

Conclusion: Post-conclusion continuation in answer-correct long-CoT traces is harmful to training. HCC provides a practical lightweight method to cut it, improving SFT results.

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought (CoT) traces are widely used as supervision for reasoning-oriented LLM SFT, yet answer-correct traces can still lead to markedly different fine-tuning outcomes. We study post-conclusion continuation in answer-correct long-CoT data: a continuation where the answer appears sufficiently supported, but the trace continues with additional reasoning that remains in the supervised target. To test its training effect, we use a delete-only editor to construct answer-preserving suffix removal and compare CoT-based SFT on the original and processed traces. We observe improved SFT outcomes after removing the editor-identified post-conclusion continuation, suggesting that this continuation is harmful to training in our setting. We therefore refer to this empirically supported phenomenon as harmful continuation. Beyond this intervention, we further characterize the removed post-conclusion continuation through uncertainty and hidden-state progress. We observe persistent local uncertainty together with weakened terminal-directional progress, forming an uncertainty--geometry mismatch. Finally, we instantiate Harmful Continuation Cut (HCC), a lightweight boundary proxy that approximates the editor-identified post-conclusion continuation boundary.

</details>


### [40] [Entropy-KL Divergence-based Token Masking: A Novel Approach for Selective Fine-tuning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29303)
*Qi Liu,Mingdi Sun,Yongyi He,Zhi Zheng,Tong Xu,Yi Zheng,Zhefeng Wang,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EKSFT selectively masks high-entropy or high-KL tokens during supervised fine-tuning to preserve pre-trained distribution, improving both SFT and subsequent RL performance in low-data regimes for LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard SFT in low-data regimes causes distribution shift away from pre-trained distribution, hindering effective exploration during subsequent RL training. The authors propose that SFT should prioritize activating task-relevant capabilities over memorizing limited samples.

Method: EKSFT (Entropy-KL Selective Fine-Tuning) selectively masks tokens exhibiting either high entropy or high KL divergence from a reference model during SFT, excluding high-uncertainty, distribution-shifting tokens from imitation.

Result: EKSFT consistently outperforms standard SFT on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Models fine-tuned with EKSFT followed by RL yield better post-RL performance, indicating improved exploration during RL stage.

Conclusion: In low-data regimes, SFT should focus on activating task-relevant capabilities rather than memorizing specific content. Selective token masking helps preserve pre-trained distribution while injecting task-specific knowledge.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) followed by reinforcement learning (RL) has become a standard post-training paradigm for large language models. This paradigm provides a cold-start for RL exploration, avoiding the inefficiency of pure RL where on-policy sampling yields insufficient positive samples. However, in practice, existing approaches often use a small amount of data for SFT initialization compared to the RL phase, which can cause the model to fit the limited samples and shift away from its pre-trained distribution. This distribution shift impedes the model's ability to effectively explore during subsequent RL training. To address this challenge, we propose that in low-data regimes, SFT should prioritize activating task-relevant capabilities rather than memorizing specific content. Along this line, we propose EKSFT (Entropy-KL Selective Fine-Tuning), which selectively masks tokens that exhibit either high entropy or high KL divergence from a reference model. By excluding these high-uncertainty, distribution-shifting tokens from imitation, EKSFT injects task-specific knowledge while preserving the integrity of the model's pre-trained distribution. Empirical evaluations on mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that EKSFT consistently outperforms standard SFT. Further RL fine-tuning from the EKSFT model yields consistently better post-RL performance, indicating improved exploration for the RL stage. Our codes and datasets are available at https://github.com/MINE-USTC/EKSFT.

</details>


### [41] [Rubric-Guided Process Reward for Stepwise Model Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29310)
*Shenghao Ye,Yu Guo,Zhengheng Li,Shuangwu Chen,Jian Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RoRo is a rubric-guided process reward framework for stepwise model routing that uses process rewards (not just outcome rewards) to train routers via reinforcement learning, achieving better accuracy and cost trade-offs across reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing stepwise model routing methods use reinforcement learning but only supervise routers with outcome rewards (final answer correctness), which fail to evaluate intermediate routing decisions, weakening performance and generalization.

Method: RoRo collects diverse routing trajectories, constructs preference pairs based on outcome, cost, and process quality, trains a Rubricor to generate query-specific evaluation rubrics and a Judge to score trajectories under these rubrics via alternating optimization, then combines process rewards with outcome rewards to optimize routing policy via GRPO.

Result: Experiments on five reasoning benchmarks under same-family and cross-family settings show RoRo consistently outperforms strong baselines and achieves better accuracy and cost trade-offs.

Conclusion: Process rewards from rubric-guided evaluation improve stepwise model routing performance and generalization compared to outcome-only rewards.

Abstract: Stepwise model routing improves the efficiency of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) by assigning each reasoning step to a suitable model. Recent methods formulate routing as a sequential decision process and train the router with reinforcement learning. However, although they model routing as a process, they still supervise the router with outcome rewards. Such rewards only reflect final answer correctness and fail to evaluate intermediate routing decisions, which can weaken performance and generalization. To address this gap, we propose RoRo, a rubric-guided process reward framework for stepwise model routing. RoRo first collects diverse routing trajectories and constructs preference pairs based on outcome, cost, and process quality. It then trains a Rubricor to generate a query-specific evaluation rubric and a Judge to score routing trajectories under this rubric through alternating optimization. The resulting process rewards are combined with outcome rewards to optimize the routing policy via GRPO. Experiments on five reasoning benchmarks under both same-family and cross-family settings show that RoRo consistently outperforms strong baselines and achieves better accuracy and cost trade-offs.

</details>


### [42] [ConMoE: Expert-Pool Consolidation via Prototype Reassignment for MoE Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29350)
*Yilun Yao,Jiaming Pan,Elsie Dai,Peizhuang Cong,Yaoming Li,Tong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConMoE is a train-free post-training compression method for Mixture-of-Experts language models that consolidates the expert pool by selecting a subset of pretrained experts as prototypes and deterministically remapping original expert references to these prototypes, achieving competitive or superior performance compared to pruning and merging baselines without weight updates or fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MoE language models reduce per-token computation but remain memory-intensive due to the need to store and serve all experts. Existing post-training compression methods (pruning/merging) are limited, and there is a need for a more principled approach that separates expert reduction from reuse structure.

Method: ConMoE formulates compression as expert-pool consolidation: retaining a smaller set of pretrained experts as reusable prototypes, with deterministic remapping of original expert references to selected prototypes. It uses calibration-based contribution and replaceability signals for expert selection, operates within local layer scopes with prototype sharing, preserves the original router interface, and requires no weight updates or fine-tuning.

Result: ConMoE matches or outperforms strong pruning and merging baselines on three pretrained MoE models (deepseek-moe-16b-base, Qwen3-30B-A3B, OLMoE-1B-7B-0125), achieving the best average score at both 25% and 50% routed-expert reduction on deepseek-moe-16b-base. Ablations show deterministic reassignment is the most stable component, while cross-layer sharing and weight fusion are model-dependent.

Conclusion: ConMoE provides an effective train-free approach to MoE compression through prototype remapping, demonstrating that careful expert selection and deterministic reassignment can achieve strong performance without post-compression fine-tuning, though some design choices (cross-layer sharing, weight fusion) require model-specific tuning.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models reduce per-token computation but still require storing and serving all experts, making deployment memory-intensive. Existing post-training compression methods mainly shrink this cost by pruning experts or merging their weights. We formulate post-training MoE compression as expert-pool consolidation: retaining a smaller set of pretrained experts as reusable prototypes and deterministically remapping each original expert reference to one selected prototype. This view separates the reduced expert pool from the reuse structure that represents the original expert slots, and allows prototype sharing within local layer scopes while preserving the original router interface. We propose ConMoE, a train-free prototype remapping framework that selects retained experts using calibration-based contribution and replaceability signals, then redirects original expert calls to the selected prototypes without weight updates or post-compression fine-tuning. Experiments on three pretrained MoE language models show that ConMoE matches or outperforms strong pruning and merging baselines in several settings, achieving the best average score on deepseek-moe-16b-base at both 25% and 50% routed-expert reduction, while remaining competitive on Qwen3-30B-A3B and OLMoE-1B-7B-0125. Ablations indicate that deterministic reassignment is the most stable component, whereas broader cross-layer sharing and post-hoc weight fusion are model-dependent.

</details>


### [43] [PassNet: Scaling Large Language Models for Graph Compiler Pass Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29357)
*Yiqun Liu,Yingsheng Wu,Ruqi Yang,Enrong Zheng,Honglei Qiu,Sijun He,Tai Liang,Jingjing Wu,Yuhan Zhou,Yiwei Zhang,Dongyan Chen,Weihan Yi,Xinqi Li,Siqi Bao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PassNet introduces a large-scale ecosystem for LLM-based compiler pass generation, revealing that while LLMs can achieve up to 3x speedup on individual subgraphs vs. TorchInductor, consistency remains the bottleneck — fine-tuning on just ~4K PassNet trajectories yields a 2.67x improvement toward frontier model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern tensor compilers (e.g., TorchInductor) systematically underperform on long-tail workloads — 43% of real-world subgraphs suffer end-to-end slowdowns under default compilation. Existing LLM-based optimization efforts focus on standalone kernel generation rather than structured compiler pass generation, leaving a gap in automated optimization.

Method: PassNet comprises (1) PassNet-Dataset: 18K+ unique computational graphs from 100K real-world models; (2) PassBench: 200 curated long-tail fusible tasks (2,060 subgraphs) evaluated under Error-aware Speedup Score (ES_t) with layered integrity defenses against LLM exploitation. The approach uses LLMs to author structured graph transformations integrated directly into compiler pipelines.

Result: Best frontier model trails TorchInductor by 37% in aggregate, yet individual subgraphs achieve up to 3x speedup over the same compiler. Fine-tuning a small model on ~4K PassNet trajectories yields a 2.67x improvement approaching frontier-model performance. PassBench is highly discriminative and unsaturated.

Conclusion: The bottleneck in LLM-driven compiler optimization is consistency, not capability. PassNet is validated as live training infrastructure for advancing LLM-driven compiler optimization. All data, benchmarks, and tooling are publicly available.

Abstract: Modern tensor compilers such as TorchInductor deliver substantial speedups on mainstream models, yet face a systematic performance ceiling on long-tail workloads -- our profiling shows that 43% of real-world subgraphs experience end-to-end slowdowns under default compilation. While LLMs offer a path toward automated optimization, existing efforts focus on standalone kernel generation. We argue that pass generation -- where LLMs author structured graph transformations that integrate directly into compiler pipelines -- is the more appropriate abstraction. We propose PassNet, the first large-scale ecosystem for LLM-based compiler pass generation, comprising: (1) PassNet-Dataset, over 18K unique computational graphs from 100K real-world models; and (2) PassBench, 200 curated long-tail fusible tasks (comprising 2,060 subgraphs in total) evaluated under the Error-aware Speedup Score (ES_t) -- a metric unifying correctness, stability, and performance -- with layered integrity defenses against systematic LLM exploitation. Experiments reveal that PassBench is both highly discriminative and genuinely unsaturated: the best frontier model trails TorchInductor by 37% in aggregate, yet on individual subgraphs LLMs achieve up to 3x speedup over the same compiler -- indicating that the bottleneck is consistency, not capability. Fine-tuning a small model on merely ~4K PassNet trajectories yields a 2.67x improvement approaching frontier-model performance, demonstrating substantial headroom and validating PassNet as live training infrastructure for advancing LLM-driven compiler optimization. All data, benchmarks, and tooling are publicly available.

</details>


### [44] [Scaling Monosemanticity: Extracting Interpretable Features from Claude 3 Sonnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29358)
*Adly Templeton,Tom Conerly,Jonathan Marcus,Jack Lindsey,Trenton Bricken,Brian Chen,Adam Pearce,Craig Citro,Emmanuel Ameisen,Andy Jones,Hoagy Cunningham,Nicholas L Turner,Callum McDougall,Monte MacDiarmid,Alex Tamkin,Esin Durmus,Tristan Hume,Francesco Mosconi,C. Daniel Freeman,Theodore R. Sumers,Edward Rees,Joshua Batson,Adam Jermyn,Shan Carter,Chris Olah,Tom Henighan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sparse autoencoders trained on Claude 3 Sonnet's middle layer extract interpretable, multilingual, multimodal features (including abstract concepts and safety-relevant ones like deception/power-seeking) that can causally steer model behavior, though with incomplete coverage and limited evaluation rigor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the open question of whether dictionary learning methods like sparse autoencoders can scale beyond small transformer models to extract interpretable features from production-scale language models.

Method: Trained sparse autoencoders with up to 34 million features on the middle layer residual stream of Claude 3 Sonnet, using scaling laws to guide hyperparameter selection.

Result: Features are multilingual, multimodal (generalize to images despite text-only training), respond to concrete and abstract concepts, and causally influence model outputs when manipulated. Identified features for entities, locations, sarcasm, code errors, and safety-relevant concepts like deception, power-seeking, sycophancy, and bias.

Conclusion: Sparse autoencoders can scale to production models to extract interpretable and causally effective features, but limitations remain: incomplete feature coverage and lack of rigorous methods for evaluating whether features faithfully capture model computations.

Abstract: We demonstrate that sparse autoencoders can extract interpretable features from Claude 3 Sonnet, a production-scale language model, addressing the open question of whether dictionary learning methods scale beyond small transformers. We trained sparse autoencoders with up to 34 million features on the model's middle layer residual stream, using scaling laws to guide hyperparameter selection. The resulting features are multilingual and multimodal (generalizing to images despite text-only training), respond to both concrete instances and abstract discussions of concepts, and can be used to steer model behavior in ways consistent with their interpretations. We find features corresponding to famous entities and locations, as well as more abstract concepts like sarcasm or errors in code. We also identify features relevant to ways in which language models might cause harm--including features representing deception, power-seeking, sycophancy, and bias--and show that these causally influence model outputs when manipulated. Additionally, we conduct analyses of feature interpretability, geometry, and computational function. However, significant limitations remain: our suite of features is incomplete, and we lack rigorous methods for evaluating whether our features faithfully capture model computations.

</details>


### [45] [MiraBench: Evaluating Action-Conditioned Reliability in Robotic World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29360)
*Tianzhuo Yang,Zihan Shen,Zirui Mi,Zhaoyi Zhang,Jiayi Zhou,Jiaming Ji,Juntao Dai,Jiawei Chen,Boyuan Chen,Yaodong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MiraBench is a hierarchical benchmark evaluating action-conditioned reliability in robotic world models across three levels: Physics Adherence, Action-Following Fidelity, and Optimism Bias Detection. Testing 12 model configurations with 16,000+ human judgments, it reveals that visual fidelity is a poor proxy for action fidelity, scaling doesn't improve action following, and optimism bias is pervasive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of robotic world models focus on visual fidelity, failing to assess whether predictions are physically plausible, faithful to commanded actions, and calibrated to failure—leaving a gap in understanding action-conditioned reliability.

Method: MiraBench defines a three-level hierarchical benchmark: (1) Physics Adherence (reference-free physical consistency), (2) Action-Following Fidelity (respect for task-relevant action inputs), and (3) Optimism Bias Detection (tendency to predict success under failure-inducing actions). It uses a human-annotated corpus of 16,000+ judgments across tasks, failure categories, and 12 model configurations (vector-conditioned, text-conditioned, open/closed-source, multiple scales).

Result: Three central findings: (1) visual fidelity is a poor proxy for action fidelity, (2) increasing model scale does not reliably improve action following, and (3) optimism bias is pervasive across all current world model systems.

Conclusion: MiraBench shifts evaluation from appearance-based metrics to action-conditioned reliability, providing a diagnostic foundation for assessing and improving robotic world models as faithful simulators.

Abstract: Action-conditioned world models are increasingly used as scalable simulators for robot learning, yet current evaluations provide limited evidence that their predictions are reliable under the actions they condition on. Existing benchmarks largely emphasize visual fidelity, leaving unclear whether predicted futures are physically plausible, faithful to commanded actions, and calibrated to failure when actions should not succeed. We introduce \textsc{MiraBench}, a hierarchical benchmark that defines \emph{action-conditioned reliability} as a core evaluation target for robotic world models. MiraBench decomposes this target into three progressively demanding levels: \emph{Physics Adherence}, which evaluates reference-free physical consistency; \emph{Action-Following Fidelity}, which measures whether predictions respect task-relevant action inputs; and \emph{Optimism Bias Detection}, which probes the tendency to predict successful outcomes under failure-inducing actions. To support this evaluation, we curate a human-annotated corpus with over 16,000 judgments across tasks, failure categories, and leading world models. We evaluate 12 representative model configurations spanning vector-conditioned robotic world models, text-conditioned generative world models, open-weight systems, closed-source systems, and multiple model scales. Across this broad model landscape, MiraBench reveals three central findings: visual fidelity is a poor proxy for action fidelity; increasing model scale does not reliably improve action following; and optimism bias is pervasive across current systems. By shifting evaluation from appearance to action-conditioned reliability, MiraBench provides a diagnostic foundation for assessing and improving robotic world models as faithful simulators.

</details>


### [46] [EvoMD-LLM: Learning the Language of Species Evolution in Reactive Molecular Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29394)
*Zhichen Tang,Zhengzheng Dang,Yulin Chen,Jixin Wu,Haiwen Li,Yanming Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoMD-LLM reformulates molecular dynamics as a symbolic temporal language modeling problem, using event tokens with persistence durations and temporal scaffolding to enable LLMs to model dynamic physical processes, achieving 66.14% accuracy and generating interpretable predictions without explicit supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs excel at static scientific reasoning but fail to model the temporal structure of dynamic physical processes.

Method: Discretizes reactive MD trajectories into sequences of molecular events where each token is a chemical species augmented with persistence duration; applies temporal scaffolding as a structured inductive bias to reduce hallucinated outputs; fine-tunes autoregressive LLMs on symbolic temporal language.

Result: Achieves up to 66.14% accuracy on temporal prediction tasks, consistently outperforms sequential neural networks and language-based baselines; generates chemically relevant interpretations without paired trajectory-explanation supervision.

Conclusion: Symbolic temporal language modeling provides an effective framework for grounding LLMs in dynamic physical simulations.

Abstract: While large language models (LLMs) excel at static scientific reasoning, they struggle to model the temporal structure of dynamic physical processes. We present EvoMD-LLM (Evolutionary Molecular Dynamics Large Language Model), a framework that reformulates species-level molecular dynamics as a symbolic temporal language modeling problem. Reactive MD trajectories are discretized into sequences of molecular events, where each token represents a chemical species augmented with its persistence duration, enabling standard autoregressive LLMs to learn compositional evolution over time through efficient fine-tuning. A key component of EvoMD-LLM is temporal scaffolding, which treats event duration as an explicit linguistic token and serves as a structured inductive bias, significantly reducing invalid or hallucinated molecular outputs compared to conventional sequence modeling approaches. We evaluate EvoMD-LLM on multiple temporal prediction tasks, achieving up to 66.14% accuracy and consistently outperforming sequential neural networks and language-based baselines. Beyond quantitative improvements, we qualitatively observe that the model is capable of generating interpretations for its own predictions by incorporating relevant chemical knowledge, even though it was not explicitly supervised with paired trajectory-explanation data. These results demonstrate that symbolic temporal language modeling provides an effective framework for grounding LLMs in dynamic physical simulations.

</details>


### [47] [Aligned but Fragile: Enhancing LLM Safety Robustness via Zeroth-Order Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29396)
*Zhihao Liu,Yifan Wu,Jian Lou,Di Wang,Yuxi Zhou,Yuke Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies safety alignment robustness in LLMs from an optimizer-centric view, proposing a hybrid framework combining first-order alignment with zeroth-order refinement to enhance robustness against post-alignment manipulations while preserving safety and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety alignment in LLMs is fragile under lightweight post-alignment manipulations (e.g., parameter noise, activation noise, quantization). Prior work focused on data curation, alignment objectives, or parameter identification, but the role of the optimizer itself in robustness was unexplored.

Method: The paper proposes a hybrid framework: standard first-order safety alignment followed by zeroth-order refinement. Zeroth-order optimization provides robustness-oriented signals by evaluating safety under perturbations. Layer-wise robustness sensitivity is estimated using perturbation-based evaluations to focus updates on critical layers.

Result: Both theoretically and empirically, only a few zeroth-order refinement steps enhance robustness while preserving safety alignment. The efficiency is improved by exploiting perturbation-based evaluations to concentrate updates on robustness-critical layers with modest training overhead.

Conclusion: The optimizer-centric perspective is effective for improving safety alignment robustness. Zeroth-order refinement after first-order alignment provides a practical and efficient solution to mitigate fragility in LLM safety behavior.

Abstract: Safety alignment for large language models (LLMs) aims to reduce harmful or unsafe behavior while preserving general utility. However, recent findings reveal that alignment effects can be fragile: lightweight post-alignment manipulations, such as parameter noise, activation noise, or quantization, can easily weaken the intended safety behavior. Prior efforts to improve robustness have primarily focused on data curation, modified alignment objectives, and safety-critical parameter identification, leaving the role of the optimizer itself largely unexplored.
  In this paper, we are the first to study the robustness of safety alignment from the perspective of the base optimizer. This optimizer-centric view naturally points to zeroth-order optimization, which provides a robustness-oriented signal by evaluating safety alignment under perturbations. Based on this insight, we propose a hybrid framework that first performs standard first-order safety alignment and then applies zeroth-order refinement to improve robustness. Both theoretically and empirically, we show that only a few zeroth-order refinement steps can enhance robustness while preserving safety alignment. We further improve the efficiency of zeroth-order refinement by exploiting its inherent perturbation-based evaluations to estimate layer-wise robustness sensitivity, enabling the refinement process to concentrate updates on robustness-critical layers with modest training overhead.

</details>


### [48] [Architecture-Sensitive Supervised Fine-Tuning for Screen-Conditioned Action Prediction: A PiSAR Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29400)
*Rahul Bissa,Abhishek Vyas,Yash Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuned Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct (sem_sim 0.783) drastically outperforms frontier zero-shot baselines (Claude Opus 4.7 at 0.459, GPT-5.5 at 0.482) and a comparably fine-tuned Gemma-4-26B-A4B-IT (0.441) on the PiSAR behavioral rationale benchmark, with the Gemma failure attributed to recipe-model mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether supervised fine-tuning on domain-specific behavioral rationale data (PiSAR corpus) can outperform frontier zero-shot models, and to assess generalizability of fine-tuning across different model architectures.

Method: Benchmarked three supervised fine-tuned models (Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, Gemma-4-26B-A4B-IT) against frontier zero-shot baselines (Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5.5) on a held-out 661-row slice of the PiSAR corpus using semantic similarity (sem_sim) scoring.

Result: Fine-tuned Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct achieved sem_sim 0.783 (79% rows ≥0.7), far exceeding frontier baselines (0.459–0.482, 1–2% rows ≥0.7). Gemma-4-26B-A4B-IT fine-tuned with same data/recipe scored only 0.441, matching zero-shot baselines.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning a moderately-sized reasoning model (Qwen3-VL-8B) on domain data drastically outperforms both frontier zero-shot models and same-recipe fine-tuning on a larger, reasoning-tuned model (Gemma-4-26B), which appears resistant to displacement via the current recipe.

Abstract: We benchmark three supervised fine-tuned models against frontier zero-shot baselines on a 661-row held-out slice of PiSAR (Persona, intent, Screen, Action, Rationale), a 12,929-tuple corpus of screen-anchored behavioural rationales curated from public app-store reviews, Pew American Trends Panel demographics, and the OPeRA shopper traces. Every model, frontier or fine-tuned, is evaluated on the same 661-row slice with the same scoring pipeline. Two findings. First, frontier zero-shot baselines (Claude Opus 4.7 and GPT-5.5) reach sem_sim 0.459 and 0.482 respectively; a fine-tuned Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct reaches 0.783 and clears sem_sim >= 0.7 on 79% of rows, against 1-2% for either frontier baseline, a gap of 0.30 absolute on the same test set. Second, the same training data and recipe on Gemma-4-26B-A4B-IT scores only 0.441, in the same band as the frontier zero-shot baselines rather than the fine-tuned Qwen. We read this as a recipe-vs-model mismatch: the reasoning-tuned high-parameter model resists displacement and would likely need either more data or a stronger fine-tuning method.

</details>


### [49] [When Does Persona Prompting Actually Help? A Retrieval and Metric Analysis of Expert Role Injection in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29420)
*Shuai Xiao,Su Liu,Weikai Zhou,Jialun Wu,Xinjie He,Zhiyuan Lin,Qiyang Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Persona prompting in LLMs creates a systematic tradeoff between expertise depth and clarity, rather than broadly improving response quality. Aggregate scores mask this tradeoff; effects are highly conditional, varying by domain and question type. Hybrid retrieval improves role selection but does not eliminate the tradeoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work evaluates persona prompting using aggregate scores, which obscures whether expert-role prompting consistently improves response quality or shifts responses along different quality dimensions. The authors aim to disentangle these effects.

Method: Controlled comparison of four prompting conditions (no role prompt, generic domain-expert prompt, embedding-based role retrieval, hybrid retrieval) across 1,140 open-ended questions spanning 38 expert roles and six domains, evaluated with multi-metric analysis.

Result: Small aggregate differences between conditions, but metric-level analysis reveals a consistent tradeoff: role prompting increases expertise depth while reducing clarity. Effects are conditional—role prompting excels on advisory questions and in medicine/psychology; baseline performs better on conceptual/explanatory questions in finance, legal, science, and technology. Hybrid retrieval improves over embedding-only selection but does not eliminate the tradeoff.

Conclusion: Persona prompting reshapes response characteristics rather than broadly improving capability; multi-metric evaluation is necessary to understand its effects.

Abstract: Persona prompting is widely used to steer large language models, yet its practical value remains unclear. Prior work often evaluates persona prompting using aggregate scores, making it difficult to determine whether expert-role prompting consistently improves response quality or instead changes responses along different quality dimensions. We study this question through a controlled comparison of four prompting conditions across 1,140 open-ended questions spanning 38 expert roles and six domains: no role prompt, a generic domain-expert prompt, embedding-based role retrieval, and a hybrid retrieval method combining embedding search with LLM-based role selection. Aggregate results show only small overall differences between conditions. However, metric-level analysis reveals a consistent tradeoff that aggregate averages obscure: role prompting systematically increases expertise depth while reducing clarity. These effects are highly conditional rather than universal. Role prompting performs best on advisory questions and in domains such as medicine and psychology, where structured expert framing and risk communication are intrinsically valuable. In contrast, baseline prompting performs better on conceptual and explanatory questions in finance, legal, science, and technology domains, where concise plain-language explanation is more important. We further show that hybrid retrieval significantly improves over embedding-only role selection, although better role retrieval does not eliminate the broader expertise-depth versus clarity tradeoff. Overall, our findings suggest that persona prompting primarily reshapes response characteristics rather than broadly improving capability, and that multi-metric evaluation is necessary for understanding its effects.

</details>


### [50] [ReasonLight: A Multimodal Foundation Model-Enhanced Reinforcement Learning Framework for Zero-Shot Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29425)
*Aoyu Pang,Maonan Wang,Yuejiao Xie,Chung Shue Chen,Zhiwei Yang,Man-On Pun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReasonLight is a multimodal foundation model-enhanced RL framework that integrates structured traffic measurements, multi-view camera observations, and RL-proposed phase decisions to achieve zero-shot adaptation to rare, unseen events in traffic signal control. It uses semantic-guided refinement to adjust actions and reduces emergency vehicle waiting time by up to 88.7% without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL-based TSC methods rely on predefined states and fail to respond to open-world events (e.g., emergency vehicles, temporary regulations) not seen during training. IoT-enabled intersections offer heterogeneous sensor data, creating an opportunity to improve RL adaptability to such events.

Method: ReasonLight integrates three information sources: (1) structured traffic measurements from sensors, (2) multi-view camera observations, and (3) candidate phase decisions from a pre-trained RL controller. It extracts visual semantics from multi-view images, aligns them with sensor-derived scene descriptions, then uses a semantic-guided refinement module to preserve or adjust the RL-proposed action based on traffic rules and event semantics. Invalid actions are rejected with fallback to the original RL action.

Result: ReasonLight achieves zero-shot adaptation to emergency vehicle priority and temporary traffic regulation without retraining. It reduces emergency vehicle waiting time by up to 88.7% compared to the RL-only backbone while maintaining comparable routine traffic performance.

Conclusion: Integrating multimodal foundation models with RL enables robust zero-shot adaptation to rare, unseen events in traffic signal control, significantly improving emergency response without degrading normal traffic operation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise in traffic signal control (TSC). However, its reliance on predefined states limits responsiveness to observable open-world events that are absent from training data. IoT-enabled intersections provide heterogeneous observations from roadside sensors and cameras, creating opportunities to improve RL adaptability to such events. To this end, we propose ReasonLight, a multimodal foundation model-enhanced RL framework for zero-shot TSC. ReasonLight integrates three sources of information: structured traffic measurements, multi-view camera observations, and candidate phase decisions from a pre-trained RL controller. Given an RL-proposed phase, ReasonLight extracts visual semantics from multi-view images and aligns them with compact sensor-derived scene descriptions. This alignment enables a semantic-guided refinement module to either preserve or adjust the proposed action according to traffic rules and event semantics. To ensure operational reliability, refined actions are constrained by the set of available phases. Any invalid decision is rejected, and the system falls back to the original RL action. We evaluate ReasonLight on two types of rare events not seen during RL training: emergency vehicle priority and temporary traffic regulation. Experimental results show that ReasonLight achieves zero-shot adaptation without retraining. It reduces emergency vehicle waiting time by up to 88.7% compared with the RL-only backbone while preserving comparable routine traffic performance.

</details>


### [51] [Towards Human-Like Interactive Speech Recognition With Agentic Correction and Semantic Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29430)
*Zixuan Jiang,Yanqiao Zhu,Peng Wang,Qinyuan Chen,Xinjian Zhao,Xipeng Qiu,Wupeng Wang,Zhifu Gao,Xiangang Li,Kai Yu,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Agentic ASR, a closed-loop interactive framework that refines speech recognition through multi-turn semantic correction, intent routing, and reasoning-based editing, and introduces Sentence-level Semantic Error Rate (S²ER) as a better evaluation metric than WER/CER for meaning-critical errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ASR systems follow a single-pass paradigm misaligned with human communication, where misunderstandings are resolved iteratively. Token-level metrics like WER/CER fail to capture meaning-critical errors, making it difficult to correct them once they occur.

Method: The paper formulates Interactive ASR as a multi-turn refinement task and proposes Agentic ASR, a closed-loop framework combining a single-pass ASR front-end with semantic correction, intent routing, and reasoning-based editing. It also introduces S²ER (Sentence-level Semantic Error Rate) as an LLM-based semantic evaluation metric and an Interactive Simulation System for reproducible benchmarking.

Result: Experiments on multilingual, named-entity-intensive, and code-switching benchmarks show iterative interaction consistently reduces semantic errors, with larger gains in S²ER than in conventional token-level metrics. Human-AI alignment and ablation studies validate the reliability of the semantic judge and framework robustness.

Conclusion: The proposed interactive framework significantly improves semantic accuracy over single-pass ASR, especially for meaning-critical errors, and S²ER provides better evaluation alignment with human perception of recognition quality.

Abstract: Automatic speech recognition (ASR) is a core component of human--computer interaction and an increasingly important front-end for LLM-based assistants and agents. However, most current ASR systems still follow a single-pass paradigm, which is poorly aligned with human communication, where misunderstandings are resolved through iterative clarification and refinement.
