Fetal heart rate (FHR) monitoring plays a crucial role in assessing fetal well-being during prenatal care, enabling the detection of abnormal patterns and supporting timely obstetric interventions to mitigate fetal risks during labor. Applying artificial intelligence (AI) methods to analyze large datasets of continuous FHR monitoring episodes with diverse outcomes may offer novel insights into predicting the risk of needing breathing assistance or interventions. Recent advances in wearable FHR monitors have enabled continuous fetal monitoring without compromising maternal mobility. However, sensor displacement during maternal movement, as well as changes in fetal or maternal position, often lead to signal dropout, resulting in gaps in recorded FHR data. Such missing data limits the extraction of meaningful insights and complicates automated (AI-based) analysis. Traditional approaches to handling missing data, such as simple interpolation techniques, often fail to preserve the spectral characteristics of the signals. In this paper, we propose a masked transformer-based autoencoder approach to reconstruct missing FHR signals by capturing both local temporal and frequency components of the data. The proposed method demonstrates robustness across varying durations of missing data and can be used for signal inpainting and forecasting. The proposed approach can be applied retrospectively to research datasets to support the development of AI-based risk algorithms. In the future, the proposed method could be integrated into wearable FHR monitoring devices to achieve earlier and more robust risk detection.

</details>


### [78] [Beyond Trajectory Rewards: Step-level Credit Assignment for Agentic Search via Graph Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29697)
*Yuchen Liu,Yingjie Feng,Lixiong Qin,Jiasi Chen,Jianing Yu,Sheng Gao,Sheng Yang,Weiran Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GDCR proposes a step-level reward based on graph distance to answer nodes in an Entity-Relation graph, combined with Step Advantage Policy Optimization (SAPO) that integrates step-level and trajectory-level advantages for Agentic Search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory-level outcome rewards fail to quantify individual step contributions in Agentic Search, and existing step-level reward methods rely on costly tree sampling.

Method: Proposes Graph-Distance Contribution Reward (GDCR), a step-level process reward that scores newly-retrieved/ cited entities by their distance to the answer node in a training-time Entity-Relation (ER) graph. Further proposes Step Advantage Policy Optimization (SAPO) to convert GDCR into step-level advantages and combine with trajectory-level outcome advantages.

Result: Effectiveness validated on four challenging benchmarks.

Conclusion: Graph-distance-based step-level rewards combined with step-level and trajectory-level advantage optimization improve Agentic Search performance.

Abstract: In Agentic Search, trajectory-level outcome rewards fail to quantify the behavioral contributions of individual steps, while existing step-level reward methods typically rely on costly tree sampling. We view world knowledge as a latent world graph and each IS task as search within a latent task graph, where effective steps should make graph progress toward the answer node. Based on this prior, we propose Graph-Distance Contribution Reward (GDCR), a step-level process reward that scores newly-retrieved and newly-cited entities by their distance to the answer node in a training-time Entity-Relation (ER) graph. We further propose Step Advantage Policy Optimization (SAPO), which converts GDCR into step-level advantages and combines them with trajectory-level outcome advantages. Experiments on four challenging benchmarks validate the effectiveness of our method.

</details>


### [79] [BitTP: The Lightweight Trajectory Prediction Model with BitLLM for Edge-Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29705)
*Mincheol Kang,Hyunjin Lim,Bomin Kang,Daehee Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BitTP converts LLM-based trajectory predictors into lightweight 1.58-bit weight-quantized architectures, preserving full-precision activations, achieving 14.29% ADE and 20.97% FDE improvements while reducing memory and latency for edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based trajectory predictors are memory- and compute-intensive, making deployment on resource-constrained edge devices (e.g., autonomous robot onboard computers) impractical. BitTP aims to bridge this gap via efficient quantization.

Method: Proposes BitTP, which converts LLM-based trajectory predictors into bitlinear architectures using weight-only quantization to 1.58-bit (BitTP-Weight), while keeping activations in full precision to avoid spatio-temporal reasoning degradation.

Result: BitTP-Weight reduces ADE by 14.29% and FDE by 20.97% on average compared to full-precision BF16 baseline, while reducing memory usage and inference latency relative to other quantization methods.

Conclusion: Carefully designed quantization (weight-only 1.58-bit) acts as an effective regularizer, enabling practical deployment of sophisticated LLM-based trajectory reasoning on edge devices.

Abstract: Trajectory prediction is a fundamental task for autonomous systems, requiring complex reasoning about multi-agent interactions and intents. Large language models (LLMs) have recently been adopted for this task, as they provide strong contextual reasoning and interpretable, language-based trajectory representations. However, these LLM-based predictors are extremely memory- and compute-intensive, making them difficult to deploy on resource-constrained edge devices such as on-board computers in autonomous robots. To bridge this gap, we propose BitTP, which converts an LLM-based trajectory predictor into a lightweight bitlinear architecture. We demonstrate that weight-only quantization to 1.58-bit (BitTP-Weight) is optimal. Crucially, activations must remain in full precision, as quantizing them leads to severe degradation and instability in spatio-temporal reasoning. Empirically, BitTP-Weight not only preserves but improves prediction quality over the full-precision (BF16) LLM baseline, reducing ADE by 14.29% and FDE by 20.97% on average, while simultaneously reducing memory usage and inference latency relative to other quantization methods. These results demonstrate that carefully designed quantization acts as an effective regularizer, enabling the practical deployment of sophisticated LLM-based reasoning on edge devices. Code is available at: https://github.com/MintCat98/BitTP.

</details>


### [80] [NaRA: Noise-Aware LoRA for Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of Diffusion LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29716)
*Shuaidi Wang,Zhan Zhuang,Ruping Huang,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NaRA introduces noise-aware low-rank adaptation for dLLMs, using a hypernetwork conditioned on noise levels to generate dynamic LoRA matrices, outperforming noise-agnostic PEFT methods across reasoning and code generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PEFT methods (e.g., LoRA) were designed for autoregressive models and use static parameters agnostic to noise levels, failing to account for the dynamic input distributions and varying generation difficulty along the denoising trajectory in Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs).

Method: NaRA proposes a low-rank core matrix generated by a lightweight, globally shared hypernetwork conditioned on the noise level, allowing update matrices to vary continuously across the diffusion process with negligible parameter and latency overhead.

Result: Consistent improvements over noise-agnostic baselines on commonsense reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks.

Conclusion: NaRA effectively addresses the static parameter limitation of existing PEFT methods for dLLMs by incorporating noise-level awareness, achieving better performance without significant computational overhead.

Abstract: Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising non-autoregressive generative paradigm. Given the prohibitive computational cost of full fine-tuning, Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has become the standard approach. However, existing PEFT methods (e.g., LoRA), originally tailored for autoregressive models, rely on static parameters that are agnostic to the noise level. Consequently, they ignore the intrinsic dynamics of the diffusion process, where input distributions and generation difficulty shift significantly along the denoising trajectory, rendering them suboptimal for dLLMs. To address this, we propose Noise-aware Low-Rank Adaptation (NaRA), which introduces a low-rank core matrix generated by a lightweight, globally shared hypernetwork conditioned on the noise level. This design enables the update matrices to vary continuously along the diffusion process while keeping parameter and latency overhead negligible. We provide a theoretical justification for the proposed NaRA framework and empirically demonstrate consistent improvements over noise-agnostic baselines across commonsense reasoning, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks. Our code is available at https://github.com/generaldi/NaRA.

</details>


### [81] [Uncertainty-Aware Transfer Learning for Cross-Building Energy Forecasting: Toward Robust and Scalable District-Level Energy Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29733)
*Shadmehr Zaregarizi,Khashayar Yavari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an uncertainty-aware transfer learning framework using Temporal Fusion Transformer for cross-building energy forecasting, introducing the Transfer Robustness Index (TRI) metric. Probe-Only fine-tuning (updating only 455 of 806K parameters) achieves best transfer quality, and Monte Carlo Dropout provides 93.2% prediction interval coverage. Validated on educational and multi-typology building datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling energy forecasting to district level requires re-usable models across buildings with minimal target-domain data and reliable uncertainty estimates, which existing approaches lack.

Method: Uncertainty-aware transfer learning framework based on Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT), with a four-strategy layer-freezing ablation study, Transfer Robustness Index (TRI) for quantifying generalization, and Monte Carlo Dropout for uncertainty quantification.

Result: Probe-Only fine-tuning (455/806K parameters) achieves best transfer quality (TRI=3,097); Monte Carlo Dropout yields 93.2% prediction interval coverage; monotonic improvement with increasing target-domain data.

Conclusion: TFT encoders learn transferable temporal representations; Probe-Only fine-tuning is optimal for cross-building transfer; the framework provides practical guidance for district-level energy deployment with minimal target data.

Abstract: Scaling data-driven energy forecasting to district level requires models that can be re-used across buildings with minimal target-domain data and honest uncertainty estimates. We present an uncertainty-aware transfer learning (TL) framework for cross-building energy forecasting based on the Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT), evaluated on a newly released high-resolution real sub-meter dataset: an educational building at Aalborg University, Denmark (source) and the multi-typology NEST building at EMPA, Switzerland (target). We introduce the Transfer Robustness Index (TRI), an architecture-agnostic metric for quantifying generalization quality across domain gaps. A four-strategy layer-freezing ablation shows that Probe-Only fine-tuning, updating only 455 output-layer parameters out of 806K, achieves the best transfer quality (TRI = 3,097), outperforming full fine-tuning and suggesting that TFT encoders learn transferable temporal representations. Monte Carlo Dropout yields a prediction interval coverage probability of 93.2%, close to the nominal 95% target. A data-scarcity analysis further shows monotonic improvement with increasing target-domain data, providing practical guidance for district energy deployment.

</details>


### [82] [Citation-Closure Retrieval and Per-Rule Attribution for Real-World Regulatory Compliance Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29742)
*Yeong-Joon Ju,Seong-Whan Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RefWalk proposes RegOps-Bench, a benchmark for regulatory compliance QA using an Operational Knowledge Graph, and introduces a citation-traversal framework that improves retrieval recall and citation accuracy compared to existing RAG systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems fail at regulatory compliance QA due to flattened citation edges, fragmented retrieval expansions, and fragile post-hoc attribution; unlike traditional multi-hop or legal QA, this task requires structured procedural lookups and evidence-set closure across multi-tiered authority hierarchies.

Method: RefWalk uses a shared topic anchor to traverse cross-document citations, fuse multi-view candidates via max-based aggregation, and enforce per-rule attribution to map claims to sources explicitly. RegOps-Bench is built from an Operational Knowledge Graph derived from complex national R&D regulations.

Result: Substantial improvements in retrieval recall and citation accuracy over existing systems. Contrastive evaluation on HIPAA shows existing systems saturate on flat-structure rules, validating the need for RegOps-Bench.

Conclusion: RefWalk establishes a strong baseline for Regulatory Compliance QA, and RegOps-Bench fills a gap where existing benchmarks and systems fall short on structured, citation-intensive compliance tasks.

Abstract: Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) for regulatory compliance demands rigorous traceability via comprehensive citations across multi-tiered authority structures. Unlike traditional multi-hop or legal QA, this task requires structured procedural lookups and evidence-set closure rather than entity resolution or case-law reasoning. Existing RAG systems struggle here due to flattened citation edges, fragmented retrieval expansions, and fragile post-hoc attribution. We formalize Regulatory Compliance QA with RegOps-Bench, a novel benchmark featuring an Operational Knowledge Graph derived from complex national R\&D regulations. To address these bottlenecks, we propose RefWalk, a unified framework driven by a shared topic anchor. RefWalk traverses cross-document citations, fuses multi-view candidates via max-based aggregation, and enforces per-rule attribution to explicitly map claims to sources. We establish a strong baseline with substantial improvements in retrieval recall and citation accuracy. Finally, a contrastive evaluation on a U.S. health compliance dataset (HIPAA) reveals that existing systems exhibit saturation on flat-structure rules, underscoring the need for RegOps-Bench. Our code is available at https://github.com/yeongjoonJu/RefWalk.

</details>


### [83] [Why Specialist Models Still Matter: A Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Paradigm for Medical Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29744)
*Yanan Wang,Shuaicong Hu,Jian Liu,Guohui Zhou,Aiguo Wang,Cuiwei Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HetMedAgent proposes a heterogeneous multi-agent framework that orchestrates collaboration between generalist LLMs, domain-specific specialist models, and clinicians for clinical decision-making, outperforming models used in isolation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the question of whether domain-specific medical specialist models will become obsolete with the rise of generalist LLMs, and to demonstrate that collaboration between both types of models plus clinicians is superior to either alone.

Method: HetMedAgent is a heterogeneous medical multi-agent framework featuring conflict-aware evidence fusion, uncertainty-based clinician intervention triggering, and adaptive threshold calibration.

Result: On three real-world clinical decision-making tasks, synergy between generalist LLMs and domain-specific specialist models significantly outperforms using either type alone, validating the irreplaceable value of specialist models in modality-specific analysis.

Conclusion: The future of medical AI lies in multi-agent collaboration rather than building monolithic medical foundation models; specialist models remain valuable for modality-specific precision when combined with generalist reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: The impressive performance of generalist large language models (LLMs) such as GPT and Claude in healthcare raises a critical question: will domain-specific medical specialist models become obsolete? We argue that the future of medical artificial intelligence (AI) lies not in building monolithic medical foundation models, nor in replacing human expertise, but in orchestrating collaboration among generalist LLMs, domain-specific specialist models, and clinicians. We propose HetMedAgent, a heterogeneous medical multi-agent framework that enables conflict-aware evidence fusion, uncertainty-based clinician intervention triggering, and adaptive threshold calibration. Experiments on three real-world clinical decision-making tasks demonstrate that the synergy between generalist LLMs and domain-specific specialist models significantly outperforms using either type of model alone, validating the irreplaceable value of specialist models in modality-specific analysis. HetMedAgent represents a shift from building medical LLMs or foundation models to multi-agent collaboration, achieving a balance between general reasoning capabilities and domain-specific precision.

</details>


### [84] [Benchmarking Positional Encoding Strategies for Transformer-Based EEG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29754)
*Ayse Betul Yuce,Sebastian Stober*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper benchmarks five positional encoding strategies for transformer-based EEG foundation models, finding that no single strategy universally outperforms across tasks—SPE works best for motor imagery, while ACPE is more consistent across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised EEG decoding models struggle to generalize across tasks, subjects, and datasets. Transformer-based EEG foundation models with self-supervised learning require explicit positional information, but unlike text tokens, EEG electrodes are spatially distributed on the scalp, raising the question of how to optimally encode electrode positions.

Method: Five positional encoding strategies were benchmarked within the CBraMod backbone and evaluated under linear probing and fine-tuning protocols on motor imagery classification and emotion recognition tasks.

Result: No single positional encoding strategy consistently outperforms across tasks. SPE yields strong representations for motor imagery but underperforms on emotion recognition, while ACPE demonstrates more consistent performance across tasks.

Conclusion: The optimal positional encoding strategy is task-dependent, with no universal solution across EEG decoding scenarios.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) is a widely used non-invasive technique for measuring brain activity in brain-computer interface (BCI) applications. Supervised EEG decoding models often struggle to generalize across tasks, subjects, and datasets, motivating transformer-based EEG foundation models trained with self-supervised learning. Since transformers are permutation-invariant, they require explicit positional information. Unlike textual tokens, EEG electrodes are spatially distributed across the scalp, raising the question of how electrode positions should be encoded in transformer-based EEG models. In this study, we benchmark five positional encoding strategies within the CBraMod backbone and evaluate them under linear probing and fine-tuning protocols on motor imagery classification and emotion recognition. Our results show that no single strategy consistently outperforms across tasks. Spherical Positional Encoding (SPE) yields strong representations for motor imagery but underperforms on emotion recognition, while Asymmetric Conditional Positional Encoding (ACPE) demonstrates more consistent performance across tasks. These findings suggest that the optimal positional encoding strategy is task-dependent, with no universal solution across EEG decoding scenarios.

</details>


### [85] [LFQ: Logit-aware Final-block Quantization for Boosting the Generation Quality of Low-Bit Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29756)
*Jung Hyun Lee,June Yong Yang,Jungwook Choi,Eunho Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LFQ improves low-bit weight-only post-training quantization for LLMs by addressing token probability misalignment in generative tasks. It quantizes the final Transformer block using cross-entropy loss on logits, outperforming standard block-wise PTQ on complex generation while matching FP baselines on language modeling and understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing block-wise PTQ methods degrade on generative tasks (longer responses, extended chain-of-thought) due to: (i) omitting the unembedding layer (LM head) from optimization, and (ii) using MSE loss, both of which cause token probability distribution misalignment between quantized and full-precision models.

Method: Logit-aware Final-block Quantization (LFQ) — enhances block-wise PTQ by quantizing the final Transformer block with a cross-entropy objective between FP model logits and quantized model logits, directly aligning token probabilities at the logit level.

Result: LFQ consistently improves accuracy on complex generation tasks over state-of-the-art block-wise PTQ across diverse model families, while maintaining parity with FP baselines on language modeling and understanding tasks.

Conclusion: Aligning token probability distributions at the logit level in the final block is a simple yet effective fix for the generative task degradation in low-bit weight-only PTQ, without sacrificing performance on standard NLP benchmarks.

Abstract: As large language models continue to scale, low-bit weight-only post-training quantization (PTQ) offers a practical solution to their memory-efficient deployment. Although block-wise PTQ is capable of matching the full-precision (FP) baseline on basic language modeling and understanding, its quality is degraded for generative tasks -- especially at longer responses and extended chains of thought, which is critical in boosting task accuracy. We attribute this shortfall to two factors: (i) the omission of the unembedding layer (the LM head) in block-wise optimization and (ii) the reliance on the mean squared error (MSE) objective. Both factors cause the token probability distribution of the quantized model to misalign with that of the FP model, yielding notable accuracy drops on text generation benchmarks. To rectify the discrepancy, we introduce Logit-aware Final-block Quantization (LFQ), a simple yet effective enhancement to block-wise PTQ that quantizes the final Transformer block by minimizing the cross-entropy between the logits of the FP model and those of its quantized counterpart. By aligning token probabilities at the logit level in the final block, LFQ consistently improves the accuracy of complex generation tasks over state-of-the-art block-wise PTQ across diverse model families, while maintaining parity with FP baselines on language modeling and understanding.

</details>


### [86] [From XXLTraffic to EvoXXLTraffic: Scaling Traffic Forecasting to Sensor-Evolving Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29768)
*Du Yin,Hao Xue,Arian Prabowo,Shuang Ao,Flora Salim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces XXLTraffic and EvoXXLTraffic, large-scale traffic forecasting datasets spanning up to 27 years with evolving sensor networks, and proposes a yearly streaming forecasting protocol that reveals many SOTA methods fail under realistic evolutionary conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing traffic forecasting benchmarks assume a fixed sensor set, but real road-sensor networks grow continuously as road networks change over time, creating a gap between benchmark assumptions and real-world conditions.

Method: The authors create the XXLTraffic dataset family (27 years of PeMS and NSW data) with fixed-sensor subsets for long-horizon forecasting, and extend it to EvoXXLTraffic with per-year active sensors, yearly traffic matrices, and yearly graph snapshots across nine PeMS districts. They define a yearly streaming forecasting protocol where each calendar year is a continual task, and benchmark static spatio-temporal GNNs, online schemes, evolving-graph continual methods, and retrieval/test-time methods.

Result: The dataset reveals growth ratios ranging from +305% to over +10,000%. Many state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods that work on static benchmarks no longer perform well under this evolutionary setting, demonstrating that the ultra-large evolutionary dataset better reflects real-world conditions.

Conclusion: The EvoXXLTraffic dataset complements existing benchmarks by enabling more realistic forecasting under ultra-long evolutionary road networks, and reveals limitations of current SOTA methods in handling sensor evolution.

Abstract: Existing traffic forecasting benchmarks assume a fixed sensor set, but real road-sensor networks grow continuously as the road network changes year by year. We introduce the XXLTraffic dataset family, which spans up to 27 years of California PeMS and Transport for NSW data. The fixed-sensor subsets of XXLTraffic support extremely long forecasting with multi-year gaps and standard hourly / daily long-horizon forecasting. We extend it to EvoXXLTraffic, a sensor-evolving reorganization that exposes per-year active sensors, yearly traffic-flow matrices, and yearly graph snapshots across nine PeMS districts, with growth ratios ranging from +305% to over +10,000%. We define a yearly streaming forecasting protocol on EvoXXLTraffic in which each calendar year is a continual task, and benchmark a wide range of representative baselines drawn from static spatio-temporal GNNs, naïve online schemes, evolving-graph continual methods, and retrieval / test-time methods. We find that our ultra-large evolutionary dataset better reflects the real world, and many state-of-the-art (SOTA) results no longer work. Our dataset complements existing benchmarks by enabling more realistic forecasting under ultra-long evolutionary road networks.

</details>


### [87] [Croissant Tasks: A Metadata Format for Reproducible Machine Learning Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29786)
*Omar Benjelloun,Leonardo Martins Bianco,Isabelle Guyon,Thanh Gia Hieu Khuong,Jonathan Lebensold,Sebastian Lobentanzer,Luis Oala,Benedictus Kent Rachmat,Ihsan Ullah,Peyman Vahidi,Joaquin Vanschoren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Croissant Tasks is a declarative, machine-actionable metadata format that abstracts ML implementations into high-level task specifications, enabling conceptual reproducibility through autonomous agent-generated reimplementations rather than direct code replication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducibility remains a critical challenge in ML due to underspecified execution details and brittle software environments. Human-centric remedies (checklists, manual verification) require intensive effort and fail to scale.

Method: (1) Croissant Tasks specification that formally decouples task problem from solution; (2) an automated LLM pipeline that retrofits existing benchmarks into this format; (3) empirical validation using autonomous agents to ingest specifications and generate functional reproduction pipelines.

Result: Autonomous agents can ingest Croissant Tasks specifications to generate functional, accurate reproduction pipelines from scratch.

Conclusion: This format is envisioned as a new foundation for automated and conceptual reproducibility in machine learning.

Abstract: Reproducibility is fundamental to the scientific method, yet remains a critical challenge in machine learning. Contributing factors include underspecified execution details and brittle software environments. Human-centric remedies, such as checklists and manual verification, help but require intensive effort and fail to scale. To address this, we introduce Croissant Tasks: a declarative, machine-actionable metadata format that abstracts low-level implementation details into high-level specifications. This format enables conceptual reproducibility: verifying claims via independent, agent-generated implementations rather than brittle source code replication. We contribute: (1) the Croissant Tasks specification, formally decoupling task problem from solution; (2) an automated LLM pipeline that retrofits existing benchmarks into this format; and (3) empirical validation showing autonomous agents can ingest these specifications to generate functional, accurate reproduction pipelines from scratch. We envision this format as a new foundation for automated and conceptual reproducibility in machine learning.

</details>


### [88] [Certified Policy Optimisation for Nested Causal Bandits via PAC-Bayes Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29788)
*Tim Woydt,Paul-David Zuercher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs) to model hierarchical causal decision-making across timescales, proposes Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS) with a PAC-Bayesian excess-risk certificate for safe deployment, and demonstrates superior zero-shot transfer and certified handover from legacy to learned controllers under distribution shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture the causal coupling between strategic (meta) and tactical (inner) decisions across timescales, where a strategic decision causally shapes the context for all subsequent tactical choices.

Method: Formalizes the problem as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs) with a hierarchical SCM; proposes Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS) which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively; derives a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound for off-policy, anytime certification.

Result: NCTS outperforms RFF-GP joint regression in zero-shot transfer under exogenous shifts; recursive meta-to-inner commit dominates joint-commit alternatives; the PAC-Bayesian certificate contracts with more offline data; progressive certified handover enables safe timescale-independent flipping from legacy to learned controllers.

Conclusion: The factorised SCM-mechanism posterior with recursive decision-making provides certifiably safe hierarchical bandit learning, enabling progressive certified handover for deployment where each timescale can be independently trusted and switched.

Abstract: Critical sequential decisions are rarely single-timescale: a strategic decision causally shapes the context in which every subsequent tactical choice is made; standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture this causal coupling between timescales. We formalise the problem class as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs), a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution, and propose Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS), which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively under it. Our main theoretical result is a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound that certifies any candidate deployment policy from historic data alone, off-policy and anytime, answering the deployment question: can we trust this agent here, and at what risk? Experiments on a hierarchical SCM show that, against a matched RFF-GP joint regression on the same function class, the factorised SCM-mechanism posterior transfers significantly better zero-shot under exogenous distribution shifts, the recursive meta-to-inner commit significantly dominates the joint-commit alternative in distribution, and the certificate significantly contracts as offline data accumulates. Combining these results, we establish progressive certified handover, a safe-deployment method: each timescale flips from a legacy controller to NCTS when gains can be certified, independently of the others.

</details>


### [89] [SkillsInjector: Dynamic Skill Context Construction for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29794)
*Yanchao Li,Wanhao Liu,Ben Gao,Jiaqing Xie,Zhehong Ai,Na Zou,Yuqiang Li,Tianfan Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillsInjector is a two-stage adaptive method that jointly optimizes skill selection, budgeting, and presentation for LLM agents, achieving significant improvements across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static skill injection methods can degrade agent performance because they use fixed selection criteria, predetermined budgets, and leave descriptions unchanged, failing to account for how skill exposure, quantity, and presentation affect downstream task completion.

Method: SkillsInjector has two stages: (1) a context planner that learns execution-grounded skill preferences and adaptively determines how many skills to include per task; (2) a set-aware renderer that tailors skill descriptions relative to their co-injected neighbors.

Result: Outperforms strongest baselines by 3.9, 6.1, and 7.3 percentage points on tau2-bench, SkillsBench, and ALFWorld respectively. Ablations confirm skill selection, adaptive budgeting, and set-aware rendering each contribute.

Conclusion: Skill-augmented LLM agents benefit from actively optimizing the injected context itself rather than treating skill injection as a static step.

Abstract: LLM agents now draw on growing skill libraries to handle complex tasks. However, injecting more skills does not always improve task completion and can even degrade it. Existing methods still treat skill injection as a static step, selecting skills with fixed criteria, fixing the budget in advance, and leaving descriptions unchanged. We argue that this static treatment can undermine the utility of skills, because which skills are exposed, how many are included, and how they are presented all affect downstream performance. We propose SkillsInjector, a two-stage adaptive method that jointly addresses these decisions. First, a context planner learns execution-grounded skill preferences and admits an adaptive number of skills for each task. A set-aware renderer then tailors how selected descriptions are presented relative to their co-injected neighbors. Across tau2-bench, SkillsBench, and ALFWorld, SkillsInjector achieves the highest score, improving over the strongest baseline by 3.9, 6.1, and 7.3 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies show that skill selection, adaptive budgeting, and set-aware rendering each contribute to the gain. These results show that skill-augmented agents benefit from optimizing the injected context itself. Code will be released upon publication

</details>


### [90] [MEMENTO: Leveraging Web as a Learning Signal for Low-Data Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29795)
*Ashutosh Ojha,Vinay Aggarwal,Ashutosh Srivastava,Siddharth Yedlapati,Yaman K Singla,Jitendra Ajmera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEMENTO is a framework that treats the web as a learning signal rather than a stateless retrieval interface, enabling agents to iteratively explore the web within sessions via an Adaptive Exploration Tree (AET) and accumulate knowledge across sessions through dual-channel memory (declarative + procedural), achieving significant performance gains over ReAct baselines (+25.6% on sales automation, +36.5% on legal research) in low-data domains without additional model training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing low-data learning approaches (few-shot prompting, instruction tuning, synthetic data generation) still rely on labeled or pseudo-labeled data as the primary learning signal. In contrast, humans learn through repeated, self-directed web interaction, progressively refining both knowledge and search strategies. This gap motivates a framework that leverages the web itself as a scalable learning source.

Method: MEMENTO operates at two levels: (1) Within-session: uses an Adaptive Exploration Tree (AET) that decomposes tasks into evolving questions and reflects on intermediate findings for iterative web exploration. (2) Across-sessions: accumulates experience via dual-channel memory, separating declarative knowledge (facts) from procedural knowledge (search strategies), enabling reusable learning from web interaction trajectories without additional model training.

Result: Evaluated on sales automation and legal research, MEMENTO achieves +25.6% improvement over ReAct-based baselines on sales automation and +36.5% on legal research, demonstrating that the web can serve as a scalable learning source for task-specific expertise in data-scarce settings.

Conclusion: The web can serve as a scalable learning signal for acquiring task-specific expertise in low-data regimes. By separating declarative and procedural knowledge across sessions, MEMENTO enables agents to learn reusable research strategies and domain expertise without additional model training, significantly outperforming ReAct-based approaches.

Abstract: Real-world tasks often lack large labeled datasets, motivating extensive work on learning in low-data regimes. However, existing approaches such as few-shot prompting, instruction tuning, and synthetic data generation, continue to treat labeled or pseudo-labeled data as the primary learning signal. In contrast, human practitioners acquire expertise through repeated, self-directed interaction with the open web, progressively refining both domain knowledge and search strategies. We propose MEMENTO, a framework that treats the web as a learning signal rather than a stateless retrieval interface. MEMENTO operates at two levels: within each session, it conducts iterative web exploration via an Adaptive Exploration Tree (AET) that decomposes tasks into evolving questions and reflects on intermediate findings; across sessions, it accumulates experience through dual-channel memory, separating declarative knowledge (facts) from procedural knowledge (search strategies). This design enables agents to learn reusable research strategies and domain expertise from trajectories of web interaction without additional model training. We evaluate MEMENTO on two low-data professional domains: sales automation and legal research. Our empirical results show consistent improvements in performance over ReAct based baselines (+25.6% on sales automation and 36.5% on legal research), demonstrating that the web can serve as a scalable learning source for acquiring task-specific expertise in data-scarce settings.

</details>


### [91] [SAAS: Self-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Over-Search Mitigation in Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29796)
*Yunbo Tang,Chengyi Yang,Shiyu Liu,Zhishang Xiang,Zerui Chen,Qinggang Zhang,Jinsong Su*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAAS is an RL framework that trains LLM agents to become self-aware of their knowledge boundaries, reducing unnecessary searches (over-search) in multi-hop question answering while maintaining accuracy, via search boundary modeling, boundary-aware rewards, and stage-wise optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic search systems suffer from "over-search": LLM agents lack self-awareness of their own knowledge boundaries, triggering unnecessary searches when internal knowledge suffices and failing to stop even after collecting adequate evidence, leading to high latency and computational cost.

Method: SAAS introduces three components: (i) search boundary modeling — identifies when the evolving policy can answer without search by contrasting search-disabled vs search-enabled rollouts; (ii) boundary-aware reward module — translates boundary awareness into trajectory-level penalties to suppress unnecessary/redundant searches; (iii) stage-wise optimization — uses a sequential curriculum that prioritizes reasoning before search regularization to avoid reward hacking.

Result: Extensive experiments show SAAS substantially reduces over-search while maintaining accuracy.

Conclusion: SAAS effectively cultivates dynamic self-awareness in LLM agents to regulate search behavior, solving the over-search problem without sacrificing answer accuracy, via a carefully designed RL framework with boundary modeling and curriculum optimization.

Abstract: Agentic search enables LLMs to solve complex multi-hop questions through iterative reasoning and external search. Despite the effectiveness, these systems often suffer from a critical limitation in practice: agents fail to recognize their own knowledge boundaries, blindly triggering searches when internal knowledge suffices and failing to terminate search even when adequate evidence has been collected. The lack of self-awareness leads to severe \textbf{over-search}, incurring substantial inference latency and prohibitive computational cost. To this end, we propose SAAS, a novel RL framework designed to cultivate dynamic self-awareness that precisely regulates search behavior without compromising accuracy. SAAS introduces three key components: (i) a search boundary modeling mechanism, which identifies the search boundary under the evolving policy by contrasting search-disabled and search-enabled rollouts; (ii) a boundary-aware reward module, which translates this boundary awareness into trajectory-level penalties, suppressing unnecessary and redundant searches; and (iii) a stage-wise optimization strategy, which leverages a sequential curriculum to prioritize reasoning over search regularization, thereby avoiding reward hacking. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SAAS substantially reduces over-search, while maintaining accuracy. Our code is anonymously released at https://github.com/XMUDeepLIT/SAAS.

</details>


### [92] [AgentDoG 1.5: A Lightweight and Scalable Alignment Framework for AI Agent Safety and Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29801)
*Dongrui Liu,Yu Li,Zhonghao Yang,Peng Wang,Guanxu Chen,Yuejin Xie,Qinghua Mao,Wanying Qu,Yanxu Zhu,Tianyi Zhou,Leitao Yuan,Zhijie Zheng,Qihao Lin,Yimin Wang,Haoyu Luo,Shuai Shao,Chen Qian,Qingyu Liu,Ling Tang,Ruiyang Qin,Qihan Ren,Junxiao Yang,Kun Wang,Zhiheng Xi,Linfeng Zhang,Ranjie Duan,Bo Zhang,Wenjie Wang,Wen Shen,Qiaosheng Zhang,Yan Teng,Chaochao Lu,Rui Mei,Man Li,Jialing Tao,Xi Lin,Tianhang Zheng,Yong Liu,Quanshi Zhang,Lei Zhu,Xingjun Ma,Junhua Liu,Hui Xue,Xiaoxiang Zuo,Xiangnan He,Chao Shen,Xianglong Liu,Minlie Huang,Jing Shao,Xia Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentDoG 1.5 is a lightweight, scalable agent safety alignment framework that achieves state-of-the-art safety moderation using only ~1k samples across 0.8B–8B parameter models, with two orders of magnitude reduction in deployment overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern open-world agents (e.g., OpenClaw) and frontier AI models introduce new safety risks and lower attack barriers, making existing agent alignment frameworks inadequate for real-world deployment.

Method: Updates agent safety taxonomy for emergent Codex/OpenClaw risks; builds taxonomy-guided data engine with influence-function purification; trains lightweight AgentDoG 1.5 variants (0.8B–8B) with ~1k samples; constructs efficient safety SFT and RL training environment reducing Docker-level overhead by two orders of magnitude.

Result: AgentDoG 1.5 achieves comparable performance to leading closed-source models (e.g., GPT-5.4) with only ~1k training samples and state-of-the-art safety moderation in diverse interactive agentic scenarios, with significant deployment efficiency gains.

Conclusion: The framework provides a scalable, training-free online guardrail for real-time safety moderation, with all models and datasets publicly released.

Abstract: Modern open-world agents such as OpenClaw exhibit powerful cross-environment execution capabilities yet introduce broad new safety risk sources. Meanwhile, advanced frontier AI models drastically lower attack barriers, rendering current agent alignment frameworks inadequate for real-world deployment. To tackle these emerging threats, we propose a lightweight and scalable agent safety alignment framework. Specifically, we update the agent safety taxonomy to accommodate emergent risks from Codex and OpenClaw execution scenarios. We further build a taxonomy-guided data engine with influence-function purification to train lightweight AgentDoG 1.5 variants (0.8B, 2B, 4B, and 8B parameters) using only around 1k samples, achieving comparable performance with leading closed-source models (e.g., GPT-5.4). Based on AgentDoG 1.5, we construct a highly efficient agentic safety SFT and RL training environment, which reduces deployment overhead in Docker-level environments by two orders of magnitude. Finally, we deploy AgentDoG 1.5 as a training-free online guardrail for real-time safety moderation. Extensive experimental results indicate that AgentDoG 1.5 achieves state-of-the-art performance in diverse and complex interactive agentic scenarios. All models and datasets are openly released.

</details>


### [93] [PRAIB: Peer Review AI Benchmark of Behaviour of LLM-Assisted Reviewing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29815)
*Krzysztof Żurawicki,Julia Farganus,Arkadiusz Gaweł,Mateusz Bystroński,Tomasz Jan Kajdanowicz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PRAIB is a novel benchmark framework that evaluates how LLMs diverge from human reviewers across metrics of specificity, style, and engagement behavior. Using 11,000 machine-generated reviews for 1,000 papers, the study finds LLM ratings are less variable, positively biased, overconfident, and produce longer reviews that miss atomic weaknesses humans identify.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLMs engage with scientific manuscripts like human reviewers or merely produce review-looking text, given the growing interest in using LLMs to scale the peer review process.

Method: Introduces PRAIB (Peer Review AI Benchmark) with metrics for review specificity, style, and engagement behavior. Conducts a large-scale empirical study with 11,000 reviews from 5 proprietary and open-source LLMs for 1,000 ICLR and NeurIPS papers (2021–2025), comparing machine-generated reviews against original human feedback across diverse prompting strategies.

Result: LLM reviews diverge significantly from human reviewers: LLM ratings are less variable, positively biased, and overconfident; cross-reference patterns are model-dependent and distinct from human norms; LLMs generate longer, more complex reviews but frequently overlook atomic weaknesses noted by humans.

Conclusion: PRAIB provides a diagnostic tool for identifying which aspects of the review process LLMs can reliably support today and which require further development before deployment.

Abstract: The growing number of submitted papers has motivated the exploration of Large Language Models (LLMs) as a means to support and augment the peer review process, particularly in terms of improving its speed and scalability. Yet, it remains unknown whether LLMs engage with scientific manuscripts in the same manner as human reviewers, or whether they merely produce review-looking text. To address this, we introduce the Peer Review AI Benchmark (PRAIB), a novel framework comprising thoroughly defined metrics that measure review specificity, style, and behavior of engagement. To complement the PRAIB framework, we conduct a large-scale empirical study leveraging a dataset of 11,000 reviews generated by five proprietary and open-source models for 1,000 ICLR and NeurIPS papers. Spanning the 2021--2025 period, these machine-generated reviews are compared against original human feedback across diverse prompting strategies to identify systematic behavioral divergences. Our analysis reveals that the generated reviews diverge significantly from feedback provided by human reviewers: LLM ratings are less variable, positively biased, and overconfident, and their cross-reference patterns are model-dependent and distinct from human norms. Furthermore, when evaluated through PRAIB, we observe that LLMs tend to generate longer, more complex reviews, yet frequently overlook the atomic weaknesses noted by human reviewers. By characterizing where and how LLMs reviewing behavior departs from human norms, PRAIB provides the community with a diagnostic tool for identifying which aspects of the review process LLMs can reliably support today and which require further development before deployment.

</details>


### [94] [Harnessing non-adversarial robustness in large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29816)
*Qinghua Zhou,Ellina Aleshina,Andrey Lovyagin,Oleg Somov,Mikhail Seleznyov,Alexander Panchenko,Ivan Oseledets,Elena Tutubalina,Ivan Y. Tyukin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs can be made robust to semantically-neutral prompt variations without full retraining, proposing and validating a debiasing fine-tuning approach that enhances robustness and provides certification against random prompt perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent works show that semantically similar but textually different prompts significantly impact LLM performance, raising the question of whether robustness can be achieved without expensive full-model retraining.

Method: The authors conduct theoretical analysis identifying a perturbation-induced bias in neural network outputs as a key factor affecting robustness, then propose a simple fine-tuning process called "debiasing for robustness," identifying conditions under which it succeeds or fails.

Result: Through both theory and extensive experiments, debiasing for robustness is shown to be a quick and efficient tool that enhances LLM robustness and provides certification against random prompt perturbations.

Conclusion: Robustness to semantically-neutral prompt alterations can be acquired without expensive retraining via a simple debiasing fine-tuning approach, which also enables certification against random perturbations.

Abstract: The work presents an approach for addressing the challenge of robustness in Large Language Models (LLMs) to alterations and potential errors caused by semantically similar but textually different prompts. Recent works have shown that these kinds of prompt variations can significantly impact the performance of LLMs on tasks. The central question is: can LLMs' robustness to semantically-neutral prompt alterations be acquired without expensive retraining of the entire model? We address this question both theoretically and through experiments. Our theoretical analysis reveals a crucial factor impacting model robustness - a systematic expected shift or perturbation-induced bias in neural network module outputs. Motivated by this analysis, we show that robustness can be achieved via a simple fine-tuning process: debiasing for robustness. We identify conditions when debiasing helps and when it does not, and demonstrate, through both theory and extensive experiments, that debiasing for robustness may indeed be a quick and efficient tool to enhance robustness and provide certification against random prompt perturbations.

</details>


### [95] [Quantifying and Optimizing Simplicity via Polynomial Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29823)
*Tianren Zhang,Xiangxin Li,Minghao Xiao,Guanyu Chen,Feng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces polynomial representations as a distribution-aware, low-dimensional surrogate for neural functions, using orthogonal polynomial bases along data-dependent interpolation paths. The effective degree of this representation serves as a simplicity metric predictive of generalization, outperforming sharpness-based proxies. A differentiable simplicity regularizer derived from this representation consistently improves generalization across image/text classification, vision-language fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep networks exhibit a simplicity bias crucial for generalization, but a broadly applicable, quantitative measure of simplicity has been lacking.

Method: Approximate a network's predictive behavior along data-dependent interpolation paths using orthogonal polynomial bases to obtain compact functional representations; use the effective degree as a simplicity metric and derive a differentiable regularizer.

Result: The proposed simplicity metric predicts generalization across tasks and architectures, consistently outperforming existing proxies like sharpness. The simplicity regularizer improves generalization in image classification, text classification, vision-language model fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: Polynomial representations provide an effective, distribution-aware simplicity measure and regularizer that enhances generalization across diverse domains.

Abstract: Deep networks often exhibit a preference for "simple" solutions, and such a simplicity bias is widely believed to play a key role in generalization. Yet a broadly applicable, quantitative measure of simplicity remains elusive. We introduce polynomial representations as a distribution-aware, low-dimensional surrogate for neural functions: we approximate a network's predictive behavior along data-dependent interpolation paths using orthogonal polynomial bases, yielding a compact functional representation. We show that the effective degree of this representation serves as a practical simplicity metric that is predictive of generalization across tasks and architectures, and consistently outperforms existing generalization proxies such as sharpness. Finally, polynomial representations naturally yield a differentiable simplicity regularizer, which consistently improves generalization in image and text classification, fine-tuning contrastive vision-language models, and reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [96] [OptSkills: Learning Generalizable Optimization Skills from Problem Archetypes via Cluster-Based Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29829)
*Haochen Yang,Ke Zhao,Mengyuan Ma,Xingyu Lu,Xiangfeng Wang,Hong Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OptSkills is an LLM-based agent system that learns archetype-centric, workflow-level skills from optimization problem clusters to improve generalization across narrative variants, shifted problem types, and emerging domains, achieving SOTA accuracy of 68.27% on diverse benchmarks and 26.91% on MIPLIB-NL (outperforming DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking by 4.53%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based automated optimization methods suffer from limited generalization: they are sensitive to superficial narrative changes, rely on case-level experience reuse, and fail to adapt to shifted or emerging problem types.

Method: OptSkills clusters problems by underlying archetypes (not surface narratives), distills successful trajectories into reusable workflow-level skills via diverse modeling and solver exploration within each cluster, and refines/expands the skill library with new trajectories for out-of-distribution adaptation.

Result: 68.27% micro-averaged accuracy on diverse benchmarks; 26.91% on MIPLIB-NL (4.53% higher than DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking); 72.79% on OOD NLCO benchmark after skill learning on Nano-CO.

Conclusion: Archetype-centric skill learning significantly improves robust, in-distribution, and out-of-distribution generalization for LLM-based automated optimization modeling and solving.

Abstract: Leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically formulate and solve optimization problems from natural language has emerged as an efficient paradigm for automated optimization. However, existing methods still exhibit limited generalization: they are sensitive to superficial narrative variations, reuse experience mainly at the case level, and struggle to adapt to shifted or emerging problem types. We propose OptSkills, an archetype-centric skill learning and reasoning agent system for optimization modeling and solving. To improve robust generalization, our system clusters problems by their underlying archetypes rather than surface narratives. To improve in-distribution generalization, it explores diverse modeling paradigms and solver configurations within each cluster, then distills successful trajectories into reusable workflow-level skills. To improve out-of-distribution generalization, it refines existing skills or expands the skill library using newly obtained trajectories. Our system achieves a state-of-the-art micro-averaged accuracy of 68.27% on datasets encompassing diverse problem types and scenarios. In addition, on MIPLIB-NL, a highly challenging large-scale and high-dimensional benchmark, it achieves 26.91% accuracy, outperforming DeepSeek-V3.2-Thinking by 4.53%. After skill learning on Nano-CO, it reaches 72.79% on the OOD NLCO benchmark. Code and skills are available at https://github.com/fujiwaranoM0kou/OptSkills.

</details>


### [97] [OmniMatBench: A Human-Calibrated Multimodal Reasoning Benchmark Across 19 Materials Science Subfields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29833)
*Wanhao Liu,Jiaqing Xie,Qian Tan,Weida Wang,Jue Wang,Ran Sun,Zhuo Yang,Wanli Ouyang,Lei Bai,Tianfan Fu,Lu Chen,Xin Chen,Yuqiang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniMatBench is a human-calibrated multimodal reasoning benchmark with 3,171 expert-curated problems across 19 materials-science subfields, revealing that top MLLMs achieve only a 0.372 overall score, indicating substantial gaps in materials-science reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing materials benchmarks focus narrowly on property prediction, knowledge QA, or characterization understanding, but fail to assess the broader reasoning process from materials knowledge to application. OmniMatBench fills this gap.

Method: The benchmark comprises 3,171 expert-curated QA and calculation problems spanning 19 subfields across four categories: fundamental knowledge, structural/engineering materials, processing/manufacturing, and functional/applied materials. 13 open-source and closed-source MLLMs were evaluated.

Result: Best model achieves only 0.372 overall score; strong variation across subfields, fixed reasoning heuristics, uneven materials knowledge, and limited high-level knowledge application under formula-, retrieval-, and code-assisted settings.

Conclusion: OmniMatBench reveals critical limitations of current MLLMs in materials-science reasoning and establishes a foundation for developing reliable AI assistants in materials-science research.

Abstract: As multimodal language models play an increasingly important role in scientific research, materials science offers a critical testbed due to its interdisciplinary, multimodal, and application-driven nature. However, existing materials benchmarks mainly focus on property prediction, knowledge QA, or characterization understanding, leaving the broader reasoning process from materials knowledge to application underexplored. To fill this gap, we present OmniMatBench, a human-calibrated multimodal reasoning benchmark for materials science. OmniMatBench contains 3,171 expert-curated QA and calculation problems across 19 materials-science subfields, spanning fundamental materials knowledge, structural and engineering materials, materials processing and manufacturing, and functional and applied materials. We evaluate 13 open-source and closed-source MLLMs and find that the best model achieves only a 0.372 overall score, revealing a substantial gap in current materials-science reasoning. Further analysis shows strong variation across subfields, fixed reasoning heuristics, uneven materials knowledge, and limited high-level knowledge application under formula-, retrieval-, and code-assisted settings. OmniMatBench provides crucial insights into the capabilities and limitations of current MLLMs and establishes a foundation for reliable AI assistants in materials-science research.

</details>


### [98] [Moment-KV: Momentum-Based Decode-Time KV Cache Compression for Long Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29873)
*Soumyadeep Jana,Sagar Nishad,Sanasam Ranbir Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Moment-KV is a decoding-time KV cache compression method that uses momentum-driven temporal attention aggregation to capture both long-term and short-term token importance, improving generation fidelity by 2.3-3.2% in long-generation tasks without increasing decoding latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV cache compression methods apply uniform compression across prefill and decoding caches, but compressing prefill cache degrades performance. Decoding-phase compression remains underexplored, with rigid recency windows or instantaneous attention failing to capture the temporal dynamics of token importance.

Method: Moment-KV models token importance as a continuously evolving state using momentum-driven temporal attention aggregation with decay, capturing both long-term sustained attention and short-lived local reasoning bursts.

Result: Achieves 2.3-3.2% improvement in generation fidelity on long-generation tasks while maintaining decoding latency.

Conclusion: Preserving prefill cache while applying momentum-based temporal aggregation for decoding-phase compression is effective, outperforming static heuristics that lead to premature eviction or stale retention of tokens.

Abstract: Key-Value (KV) cache remains a major bottleneck for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-generation tasks. Prior work often applies uniform compression across both prefill and decoding caches, but compressing the prefill cache degrades performance by corrupting critical context. While preserving the prefill cache is essential, decoding-phase compression remains underexplored, with existing methods relying on rigid recency windows or instantaneous attention. Our analysis of attention dynamics reveals strong temporal patterns: critical tokens receive sustained attention over long horizons, while local reasoning involves short-lived bursts. Static heuristics fail to capture this behavior, leading to premature eviction of important tokens or retention of stale ones. We propose Moment-KV, a decoding-time KV cache compression method based on momentum-driven temporal attention aggregation. Our method models token importance as a continuously evolving state, where attention is aggregated with decay, capturing both long-term influence and recent relevance. Experiments show that Moment-KV significantly improves generation fidelity in long-generation tasks (2.3-3.2 %) while maintaining decoding latency.

</details>


### [99] [Redundant or Necessary? A Benchmark for Detecting Redundant Steps in Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29893)
*Minyang Hu,Bo Yang,Zhinuo Zhou,Jiachen Liang,Guo Jiahao,Yiyang Yin,Xiongwei Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RedundancyBench, a benchmark for detecting redundant steps in LLM agent trajectories, and finds that existing methods perform poorly (best achieving only 24.88% score), highlighting the need for further research in this new area.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation protocols for LLM-based agents primarily focus on task success and overlook execution efficiency, causing agent trajectories to contain redundant steps that waste resources while contributing little to task completion.

Method: The authors propose and formulate redundant step detection as a new research area, build RedundancyBench with diverse tasks and carefully annotated trajectories where each step is labeled by contribution, and evaluate 3 representative methods for detecting redundant vs. necessary steps.

Result: The best-performing method achieves only 24.88% score in detecting redundant steps, and some methods perform worse than random guessing.

Conclusion: Redundant step detection is a complex and challenging task that requires further research beyond current methods.

Abstract: LLM-based agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in solving complex tasks through multi-step reasoning and tool use. However, existing evaluation protocols primarily focus on task success, overlooking a critical aspect of agent behavior: execution efficiency. In practice, agent trajectories often contain redundant steps that consume substantial resources while contributing little to task completion. In this work, we propose and formulate a new research area: \textbf{redundant step detection} for agent trajectories. To support this initiative, we introduce \textbf{RedundancyBench}, a new benchmark that contains diverse tasks with carefully annotated trajectories, where each step is labeled according to its contribution to task completion. Using RedundancyBench, we develop and evaluate 3 representative methods to answer whether a step within trajectory is redundant or necessary. Our results show that even the best-performing method achieves only 24.88\% score in detecting redundant steps, while some methods perform worse than random guessing. These results highlight the task's complexity and the need for further research in this area. \footnote{Code and dataset in this paper are both available in \href{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RedundancyBench}{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/RedundancyBench}.}

</details>


### [100] [On the Geometry of Games and their Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29919)
*Yaqi Sun,Julian Ma,David Mguni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a structure-aware solver synthesis framework that learns a continuous, solver-aligned geometry of games, enabling adaptive equilibrium computation by mapping games to low-dimensional representations and selecting effective solver primitives, revealing structured regions of algorithmic validity and regime mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing equilibrium computation research is fragmented—solvers and game classes are studied in isolation, yielding strong local guarantees but no unified understanding. Discrete taxonomies provide incomplete accounts of where algorithms succeed, and classical theory offers limited insight into intermediate or overlapping solvability regimes.

Method: The framework consists of (1) a learned structure recogniser that maps each game to a low-dimensional solver-aligned representation, (2) a policy that maps this representation to effective primitive solver mechanisms, and (3) a bounded residual that acts as a local corrector and diagnostic signal for incomplete solver bases or representations.

Result: Fixed primitives exhibit systematic regime mismatch, while the learned representation organises game space into a structured cartography aligned with solver behaviour. Games with similar optimisation dynamics cluster together, revealing continuous regions of algorithmic validity and overlapping solver behaviour.

Conclusion: Equilibrium computation should be viewed as the joint problem of learning solver mechanisms and mapping the geometry of solvability, moving beyond isolated solver-game analyses toward a unified, adaptive framework.

Abstract: A central challenge in game theory and learning systems such as GANs is understanding which algorithms can efficiently compute equilibria across the heterogeneous landscape of games. Equilibrium computation is typically studied solver by solver and game class by game class, yielding strong local guarantees but a fragmented view of solver behaviour. Existing discrete taxonomies often provide an incomplete account of where algorithms succeed. We study this problem through a solver-game map linking games to effective solver dynamics. Classical theory identifies isolated regions of this map but provides limited insight into intermediate or overlapping regimes, suggesting that solvability is governed by latent structural properties defining a continuous solver-aligned geometry of games. We formalise this perspective through structure-aware solver synthesis. A learned structure recogniser maps each game to a low-dimensional solver-aligned representation, and a policy maps this representation to effective primitive mechanisms, adapting solver behaviour across regimes. This reveals regions where particular solver dynamics are effective and where mixtures of primitives are required rather than a single dominant solver. A bounded residual acts as a local corrector and diagnostic signal for incomplete solver bases or representations. The framework yields both an adaptive solver and an analytical lens: games with similar optimisation dynamics cluster together, revealing continuous regions of algorithmic validity and overlapping solver behaviour. Empirically, we show that fixed primitives exhibit systematic regime mismatch, while the learned representation organises game space into a structured cartography aligned with solver behaviour. These results suggest viewing equilibrium computation as the joint problem of learning solver mechanisms and mapping the geometry of solvability.

</details>


### [101] [Toward AI Systems That Understand Self and Others: A Multi-Phase Inference Framework for Human Cognitive Diversity and World-Model Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29930)
*Toru Takahashi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a Multi-Phase Inference Mechanism (MIM) that formalizes how heterogeneous world models arise from shared observations, reframing alignment as making different representations mutually processable rather than forcing consensus, with applications to philosophy, cognition, social fragmentation, and AI alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mutual misunderstanding persists even when people share observations, because different subjects form different inferential targets, state representations, prediction errors, and update priorities. Existing approaches fail to address this fundamental source of disagreement.

Method: The paper introduces the Multi-Phase Inference Mechanism (MIM), defined through four components: (1) a phase-formation space, (2) a foregrounding field, (3) subject-specific profile states, and (4) alignment maps between state representations. It formalizes how heterogeneous world models emerge from identical observations.

Result: A formal framework that reframes world-model alignment as the problem of making heterogeneous representations mutually processable, rather than forcing agreement or convergence to a single value system. The formalism connects to philosophical disagreements, cognitive typology, social fragmentation, and AI alignment.

Conclusion: The aim is to provide a constructive vocabulary for AI systems that help humans understand self and others by making differences in meaning, value, and prediction error visible, comparable, and transformable.

Abstract: Mutual misunderstanding in contemporary society does not arise merely because people hold different opinions or values. Even under the same observations, different subjects may form different inferential targets, state representations, prediction errors, and update priorities. This paper proposes a multi-phase inference framework and defines its core internal mechanism as the Multi-Phase Inference Mechanism (MIM). MIM formalizes how heterogeneous world models arise through a phase-formation space, a foregrounding field, subject-specific profile states, and alignment maps between state representations. On this basis, the paper reframes world-model alignment as the problem of making heterogeneous representations mutually processable, rather than forcing agreement or convergence to a single value system. It further connects this formalism to philosophical disagreements, cognitive typology, social fragmentation, and AI alignment. The aim is to provide a constructive vocabulary for AI systems that can help humans understand self and others by making differences in meaning, value, and prediction error visible, comparable, and transformable.

</details>


### [102] [It`s All About Speed: AI`s Impact on Workflow in Music Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29931)
*Finn McClellan,Fabio Morreale*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An ethnographic study examining how AI and automated tools impact music production workflows among recording engineers, mixers, and producers, revealing tensions around speed, control, and creative agency, with design recommendations to alleviate these tensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the real-world impact of AI and automation tools on professional music production workflows and identify tensions between users and these tools.

Method: Ethnographic study focusing on professional recording engineers, mixers, and producers, analyzing their usage of AI/automated software and their sentiments toward these tools.

Result: Identified key tensions in speed/efficiency, controllability, and creative agency between users and automated tools in music production.

Conclusion: These tensions can be alleviated through thoughtful tool design that balances automation with user control and creative agency.

Abstract: In this paper, we present the results of an ethnographic study into the impact of AI and automated tools on music production workflow. Focusing specifically on professional participants who identified as recording engineers, mixers, and producers, we discuss their usage of common AI and automated software, as well as their sentiments on the proliferation of these tools. We discuss tensions that may be created between users and automated tools in key areas such as the need for speed and efficiency, controllability, and maintaining creative agency, and how these tensions may be alleviated through tool design.

</details>


### [103] [Make LLM Learn to Synthesize from Streaming Experiences through Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29940)
*Zhenlin Hu,Yan Wang,Zhen Bi,Zihao Xue,Bingyu Zhu,Longtao Huang,Xiongtao Zhang,Zeyu Yang,Zhixuan Chu,Jungang Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StreamSynth introduces sequential task streams for synthetic data generation, and SynLearner enables models to accumulate and transfer synthesis experience across tasks, improving performance on future tasks through exploration, feedback, and diversity balancing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches treat synthetic data generation as isolated tasks, overlooking whether models can learn to synthesize by accumulating and transferring experience from past tasks to future ones.

Method: SynLearner is a general framework that enables synthesis models to acquire reusable experience over a task stream by exploring diverse synthesis patterns, learning from feedback, and balancing sample quality with set-level diversity as tasks evolve.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show SynLearner effectively leverages experience from earlier tasks to improve synthesis performance on later ones, demonstrating consistent cross-task transferability.

Conclusion: Synthetic data generation can be framed as an experience-driven process that benefits from task streams, providing evidence for the feasibility of StreamSynth.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been widely adopted for synthetic data generation, significantly reducing annotation costs. However, most existing studies treat synthesis as a set of isolated tasks and overlook a more fundamental question: whether a model can learn to synthesize by accumulating experience from past tasks and transferring it to future ones. In this work, we introduce StreamSynth, a new setting in which synthesis tasks arrive sequentially and experience from historical tasks provides informative signals for future synthesis. To address this setting, we propose SynLearner, a general framework that enables synthesis models to acquire reusable synthesis experience over a task stream. Instead of generating data independently for each task, SynLearner encourages the model to explore diverse synthesis patterns, learn from feedback, and balance sample quality with set-level diversity as tasks evolve. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks show that SynLearner effectively leverages experience from earlier tasks to improve synthesis performance on later ones, exhibiting consistent cross-task transferability. These findings provide evidence for the feasibility of StreamSynth and highlight synthetic data generation as an experience-driven process that can benefit from task streams.

</details>


### [104] [MuPHI: Learning Implicit Multimodal Harm Reasoning via Semantically Grounded Reward Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29951)
*Anisha Saha,Varsha Suresh,Teodora Kamova,Sophia Wiedmann,Timothy Hospedales,Vera Demberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MuPHI is a dataset for multimodal pragmatic harm interpretation, and MuPHIRM is a reasoning-augmented training framework that uses multi-perspective reward optimization to improve VLMs' harm detection and reasoning, showing superior OOD robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLMs excel at literal reasoning over perceptual cues but fail to derive harmful semantics that rely on implicit, context-dependent reasoning from otherwise benign image-text pairs.

Method: MuPHIRM: a reasoning-augmented training framework that learns joint semantics by optimizing multi-perspective rewards.

Result: MuPHIRM improves both harm detection and reasoning quality of VLMs while demonstrating superior out-of-distribution robustness compared to both trained and inference-time baselines.

Conclusion: Reasoning-oriented reward optimization offers a promising direction towards building multimodal systems that generalize beyond benchmark-specific shortcuts.

Abstract: Understanding how harm emerges from interaction between otherwise benign image-text pairs requires intent-aware cross-modal reasoning beyond surface-level features. Existing vision-language models (VLMs) excel at literal reasoning over perceptual cues but often fail to derive harmful semantics that rely on implicit, context-dependent reasoning. To evaluate VLMs on compositional harm detection and reasoning, we introduce Multimodal Pragmatic Harm Interpretation (MuPHI), a dataset containing image-text pairs where harm is encoded in subtle multimodal cues. MuPHI spans diverse harm categories and includes annotated harm rationales for assessing VLM reasoning chains. To improve both detection and reasoning in VLMs, we propose MuPHIRM, a reasoning-augmented training framework which learns joint semantics by optimizing multi-perspective rewards. MuPHIRM improves both harm detection and reasoning quality of VLMs while demonstrating superior out-of-distribution robustness compared to both trained and inference-time baselines. Our findings suggest that reasoning-oriented reward optimization offers a promising direction towards building multimodal systems that generalize beyond benchmark-specific shortcuts.

</details>


### [105] [Formalizing Mathematics at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29955)
*Ahmad Rammal,Niket Patel,Fabian Gloeckle,Amaury Hayat,Julia Kempe,Remi Munos,Charles Arnal,Vivien Cabannes*

Main category: cs.AI
