TL;DR: AutoformBot is a multi-agent system that uses thousands of LLM agents to autoformalize 26 open-access textbooks into Lean 4, producing Atlas — a verified library of over 45,000 declarations and 500k lines of code — demonstrating the economic and technical feasibility of large-scale autoformalization of graduate-level mathematics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the scalability bottleneck of manually translating informal mathematical prose into machine-verified formal proofs, enabling automated verification of human- and machine-generated mathematics at research scale.

Method: A multi-agent LLM system (AutoformBot) that orchestrates thousands of agents with formal verification tools, dependency-aware task scheduling, and collaborative version control to translate textbook content into Lean 4 code.

Result: Produced Atlas: a verified library of over 45,000 Lean 4 declarations and 500k lines of code from 26 open-access textbooks across analysis, algebra, topology, combinatorics, and probability.

Conclusion: Autoformalizing graduate-level mathematics at scale is now economically and technically feasible, opening the door to automated verification of research-level mathematics.

Abstract: We present AutoformBot, a multi-agent system for building an Autoformalized Textbook Library At Scale (Atlas) in Lean 4. AutoformBot orchestrates thousands of LLM agents, equipped with formal verification tools, dependency-aware task scheduling, and collaborative version control, to translate informal textbook prose into machine-checked definitions and proofs. We apply our methods to a corpus of 26 open-access textbooks spanning analysis, algebra, topology, combinatorics, and probability, producing Atlas: a verified library of over 45,000 Lean 4 declarations and 500 thousand lines of code. We release two artifacts: (i) AutoformBot, the open-source multi-agent framework; and (ii) Atlas, the resulting formal library. Our results suggest that autoformalizing the core content of graduate-level mathematics at scale is now economically and technically feasible. This opens the door to the automated verification of both human- and machine-generated mathematics at a research level.

</details>


### [106] [Meta-Programming for Linear-time Temporal Answer Set Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29965)
*Susana Hahn,Amade Nems,Javier Romero,Torsten Schaub*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a flexible meta-programming framework that extends ASP meta-programming with type specifications and nesting capabilities, enabling unified operationalization of temporal equilibrium logics (TEL, MEL, DEL) and introduces the metasp system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rigidity of highly optimized ASP systems hinders rapid exploration and implementation of alternative logical designs for temporal extensions of ASP.

Method: Extends standard ASP meta-programming by augmenting clingo's theory grammar with formal type specifications and nesting capabilities, with a transformation pipeline that protects nested modalities from stable-model-based simplifications during grounding.

Result: Implemented meta-encodings for TEL, MEL, and DEL; provided comprehensive account of TEL and key features for managing interval constraints of MEL and Fischer-Ladner closure in DEL; introduced the metasp system.

Conclusion: The proposed meta-programming framework enables flexible and rapid exploration of varied temporal logic designs within ASP, demonstrated through implementations of multiple temporal equilibrium logics in a unified declarative manner.

Abstract: The development of temporal extensions of Answer Set Programming (ASP) has led to the emergence of non-monotonic linear-time (TEL), dynamic (DEL), and metric (MEL) temporal equilibrium logics. However, the inherent rigidity of highly optimized ASP systems often hinders the rapid exploration and implementation of alternative logical designs. In this work, we propose a flexible meta-programming framework that operationalizes the semantics of varied temporal logics through a unified, declarative framework. Our approach extends standard ASP meta-programming by augmenting clingo's theory grammar with formal type specifications and nesting capabilities. To ensure semantic correctness, we introduce a transformation pipeline that protects nested modalities from stable-model-based simplifications during grounding. We demonstrate the extensibility of our framework by implementing meta-encodings for TEL, MEL, and DEL. We provide a comprehensive account of TEL and highlight the key features for managing the interval constraints of MEL and the Fischer-Ladner closure in DEL. Finally, we introduce the metasp system, a versatile tool that encapsulates this workflow.

</details>


### [107] [Compass: Navigating Global Marine Lead Data Integration through Expert-Guided LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29966)
*Yiming Liu,Bin Lu,Meng Jin,Ziyuan Sang,Shuo Jiang,Lei Zhou,Xinbing Wang,Chenghu Zhou,Jing Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents Compass, an expert-guided LLM agent framework that uses a Knowledge Tree to extract marine lead isotope data from unstructured scientific literature, creating the largest integrated marine Pb database (3,751 records) with 92% accuracy from 230,000+ papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Marine Pb and isotopes are critical tracers for ocean circulation and pollution research, but in-situ observations are costly and sparse. Vast historical data remain trapped in unstructured academic papers ("data silos"), and manual extraction is unscalable. General-purpose LLMs lack domain-specific knowledge, causing hallucinations and invalid outputs.

Method: An expert-guided adaptation approach using Compass, an LLM agent framework enhanced by a Knowledge Tree co-designed with marine scientists. The Knowledge Tree decomposes complex extraction tasks into verifiable steps to guide agent reasoning and ensure scientific validity, without requiring model fine-tuning.

Result: Applied to 230,000+ open-access papers, successfully extracted 3,751 previously unincorporated Pb records, establishing the largest integrated marine Pb database. Achieved 92% accuracy confirmed via expert manual verification, expanding coverage in under-sampled regions (East China Sea, Southern Ocean).

Conclusion: Expert-guided agents can effectively bridge the gap between general-purpose LLMs and high-stakes scientific domains, enabling scalable data discovery in geosciences. An interactive visualization platform was released to facilitate open scientific access.

Abstract: Marine lead (Pb) and its isotopes are critical tracers for ocean circulation and anthropogenic pollution, yet in-situ observations remain costly and sparse. While vast historical records exist, they lie buried within the unstructured content of academic papers, creating "data silos" inaccessible to comprehensive analysis. Manual extraction is unscalable, while general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) lack the necessary domain-specific knowledge, leading to hallucinations and scientifically invalid outputs. To address this, we introduce an expert-guided adaptation approach that enables LLMs to perform rigorous scientific data extraction without fine-tuning. We operationalize this approach through Compass, an LLM agent framework enhanced by a Knowledge Tree co-designed with marine scientists, which decomposes complex tasks into verifiable steps, guiding the agent's reasoning to ensure scientific validity. Deploying Compass across a corpus of over 230,000 relevant open-access papers, we successfully extract 3,751 previously unincorporated Pb records. This effort establishes the largest integrated marine Pb database to date. Beyond standard metrics, Compass demonstrates superior reliability through multi-layered validation, achieving 92% accuracy as confirmed through expert manual verification. The newly integrated data expand coverage in previously under-sampled regions such as the East China Sea and the Southern Ocean, providing an enriched data foundation for future scientific discoveries. We release an interactive visualization platform to facilitate open scientific access. Our work demonstrates that expert-guided agents can effectively bridge the gap between general-purpose LLMs and high-stakes scientific domains, enabling scalable data discovery in geosciences.

</details>


### [108] [Accelerating Constrained Decoding with Token Space Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29986)
*Michael Sullivan,Alexander Koller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CFGzip is an offline compression technique that reduces the token search space in CFG-constrained LLM decoding, achieving up to 7.5x speedup in total constrained generation time and reducing latency by up to two orders of magnitude for complex CFGs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current CFG-constrained decoding engines suffer from high computational overhead due to the massive per-step search space (the entire token vocabulary), especially for complex CFGs where these engines are most needed, limiting their scalability and practical applicability.

Method: CFGzip is an offline compression technique that reduces the token search space during CFG-constrained decoding, thereby massively cutting down the overhead incurred by grammar engines at each generation step.

Result: Latency reduction of up to two orders of magnitude when combined with a state-of-the-art grammar engine, yielding up to 7.5x speedup in total constrained generation time.

Conclusion: With CFGzip, constrained decoding becomes feasible at scale for complex CFGs, addressing a key bottleneck in structured LLM output generation.

Abstract: To guarantee that an LLM's outputs conform to a specified structure, context-free grammar (CFG) decoding engines force the selection of next tokens that produce strings that conform to a given CFG. While current CFG-constrained decoding engines are highly optimized, the inherent costs arising from the massive per-step search space -- i.e. the entire token vocabulary -- result in intractably high overhead for more complex CFGs: precisely the situation where CFG engines are most useful. In this paper, we introduce CFGzip, an offline technique for compressing the token search space, which massively reduces CFG engine overhead. In experiments, we report latency reduction of up to two orders of magnitude when CFGzip is used with a SoTA grammar engine, yielding an up to 7.5x speedup in total constrained generation time: with CFGzip, constrained decoding is now feasible at scale for complex CFGs.

</details>


### [109] [Cookie-Bench: Continuous On-screen Key Interaction Evaluation for Web Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30000)
*Haoyue Yang,Zhangxiao Shen,Fan Ding,Hangting Lou,Yifeng Kou,Haoqing Yu,Jingyao Li,Zhengfan Wu,Siqi Bao,Jing Liu,Hua Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a reference-free, autonomous evaluation framework (named \framename) for front-end web code generation, supported by a new benchmark (\dataname) spanning 1,000 queries across 11 domains. The framework mimics human review through a three-stage metacognitive process, aligning closely with expert ratings while revealing performance gaps across 13 frontier LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human-judged leaderboards like Arena are costly and do not scale for evaluating interactive front-end web applications. Existing automated proxies rely on reference implementations, test suites, or rigid checklists, failing to capture the reasoned synthesis a human reviewer performs over a live session.

Method: The paper proposes a new evaluation regime that is reference-free, autonomously driven, and holistically reasoned, instantiated via two artifacts: (1) \dataname, a 1,000-query benchmark across 11 domains with 54 leaf tasks, balanced across three difficulty tiers and three target-language groups; (2) \framename, a metacognitive monitoring framework (grounded in Flavell's theory) with three stages: Static Perception (passive first impression), Agent-Driven Interaction (autonomous exploration with continuous video/audio/screenshot capture), and Dynamic Scoring (holistic functionality and aesthetics verdicts with structured failure attribution).

Result: On \dataname, \framename aligns closely with expert human ratings while revealing substantial performance headroom across 13 frontier LLMs on interactive web generation tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed reference-free, metacognitive evaluation framework effectively approximates human judgment for front-end web code generation, and current LLMs still have significant room for improvement on interactive web development tasks.

Abstract: Front-end web code has become a core product surface for every frontier LLM release, yet evaluating these interactive applications at development speed remains costly because human-judged leaderboards like Arena do not scale. Existing automated proxies typically lean on reference implementations, test suites, or rigid checklists, and tend to miss the reasoned synthesis a human reviewer performs over a live session. We articulate a new evaluation regime that is simultaneously reference-free, autonomously driven, and holistically reasoned, and instantiate it through two artifacts. \textbf{\dataname} is an 11-domain, 54-leaf, 1,000-query WebDev benchmark spanning both static-presentation and interactive-application tasks, balanced across three difficulty tiers and three target-language groups, with briefs rewritten to resist recall from circulated prompts. \textbf{\framename}, grounded in Flavell's metacognitive monitoring, separates evidence accumulation from judgment across three stages: Static Perception forms a first impression from passive observation; Agent-Driven Interaction explores the application autonomously while capturing continuous screen video, audio, and per-step screenshots; Dynamic Scoring issues holistic functionality and aesthetics verdicts with structured failure attribution only after the evidence chain is complete. On \dataname, \framename aligns closely with expert human ratings while surfacing substantial headroom across 13 frontier LLMs on interactive web generation. \noindenthttps://anonymous.4open.science/r/Cookie-3CE/

</details>


### [110] [KairosAgent: Agentic Time Series Forecasting with Fused Semantic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30002)
*Kun Feng,Ziwei Shan,Yuchen Fang,Yiyang Tan,Sihan Lu,Shuqi Gu,Lintao Ma,Xingyu Lu,Kan Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KairosAgent is a novel agentic framework that combines LLM-based reasoning with TSFM-based forecasting for cross-domain multimodal time series forecasting, using dynamic tool invocation, a large-scale trajectory corpus, and reinforcement learning to achieve superior zero-shot performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TSFMs lack semantic understanding and future-oriented reasoning, while LLMs struggle with numerical comprehension and accurate quantitative forecasting, creating a gap in cross-domain multimodal time series forecasting.

Method: KairosAgent integrates an LLM-based reasoner and a TSFM-based forecaster, dynamically invoking analytical tools for enhanced numerical understanding and semantic reasoning. It uses a large-scale trajectory corpus and reinforcement learning from forecasting with multi-turn refinement and turn-level credit assignment.

Result: Superior zero-shot forecasting performance with efficient utilization of pretrained LLMs and TSFMs.

Conclusion: KairosAgent presents a promising direction for efficient and interpretable time series agents by unifying textual reasoning and numerical forecasting.

Abstract: Cross-domain multimodal time series forecasting is a challenging task, requiring models to integrate precise numerical comprehension, cross-domain semantic understanding, and effective multimodal fusion. Existing approaches either build Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) from scratch or leverage pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs). However, TSFMs often overlook semantic understanding and lack the ability to perform future-oriented semantic reasoning, and LLMs struggle with numerical comprehension and accurate quantitative forecasting. To overcome these limitations, we propose KairosAgent, a novel agentic framework for multimodal time series forecasting, including an LLM-based reasoner and a TSFM-based forecaster. KairosAgent unifies textual reasoning and numerical forecasting by dynamically invoking analytical tools to enhance the numerical understanding and semantic reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The reasoning results are subsequently fused into the TSFM pipeline, enabling more accurate and reliable future predictions. To further improve the reasoning, we curate a large-scale corpus of high-quality trajectories, alongside a reinforcement learning from forecasting paradigm with multi-turn refinement and turn-level credit assignment. Experiments demonstrate that KairosAgent achieves superior zero-shot forecasting performance while maximizing the utility of pretrained LLMs and TSFMs, presenting a promising direction for efficient and interpretable time series agents. The project page is at https://foundation-model-research.github.io/KairosAgent .

</details>


### [111] [From GPS Points to Travel Patterns: Flexible and Semantic Trajectory Generation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30014)
*Silin Zhou,Chenhao Wang,Yuntao Wen,Shuo Shang,Lisi Chen,Panos Kalnis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HTP is a hierarchical trajectory generation framework that first quantizes GPS points into travel pattern tokens using a trajectory-specific RQ-VAE, then fine-tunes an LLM to generate these tokens under various conditions, outperforming baselines by ~30% in generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privacy concerns restrict access to large-scale, high-quality trajectory data for urban modeling; existing trajectory generation methods fail to explicitly capture travel patterns and can only produce fixed-length trajectories under a single condition.

Method: Proposes HTP with two stages: (1) a trajectory-specific residual quantization VAE (RQ-VAE) that quantizes micro-level GPS trajectories into coarse-to-fine macro-level travel pattern tokens capturing spatial irregularities (e.g., point density variations); (2) extending LLM vocabulary with these tokens and applying supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to align the LLM for generating travel pattern sequences under diverse conditions.

Result: On two real-world datasets, HTP outperforms the strongest baseline by an average of 29.78% in generation quality.

Conclusion: Hierarchically generating travel patterns first then GPS points via LLMs significantly improves trajectory generation quality and flexibility over direct GPS point generation methods.

Abstract: Urban trajectories play a crucial role in modeling urban dynamics and supporting various smart city applications. However, privacy concerns restrict access to large-scale and high-quality trajectory datasets. Trajectory generation provides a promising alternative by synthesizing realistic data to mitigate privacy risks. However, existing methods fail to explicitly capture travel patterns and can only generate fixed-length trajectories under a single condition. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{HTP}, which \textbf{H}ierarchically generates \textbf{T}ravel patterns first and then generates GPS \textbf{P}oints by using large language models (LLMs), rather than directly generating GPS points. We first design a trajectory-specific residual quantization variational autoencoder (RQ-VAE) that quantizes micro-level GPS trajectories into compact, macro-level travel pattern tokens in a coarse-to-fine manner. These tokens capture rich segment spatial irregularities, such as point density variations caused by traffic conditions. Then, we extend the LLM vocabulary with travel pattern tokens to align trajectory representations with the LLM input, and apply supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to align the LLM with the trajectory generation task, enabling generation of travel pattern sequences under various conditions. Extensive experiments on two real-world datasets show that HTP outperforms the strongest baseline by an average of 29.78\% in terms of generation quality. Our code is available at https://github.com/slzhou-xy/HTP.

</details>


### [112] [RAISE: RAG Design as an Architecture Search Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30029)
*Zhen Chen,Yibing Liu,Weihao Xie,Yu Liang,Peilin Chen,Shiqi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces RAISE, a benchmark and framework for systematically optimizing RAG pipeline hyperparameters (query rewriting, chunking, retrieval depth, reranking, context compression) using 13 search algorithms across 7 text and multimodal datasets, finding that optimization performance is highly task-dependent with no universally superior strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems involve numerous design choices (query rewriting, chunking, retrieval depth, reranking, context compression) that are currently configured through heuristics, hindering systematic evaluation and reproducibility. The paper argues this should be formulated as a RAG architecture search problem.

Method: The authors propose RAISE (RAG Intelligence Search Engine), a comprehensive framework and benchmark for RAG hyperparameter optimization. It implements 13 search algorithms and evaluates them across 7 public datasets (text and multimodal) using 3 random seeds, under standardized search spaces and budgets.

Result: Optimization performance is highly task-dependent; methods performing strongly on one dataset may not generalize consistently across others. Aggregate rankings should not be interpreted as evidence of universally superior strategies.

Conclusion: RAISE provides a common experimental substrate for fair, reproducible, and systematic research on RAG hyperparameter optimization, cautioning against overinterpreting aggregate rankings as proof of generalizable superiority.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems expose numerous design choices spanning query rewriting, chunking, retrieval depth, reranking, and context compression. In practice, these choices are often configured through heuristics, hindering systematic evaluation and reproducibility across settings. We argue that this challenge is best formulated as RAG architecture search. To support controlled and reproducible study of this problem, we introduce the RAG Intelligence Search Engine (RAISE), a comprehensive framework and benchmark for RAG hyperparameter optimization, which evaluates optimization methods for RAG pipelines under standardized search spaces and budgets. RAISE implements 13 search algorithms and evaluates them across seven public text and multimodal datasets using three random seeds. Our experiments show that optimization performance is highly task-dependent: methods that perform strongly on one dataset may not generalize consistently across others, cautioning against interpreting aggregate rankings as evidence of universally superior strategies. RAISE provides a common experimental substrate for fair, reproducible, and systematic research on RAG hyperparameter optimization.

</details>


### [113] [Teaching Values to Machines: Simulating Human-Like Behavior in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30036)
*Asaf Yehudai,Naama Rozen,Ariel Gera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs prompted with human psychological value structures show strong alignment with human value systems and value-behavior relationships across large-scale experiments (5M+ questions), and incorporating human value distributions improves population-level simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether LLMs can manifest coherent, human-like value structures by drawing on established psychological value theory, beyond simply adopting personas.

Method: Used validated psychological questionnaires to conduct large-scale experiments (5M+ questions) evaluating value structures and value-behavior relationships in leading LLMs, comparing them to humans using value-prompting techniques.

Result: Strong agreement between value-prompted LLMs and humans across both value structure and value-behavior relationship dimensions; incorporating human value distributions improves population-level simulations.

Conclusion: Value-induced LLMs can serve as effective, psychologically grounded tools for simulating human behavior.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate a remarkable capacity to adopt different personas and roles; however, it remains unclear whether they can manifest behavior that adheres to a coherent, human-like value structure. In this work, we draw on established psychological value theory to induce human-like values in LLMs and assess their alignment with patterns observed in human studies. Using validated psychological questionnaires, we conduct large-scale experiments -- over 5 million questions -- to evaluate value structures and value-behavior relationships in leading LLMs and compare them to humans. Our findings reveal strong agreement between value-prompted LLMs and humans across both dimensions. Moreover, incorporating human value distributions enhances population-level simulations with value-induced LLMs. These findings highlight the potential of value-induced LLMs as effective, psychologically grounded tools for simulating human behavior.

</details>


### [114] [Domain-Specific Data Synthesis for LLMs via Minimal Sufficient Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30039)
*Tong Ye,Hang Yu,Tengfei Ma,Xuhong Zhang,Jianguo Li,Peng Di,Peiyu Liu,Jianwei Yin,Wenhai Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DOMINO learns domain representations from reference examples (not natural language descriptions) to synthesize training data, improving domain-specific fine-tuning by up to 4.63% Pass@1 on coding benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acquiring high-quality domain-specific data is challenging; existing data synthesis relies on explicit natural language domain descriptions and careful prompt engineering (deductive paradigm), which fails when domains are hard to articulate. The authors tackle the underexplored inductive paradigm where only reference examples define the domain.

Method: DOMINO learns a minimal sufficient domain representation from reference samples via prompt tuning with a contrastive disentanglement objective that separates domain-level patterns from sample-specific noise, then uses that representation to guide synthetic data generation.

Result: On coding benchmarks with implicit domain definitions, DOMINO improves Pass@1 accuracy by up to 4.63% over strong instruction-tuned backbones, providing theoretical proof that it expands synthetic data diversity.

Conclusion: DOMINO establishes a new inductive paradigm for domain-specific data synthesis, enabling scalable domain adaptation without manual prompt design or natural language domain specifications.

Abstract: Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable progress in general-purpose capabilities and can achieve strong performance in specific domains through fine-tuning on domain-specific data. However, acquiring high-quality data for target domains remains a significant challenge. Existing data synthesis approaches follow a deductive paradigm, heavily relying on explicit domain descriptions expressed in natural language and careful prompt engineering, limiting their applicability in real-world scenarios where domains are difficult to describe or formally articulate. In this work, we tackle the underexplored problem of domain-specific data synthesis through an inductive paradigm, where the target domain is defined only through a set of reference examples, particularly when domain characteristics are difficult to articulate in natural language. We propose a novel framework, DOMINO, that learns a minimal sufficient domain representation from reference samples and leverages it to guide the generation of domain-aligned synthetic data. DOMINO integrates prompt tuning with a contrastive disentanglement objective to separate domain-level patterns from sample-specific noise, mitigating overfitting while preserving core domain characteristics. Theoretically, we prove that DOMINO expands the support of the synthetic data distribution, ensuring greater diversity. Empirically, on challenging coding benchmarks where domain definitions are implicit, fine-tuning on data synthesized by DOMINO improves Pass@1 accuracy by up to 4.63\% over strong, instruction-tuned backbones, demonstrating its effectiveness and robustness. This work establishes a new paradigm for domain-specific data synthesis, enabling practical and scalable domain adaptation without manual prompt design or natural language domain specifications.

</details>


### [115] [Learning to Choose: An Empowerment-Guided Multi-Agent System with semantic communication for Adaptive Method Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30042)
*Geremy Loachamín-Suntaxi,Robert Lazar,Dimitrios G. Giovanis,Ioannis G. Kevrekidis,Eleni D. Koronaki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework combining contextual bandits, structured inter-agent communication, and semantic checkpoints to preserve action-outcome fidelity and prevent semantic drift in scientific computing workflows, demonstrating improved convergence, robustness, and adaptation over systems without such consistency guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous scientific computing workflows require not only executable code generation but also faithful propagation of decisions across multi-agent pipelines, where small inconsistencies cause semantic drift that corrupts downstream evaluation and adaptation.

Method: A multi-agent framework integrating contextual bandits, structured inter-agent communication, semantic checkpoints for action-outcome fidelity, specialized LLM agents, grounded code generation, and self-healing execution loops, interpreted through the lens of empowerment.

Result: Unchecked semantic drift degrades policy learning; the proposed framework improves convergence, robustness, and adaptation to novel problem contexts in sensitivity analysis and uncertainty quantification workflows.

Conclusion: Adaptive decision-making in scientific multi-agent systems must be coupled with explicit mechanisms guaranteeing semantic consistency and reliable information flow across the computational pipeline.

Abstract: Automating scientific computing workflows requires more than generating executable code: autonomous systems must also select appropriate computational strategies, implement them faithfully, and ensure that the resulting outcomes remain causally attributable to the decisions that produced them. In multi-agent pipelines, this process is particularly fragile, as small inconsistencies between agent intentions and actions can lead to semantic drift, where the eventually executed procedure no longer reflects the originally selected strategy, thereby corrupting downstream evaluation and adaptation. In this work, motivated by the ATHENA framework (Toscano et al., 2025; Toscano et al., 2026) and the concept of empowerment (Yiu et al., 2025), we introduce a multi-agent framework that combines contextual bandits with structured inter-agent communication and, most importantly, semantic checkpoints that preserve action-outcome fidelity throughout the pipeline. The system integrates specialized large language model (LLM) agents, grounded code generation, and self-healing execution loops within an adaptive decision-making architecture. Interpreting the framework through the lens of empowerment, we show that reliable autonomous learning requires not only identifying high-quality actions, but also preserving the integrity of their propagation across agents. Using sensitivity analysis and uncertainty quantification workflows as representative case studies, we demonstrate that unchecked semantic drift degrades policy learning, whereas the proposed framework improves convergence, robustness, and adaptation to novel problem contexts. These results suggest a broader design principle for scientific multi-agent systems: adaptive decision-making must be coupled with explicit mechanisms that guarantee semantic consistency and reliable information flow across the computational pipeline.

</details>


### [116] [Robust and Generalizable Safety Steering for Text-to-Image Diffusion Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30049)
*Zihao Xue,Yan Wang,Zhen Bi,Long Ma,Zhonglong Zheng,Zeyu Yang,Bingyu Zhu,Longtao Huang,Jie Xiao,Jungang Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeDIG is a safety steering framework for Diffusion Transformers that uses position-aware sparse feature transfer via Sparse Autoencoders, robustness-aware routing, and decoder adaptation to transfer safety knowledge across risk domains, achieving reduced unsafe generation rates on FLUX.1 Dev and Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Transformers have layered, cross-modal generation processes where harmful semantics can be weakly expressed in text, bound to visual latents, and entangled with rendering dynamics. Existing prompt-level filtering or output-level detection are insufficient, and fixed-layer safety steering is unstable under target risk domain shifts.

Method: SafeDIG (1) constructs Sparse Autoencoders over functionally distinct DiT intervention positions, (2) uses robustness-aware pre-training routing to prioritize stable intervention sites under risk shift, (3) freezes the SAE encoder as a reusable sparse safety dictionary and adapts only the decoder to the target-domain activation manifold, (4) applies Blend and Repel operations during inference to steer unsafe activations.

Result: Experiments on FLUX.1 Dev and Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large show consistent reduction in target-domain and overall unsafe generation rates while preserving source-domain safety and image quality.

Conclusion: SafeDIG effectively enables transferable safety steering for DiTs across risk domains, addressing the instability and domain-shift limitations of prior safety approaches.

Abstract: Diffusion Transformers have become a powerful backbone for text-to-image generation, but their layered and cross-modal generation process makes safety control fundamentally different from prompt-level filtering or output-level detection. Harmful semantics may be weakly expressed in text representations, progressively bound to visual latents, and finally entangled with rendering dynamics. As a result, safety steering at a fixed layer can be unstable, and a steering mechanism learned from known risks may not transfer reliably to a shifted target risk domain. We propose SafeDIG, a safety steering framework that formulates DiT safety adaptation as position-aware sparse feature transfer. SafeDIG first constructs Sparse Autoencoders over functionally distinct DiT intervention positions and uses robustness-aware pre-training routing to prioritize intervention sites that are expected to remain stable under source-target risk shift. It then separates transferable safety features from domain-specific activation geometry by freezing the SAE encoder as a reusable sparse safety dictionary and adapting only the decoder to the target-domain activation manifold. During inference, SafeDIG combines Blend and Repel operations to steer unsafe activations toward transferred safety manifolds or away from harmful sparse directions. Experiments on FLUX.1 Dev and Stable Diffusion 3.5 Large show that SafeDIG consistently reduces target-domain and overall unsafe generation rates while preserving source-domain safety and image quality.

</details>


### [117] [Conformal Certification of Reasoning Trace Prefixes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30085)
*Matt Y. Cheung,Ashok Veeraraghavan,Hanjie Chen,Guha Balakrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CROP is a conformal prediction method that certifies the longest safe prefix of a reasoning trace by controlling the probability of containing errors, using step-level risk proxies and a calibrated threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing uncertainty quantification methods only certify final answers or entire traces, failing to provide statistical guarantees for the proportion of a sequential trace that can be safely retained, despite reasoning traces often containing valid intermediate steps before a critical error.

Method: CROP selects a calibrated threshold from step-level risk proxies under exchangeability assumptions, returning the longest contiguous prefix where risk proxies remain below the threshold, routing the uncertified suffix for review or repair.

Result: Across six process-labeled reasoning datasets, standard step-level metrics like AUROC do not fully capture prefix utility; CROP balances over- and under-withholding, improving downstream repair accuracy by preserving valid reasoning while discarding misleading suffixes.

Conclusion: Prefix certification serves as a rigorous, practical bridge between process supervision, abstention, and repair, and verifiers should be evaluated by certified prefix length rather than standard metrics.

Abstract: Language model reasoning traces are rarely all-or-nothing; they frequently contain valid intermediate steps before a critical error occurs. Existing uncertainty quantification methods typically certify final answers or entire responses, failing to provide statistical guarantees for the proportion of a sequential trace that can be safely retained. To address this, we introduce CROP (Conformal Reasoning Output Prefixes), a verifier-agnostic calibration procedure for clean-prefix certification. Given any step-level risk proxy, CROP selects a calibrated threshold and returns the longest contiguous prefix whose step risk proxies remain below it, routing the uncertified suffix for downstream review or repair. Assuming exchangeability, CROP rigorously controls the marginal probability that the returned prefix contains an annotated error. Across six process-labeled reasoning datasets, we demonstrate that standard step-level metrics such as AUROC do not fully capture prefix utility, suggesting verifiers should instead be evaluated by certified prefix length. Furthermore, CROP balances over- and under-withholding, improving downstream repair accuracy by preserving valid intermediate reasoning while discarding misleading suffixes. Ultimately, this work positions prefix certification as a rigorous, practical bridge between process supervision, abstention, and repair.

</details>


### [118] [Selective QA over Conflicting Multi-Source Personal Memory: A Diagnostic Testbed and Method Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30087)
*Tiancheng Yang,Matthias Schonlau,Ilia Sucholutsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating personal AI agents on selective QA over conflicting, multi-source memory, with 34,560 instances across 18 templates and 8 reasoning types. The best trained fusion resolver achieves 80.3% accuracy (85.3% selective accuracy with abstention), while frontier LLMs reach 70.0% (71.0% selective). The benchmark controls for whether errors stem from evidence or conflict resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks do not disentangle whether errors in personal AI agents arise from incomplete/conflicting evidence or from the method's conflict-resolution step. As agents move toward persistent, multi-source memory, evaluating their ability to handle conflicting and incomplete information becomes critical.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark with 18 question templates across 8 reasoning types, 480 personas, 4 random seeds, and 34,560 instances, featuring controlled source distortions and deterministic ground truth. They evaluate baselines including no-source, single-source, structured fusion methods, and frontier LLMs.

Result: Best trained fusion resolver: 80.3% accuracy (85.3% selective with abstention at 78.3% coverage). Best prompt-only LLM: 70.0% accuracy (71.0% selective at 95.4% coverage). Different models show varying strengths across reasoning types.

Conclusion: The benchmark reveals a gap between trained fusion resolvers and LLMs on conflicting multi-source QA; abstention improves selective accuracy but varies in coverage across methods. The authors release all data, code, and cached outputs for reuse.

Abstract: Emerging personal AI agents are moving toward persistent, multi-source memory. This creates an evaluation problem: systems must decide how to use conflicting or incomplete evidence; they cannot just retrieve facts from one clean history. Existing benchmarks rarely show whether an error came from the evidence given to a method or from the method's conflict-resolution step. We study this as selective QA over conflicting multi-source personal memory: systems answer based on conflicting, sometimes incomplete sources, or abstain when evidence is insufficient. We develop a benchmark containing 18 question templates across 8 reasoning types, 480 personas, 4 random seeds, and 34,560 instances, with controlled source distortions and deterministic ground truth. We evaluate the performance of baselines without access to any source, access to a single source, structured fusion methods, and frontier LLMs. The best trained fusion resolver reaches 80.3% accuracy, while the strongest prompt-only LLM baseline reaches 70.0%. With abstention, the same resolver reaches 85.3% selective accuracy at 78.3% coverage and the best LLM reaches 71.0% selective accuracy at 95.4% coverage. Different models have different strengths across reasoning types. We release the data, code, cached model outputs, and data-generating process for reuse.

</details>


### [119] [PokerSkill: LLMs Can Play Expert-Level Poker without Training or Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30094)
*Boning Li,Baoxiang Wang,Longbo Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PokerSkill is a training-free, solver-free framework that combines rule-based poker skills with LLMs to achieve competitive performance in Texas Hold'em, reducing losses by 49-61% against GTO benchmarks compared to default LLM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between LLMs (which possess poker knowledge but perform poorly when playing directly) and solver-based agents (which require millions of core-hours of training), by creating an interpretable, training-free approach that achieves competitive performance in imperfect-information games.

Method: A deterministic context engine analyzes the current game state and retrieves only relevant fragments from a layered skill library designed by human poker experts. These rule-based skills serve as a structured action-grounding interface that constrains the LLM's action choices to reasonable moves.

Result: Against GTOWizard benchmark, GPT-5.5 XHigh achieves -57±21 mbb/hand, Claude Opus 4.6 achieves -80±29 mbb/hand, and Claude Opus 4.7 achieves -87±64 mbb/hand, reducing losses by 49-61% over default-prompt baselines and outperforming Slumbot.

Conclusion: Rule-based skills alone and LLMs alone are insufficient, but their combination yields an agent requiring no training or solver access that competes with systems built on millions of core-hours. This is the first demonstration of an LLM achieving competitive performance in complex imperfect-information games without game-specific training or solver queries.

Abstract: Poker is a landmark challenge for artificial intelligence. The dominant approach relies on equilibrium solvers built on counterfactual regret minimization, requiring millions of core-hours of training. Large Language Models (LLMs) possess extensive poker knowledge but perform far below solver-based agents when asked to play directly. Traditional rule-based poker agents are interpretable and training-free, but their strategic ceiling remains far below equilibrium play. We introduce \textbf{PokerSkill}, a training-free and solver-free framework that bridges this gap by using detailed rule-based poker skills as a structured action-grounding interface for LLMs. A deterministic context engine analyzes the current state and retrieves only the relevant fragments from a layered skill library, which is entirely designed by human poker experts, constraining the LLM's choice to reasonable actions. Against GTOWizard, a state-of-the-art GTO benchmark, GPT-5.5 XHigh with PokerSkill achieves $-57 \pm 21$ mbb/hand, Claude Opus 4.6 achieves $-80 \pm 29$ mbb/hand and Claude Opus 4.7 achieves $-87\pm 64$ mbb/hand, reducing losses by 49--61\% compared to default-prompt baselines and outperforming the strong bot Slumbot. Our key finding is that rule-based skills alone do not constitute a strong strategy, and LLMs alone cannot play well, but their combination yields an agent that requires neither training nor solver access yet competes with systems built on millions of core-hours of computation. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of an LLM achieving competitive performance in a complex imperfect-information game without game-specific training or solver queries. Code is available at https://github.com/lbn187/PokerSkill.

</details>


### [120] [VLA-Trace: Diagnosing Vision-Language-Action Models through Representation and Behavior Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30117)
*Haoyuan Shi,Xiancong Ren,Yingji Zhang,Qinfan Zhang,Jiayu Hu,Haozhe Shan,Han Dong,Jinpeng Lu,Yinda Chen,Yi Zhang,Yong Dai,Xiaozhu Ju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLA-Trace is a diagnostic framework that analyzes VLA models by tracing representation dynamics, causal control pathways, and behavioral manifestations. Applied to π0.5 and OpenVLA, it reveals distinct modality adaptation, multimodal routing strategies, and limitations in fine-grained semantic following during embodied control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how VLA models transform multimodal knowledge into embodied control remains an open challenge, motivating the need for a unified diagnostic framework.

Method: VLA-Trace combines (1) cross-modal and checkpoint-drift CKA to trace representation evolution, (2) attention knockout interventions to identify modality-specific control pathways, and (3) rollout-level behavioral probes to examine grounding, shortcut dependence, and semantic following.

Result: Two models exhibit distinct modality-specific adaptation dynamics during finetuning; they rely on different multimodal routing strategies and layer-wise dependencies for action decoding; VLA policies excel at visually grounded trajectory generation but are limited in fine-grained semantic following.

Conclusion: Future work should focus on representation-preserving adaptation, causal VLA circuits, and compositional semantic control.

Abstract: Understanding how Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models transform multimodal knowledge into embodied control remains an open challenge. We present VLA-Trace, a progressive diagnostic framework that analyzes VLA models through a unified evidence chain from representation dynamics to causal control attribution and behavioral manifestation. It specifically combines cross-modal and checkpoint-drift centered kernel alignment (CKA) to trace representation evolution, attention knockout interventions to identify modality-specific control pathways, and rollout-level behavioral probes to examine grounding, shortcut dependence, and semantic following. Experiments on $π_{0.5}$ and OpenVLA reveal three key findings. First, the two models exhibit distinct modality-specific adaptation dynamics during VLA finetuning. Second, they rely on different multimodal routing strategies and layer-wise dependencies during action decoding. Third, although VLA policies excel at visually grounded trajectory generation, they remain limited in fine-grained semantic following. These findings highlight future directions for representation-preserving adaptation, causal VLA circuits, and compositional semantic control.

</details>


### [121] [Enhancing Multi-Agent Communication through Attention Steering with Context Relevance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30136)
*Hongxiang Zhang,Yuan Tian,Tianyi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent-Radar is a training-free context management method for LLM-based multi-agent systems that uses temporal and spatial decay mechanisms to dynamically focus each agent's attention on relevant context, outperforming state-of-the-art methods by up to 7.64 absolute points across five benchmarks while remaining robust as the number of agents and interaction rounds increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based multi-agent systems suffer from performance degradation as conversation histories grow extremely long during collaborative reasoning, because relevant information becomes diluted by irrelevant context.

Method: Agent-Radar is a training-free context management method that dynamically steers each agent's attention toward relevant context using a novel temporal and spatial decay mechanism.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods across five benchmarks with gains up to 7.64 absolute points; remains effective and robust as the number of agents and interaction rounds increases.

Conclusion: Core components in Agent-Radar are crucial to performance and generalizable across different settings.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems have demonstrated remarkable performance on complex tasks through collaborative reasoning. However, these systems tend to rapidly accumulate extremely long conversation histories during interaction. As conversations lengthen, relevant information is increasingly diluted by irrelevant context, leading to degraded performance. In this work, we present Agent-Radar, a training-free context management method that dynamically steers each agent's attention toward relevant context with a novel temporal and spatial decay mechanism. Our experiments demonstrate that Agent-Radar outperforms state-of-the-art methods across five different benchmarks, yielding gains of up to 7.64 absolute points. Furthermore, our analysis shows that Agent-Radar remains effective and robust as the number of agents and interaction rounds increases. Finally, the ablation study shows that core components in Agent-Radar are crucial to performance and generalizable in different settings.

</details>


### [122] [AgentSchool: An LLM-Powered Multi-Agent Simulation for Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30144)
*Yulei Ye,Wenhao Li,Zhong Wen,Yunshu Huang,Yichen Hu,Zifan Wei,Yige Wang,Xinyu Xie,Haoxuan Yang,Yanjun Huang,Ruijia Li,Hong Qian,Yu Song,Bo Jiang,Bingdong Li,Lijun Li,Bo Zhang,Pinlong Cai,Xingcheng Xu,Shuangye Chen,Xia Hu,Liang He,Aimin Zhou,Jingjing Qu,Jing Shao,Xiangfeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentSchool is an LLM-driven multi-agent educational simulator that models learning as state transition, featuring cognitively growable student agents with knowledge graphs and misconceptions, adaptive teacher agents using ZPD, and a configurable scenery generator, producing realistic academic and social classroom dynamics while serving as a testbed for long-horizon memory, multi-agent coordination, and institutional reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Validating educational AI in real classrooms is intractable due to irreversible learner development, slow trials, ethical constraints, and institutional lock-in; existing LLM-based simulators merely reproduce persona-conditioned role-play and structurally penalize pedagogical innovation.

Method: AgentSchool couples growable student agents (weighted subject knowledge graphs, thinking-workflow pools, explicit misconceptions) with adaptive teacher agents that plan, scaffold, and reflect along the Zone of Proximal Development, embedded in a configurable scenery generator for formal/informal learning fields and a multi-scale simulator decoupling interaction scale, temporal granularity, and simulation duration.

Result: Structured student agents produce more differentiated mastery and misconception traces than baseline simulators; teacher-agent comparisons show ZPD-informed backbone-dependent adaptation patterns; AgentSchool generates plausible social classroom dynamics including peripheral participation, clique formation, aggressor-induced cohesion, and opinion-leader emergence.

Conclusion: AgentSchool serves both as an educational research instrument for modeling learning as state transition and as a socially meaningful testbed for long-horizon memory, multi-agent coordination, and future institutional reasoning under organizational pressure.

Abstract: Despite the rapid deployment of LLMs into classrooms, validating educational AI remains uniquely intractable: interventions act on developing learners whose cognitive and social trajectories are irreversibly shaped, while real-world trials are slow, ethically constrained, and institutionally locked. LLM-based educational simulators have emerged as a potential remedy, but many still collapse learning into persona-conditioned role-play and, when optimized only to reproduce existing classrooms, can structurally penalize the institutional novelty that pedagogical reform requires. In this work, we introduce AgentSchool, an LLM-driven multi-agent simulator that models learning as state transition rather than prompted behavior. AgentSchool couples cognitively growable student agents -- equipped with weighted subject knowledge graphs, thinking-workflow pools, and explicit misconceptions -- with adaptive teacher agents that plan, scaffold, and reflect along the Zone of Proximal Development, embedded in a configurable scenery generator that situates instruction within both formal and informal learning fields, and a multi-scale simulator that decouples interaction scale, temporal granularity, and simulation duration. Experiments show that structured student agents produce more differentiated mastery and misconception traces than a baseline simulator, while teacher-agent comparisons show backbone-dependent patterns consistent with ZPD-informed adaptation. Further, AgentSchool generates plausible traces of peripheral participation, clique formation, aggressor-induced cohesion, and opinion-leader emergence consistent with classroom social theories. Beyond its role as an educational research instrument, AgentSchool frames education as a socially meaningful testbed for long-horizon memory, multi-agent coordination, and future institutional reasoning under organizational pressure.

</details>


### [123] [Anchorless Diversification for Parallel LLM Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30150)
*Fares Nabil Ibrahim,Nafis Saami Azad,Raiyan Abdul Baten*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies inference-time controls for diversifying candidate-idea pools generated by LLMs in creative tasks, finding that anchorless methods like semantic direction stratification outperform anchored methods (self-, peer-, representative-anchor) in diversity-quality-compute trade-offs, while population-referential divergence offers a strong low-cost baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to generate diverse candidate ideas for creative tasks, but it's unclear whether anchorless diversification methods can rival those dependent on observed seed ideas, motivating a systematic comparison.

Method: The authors compare independent generation and semantic direction stratification (anchorless) against self-, peer-, and representative-anchor baselines (anchored) under neutral and population-referential divergent instructions across three creative task families.

Result: Population-referential divergence (low-cost) increases semantic diversity while preserving quality proxies. Semantic direction stratification achieves the best diversity-quality-compute frontier with a single planning call. Anchored regeneration is strong in final-pool diversity but loses advantage under full-pipeline token accounting.

Conclusion: Anchorless methods (especially semantic direction stratification) are practical and effective baselines for open-ended LLM ideation, rivaling anchored approaches when compute costs are fully accounted for.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used to generate candidate-idea pools for creative tasks where broad exploration is valuable. Parallel inference can be attractive in this setting when it broadens the pool while retaining quality and cost efficiency. We study inference-time controls for candidate-pool diversification, asking whether anchorless methods can rival methods that depend on observed seed ideas. Across three creative task families, we compare independent generation and semantic direction stratification with self-, peer-, and representative-anchor baselines, under neutral and population-referential divergent instructions. Population-referential divergence is a strong low-cost baseline, increasing semantic diversity while preserving quality proxies. Semantic direction stratification is stronger: a single planning call organizes generations across broad semantic directions, yielding the best diversity--quality--compute frontier. Anchored regeneration can be strong in final-pool diversity, but its advantage shrinks under full-pipeline token accounting. These results establish practical anchorless baselines for open-ended LLM ideation.

</details>


### [124] [Temporal Stability and Few-Shot Prompting in Math Task Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30151)
*Danielle S. Fox,Brenda L. Robles,Elizabeth DiPietro Brovey,Christian D. Schunn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This longitudinal study examined how AI tools (Gemini and Coteach) classify cognitive demand of math tasks using the TAG framework. Version updates alone showed mixed results (Gemini stable at 58%, Coteach dropped from 75% to 50%), but few-shot prompting significantly improved both (Gemini to 67%, Coteach back to 75%), showing prompt engineering is more reliable than passive model updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether AI tools' ability to classify cognitive demand of mathematics tasks changes with model version updates and few-shot prompting, addressing the need for reliable AI integration in educational contexts.

Method: Longitudinal study testing Gemini (general-purpose) and Coteach (education-specific) AI tools on classifying math tasks using the Task Analysis Guide (TAG). Models were tested at baseline, after version updates, and after few-shot prompting with two exemplar tasks per cognitive demand category.

Result: Version updates: Gemini accuracy remained stable at 58%, Coteach decreased from 75% to 50%. Few-shot prompting: Gemini improved to 67%, Coteach recovered to 75% accuracy.

Conclusion: Prompt engineering techniques produce larger and more reliable effects than passive model improvements; version updates may not always improve performance on specialized educational tasks. This has implications for AI tool selection, evaluation, and implementation by educators and researchers.

Abstract: As AI tools become increasingly integrated into educational contexts, questions arise about both their stability over time and their responsiveness to prompt engineering techniques. This longitudinal study focused on different AI tools' ability to use the Task Analysis Guide (TAG; Stein \& Smith, 1998) to classify the cognitive demand of mathematics tasks. In particular, it examined whether this classification ability changed with (1) model version updates over time and (2) few-shot prompting using exemplar tasks. We tested a general-purpose AI tool (Gemini) and an education-specific AI tool (Coteach). The specific tools were selected because of their relatively high performance on relevant published benchmarks and prior task-specific tests. Models were tested at baseline, retested with model version updates, and then tested again using few-shot prompting (two exemplar tasks for each cognitive demand category). Results revealed that newer model versions alone produced mixed effects: Gemini's accuracy remained stable at 58\%, while Coteach's accuracy decreased from 75\% to 50\%. However, few-shot prompting improved both models' performance: Gemini increased to 67\% and Coteach recovered to 75\% accuracy. These findings demonstrate that prompt engineering techniques can have larger and more reliable effects than passive model improvements, and that version updates may not always improve performance on specialized educational tasks. The study has important implications for how educators and researchers should approach AI tool selection, evaluation, and implementation in educational contexts.

</details>


### [125] [Meta-Cognitive Memory Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30159)
*Ziyan Liu,Zhezheng Hao,Yeqiu Chen,Hong Wang,Jingren Hou,Ruiyi Ding,Yongkang Yang,Wence Ji,Wei Xia,Feng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MMPO, a method that optimizes memory policies in LLM agents by using Belief Entropy—a self-supervised proxy that measures epistemic uncertainty about latent task states—to provide fine-grained supervision on intermediate summaries, outperforming existing methods on long-horizon tasks and scaling to 1.75M-token contexts with 97.1% performance retention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-augmented LLM agents optimize memory policies using outcome-based reinforcement learning, which fails to localize where intermediate memory quality degrades. Ambiguous recursive summaries accumulate semantic noise, exacerbating belief deviation and derailing long-horizon reasoning.

Method: Introduces Belief Entropy, a self-supervised proxy measuring model uncertainty about latent task states given current memory, and proposes Metacognitive Memory Policy Optimization (MMPO) that provides fine-grained, memory-specific supervision by penalizing summaries inducing high epistemic uncertainty, beyond sparse outcome-based signals.

Result: MMPO consistently outperforms existing methods on diverse long-horizon tasks, maintaining 97.1% performance even when scaled to 1.75M-token contexts.

Conclusion: Memory optimization should focus on the clarity of belief induced by intermediate summaries rather than merely trajectory-level success, and Belief Entropy serves as an effective self-supervised signal for this purpose.

Abstract: Memory-augmented LLM agents tackle complex long-horizon tasks by recursively summarizing interaction trajectories into compact memory. However, existing approaches typically train these memory policies using outcome-based reinforcement learning, failing to localize where intermediate memory quality degrades. As interactions unfold, ambiguous recursive summaries progressively discard task-relevant information and introduce semantic noise. This exacerbates belief deviation, obscuring the agent's estimate of the latent task state and ultimately derailing long-horizon reasoning. We therefore argue that memory optimization should focus not merely on trajectory-level success, but on the clarity of the belief induced by intermediate summaries. To this end, we introduce Belief Entropy, a self-supervised proxy that probes how uncertain the model remains about the latent task state given its current memory. Based on this proxy, we propose Metacognitive Memory Policy Optimization (MMPO). Instead of relying only on sparse outcome-based signals, MMPO provides fine-grained, memory-specific supervision via explicitly penalizing summaries that induce high epistemic uncertainty. Experiments show that MMPO consistently outperforms existing methods on diverse long-horizon tasks, maintaining 97.1% performance even when scaled to 1.75M-token contexts.

</details>


### [126] [BioRefusalAudit: Auditing Biosecurity Refusal Depth Using General and Domain-Fine-Tuned Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30162)
*Caleb DeLeeuw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether language model refusals of hazardous biology prompts are structurally sound (i.e., robust to prompt variations) or brittle, finding that no model cleanly discriminates benign from hazardous content, with models exhibiting hedging, format-dependent refusal collapse, over-refusal of benign biology, and refusal driven by legality/culture rather than actual CBRN hazard. It introduces a divergence score D to compare surface responses to internal SAE feature activations, showing preliminary activation-level audit signals that behavioral evaluation misses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard biosecurity evaluations only check whether models produce hazardous output, but neglect whether refusals themselves are robust: a refusal that disappears under a slight prompt reformatting, token limit change, or framing shift is structurally unsound and fails its safety purpose. This paper aims to expose that gap.

Method: Evaluated five language model architectures (Gemma 2 2B-IT, Gemma 4 E2B-IT, Qwen 2.5 1.5B, Phi-3-mini, Llama 3.2 1B) across 75 prompts with variations in framing, formatting, and output length. Tested a panel of Schedule I but non-toxic compounds to probe refusal drivers. Introduced a divergence score D comparing surface response labels to internal SAE feature activations, computed on Gemma 2 2B-IT (Gemma Scope 1) and Gemma 4 E2B-IT (author-trained bio SAE). Released two domain-fine-tuned Gemma 2 SAEs.

Result: No model cleanly discriminated benign from hazard. Gemma 2 2B-IT never genuinely refused (hedging only). Gemma 4 E2B-IT refused 65/75 with chat template, 0/75 without. Both collapsed to 0% refusal under 80-token cap. Qwen 2.5 1.5B and Phi-3-mini over-refused 83-87% of benign biology. Llama 3.2 1B showed a 61-point tier gradient. Schedule I non-toxic compounds were refused at rates exceeding genuine hazards. Divergence score D showed comply/refuse separation with 0.647-point gap and zero overlap (n=75) on Gemma 4, though preliminary.

Conclusion: Refusal behaviors across architectures are brittle, format-dependent, and often driven by legality/culture rather than actual hazard. Activation-level auditing via SAE-based divergence scores can surface failure modes invisible to behavioral evaluation, but results are preliminary with narrow catalog, within-sample calibration, and Gemma-only SAE coverage.

Abstract: Biosecurity evaluations of language models typically ask whether models produce hazardous output. This paper asks a complementary question: when a model refuses, is that refusal structurally sound, or does it disappear under modest changes to prompt framing, formatting, or output length? Across five architectures, no model cleanly discriminated benign from hazard. Gemma 2 2B-IT never genuinely refused across 75 prompts, hedging on every hazard-adjacent query. Gemma 4 E2B-IT refused 65/75 prompts with chat-template formatting and 0/75 without it. Both Gemma models collapsed to 0% under an 80-token cap. Qwen 2.5 1.5B and Phi-3-mini over-refused, flagging 83-87% of benign biology as hazardous. Llama 3.2 1B showed the only meaningful tier gradient (61-point spread). To probe what drives such over-refusal, we tested a panel of Schedule I but biologically non-toxic compounds (notably psilocybin cultivation, with FDA Breakthrough Therapy status). Some models refused these at rates exceeding genuinely hazardous biology, suggesting refusal tracks legality and cultural salience over CBRN hazard. To measure the internal side, we introduce a divergence score D comparing a model's surface response label to its internal sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature activations. Full D was computed on Gemma 2 2B-IT (Gemma Scope 1) and Gemma 4 E2B-IT (author-trained bio SAE). Two fine-tuned Gemma 2 domain SAEs were released. On Gemma 4, comply and refuse responses separated by a 0.647-point gap with zero overlap (n=75), though this is preliminary, with a narrow catalog, within-sample calibration, and Gemma-family-only SAE coverage. Built over one hackathon weekend on consumer hardware (GTX 1650 Ti Max-Q, plus Colab T4 for SAE training), this preliminary evidence suggests activation-level auditing may surface failure modes invisible to behavioral evaluation, with substantial variation across architectures.

</details>


### [127] [Modularizing Educational LLM-Agency for Fostering Responsible Learning Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30187)
*Julius Gabelmann,Felix Jahn,Kevin Baum,Sophie van Rossum,Emely Wuenscher,Timo P. Gros,Verena Wolf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a modular agentic AI chatbot architecture for education that incorporates pedagogical guidance to address risks of monolithic LLM solutions, aiming for more responsible AI-assisted learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI chatbots in education risk negative effects like loss of transfer capabilities, critical thinking, and creativity due to LLMs not adhering to pedagogical concepts. Responsible deployment requires addressing these risks.

Method: The authors identify desiderata for responsible LLM-based educational systems, critique monolithic out-of-the-box solutions, and propose a modular agentic architecture with specific modules for different stages of exercise solving to incorporate targeted pedagogical advice.

Result: A conceptual modular agentic chatbot architecture that enables more controllable, transparent, and overseeable guidance through the learning process.

Conclusion: Modularizing agentic AI chatbots with pedagogical modules can contribute to more responsible AI use in education by addressing structural shortcomings of monolithic LLM solutions.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of AI chatbots in education will drastically change learning, making responsible deployment a critical concern. While large language models (LLMs) might have access to sources discussing insights from educational sciences, they are not particularly inclined to adhere to pedagogical concepts, risking negative effects on the learning process, such as a loss of transfer capabilities, critical thinking, or creativity. In this paper, we introduce an agentic AI chatbot architecture assisting students with exercise solving, specifically designed to contribute to more responsible AI use in education. We base our conceptual development on the identification of several desiderata for responsible LLM-based educational systems, argue for the structural shortcomings inherent in monolithic, out-of-the-box solutions, and instead suggest modularizing the agentic architecture. We propose specific modules for different stages of exercise solving, enabling incorporation of targeted pedagogical advice, guiding students through the learning process in a more controllable, transparent, and overseeable manner.

</details>


### [128] [Double-Edged Sword or Sharp Tool? Designing and Evaluating Triadic LLM-Teacher Collaboration for K-12 Writing at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30200)
*Canran Wang,Yuwen Yang,Zhen Wang,Ming Ma,Ding Yu,Chentai Wang,Keman Huang,Xiaoyong Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study on a triadic LLM-teacher-student collaboration system for K-12 writing education, validated through a large-scale two-year dataset, showing strategic labor division improves writing quality but with diminishing returns from excessive LLM linguistic expansion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the dual challenge of leveraging LLMs in K-12 education while mitigating teacher burnout, requiring an effective triadic collaboration mechanism among LLMs, teachers, and students.

Method: Developed a triadic collaboration system for K-12 writing learning, using a multidimensional evaluation framework grounded in Systemic Functional Linguistics, a suggestion trajectory tracing pipeline, and collected a large-scale empirical dataset (57,954 essays from 10,195 students across 120 schools over 2 years).

Result: The system improves writing quality via strategic labor division; LLM reduces teacher burnout as a generative engine, while teacher ensures feedback quality as pedagogical gatekeeper. Both are critical, but excessive linguistic expansion has diminishing marginal utility (ceiling effect).

Conclusion: Suggests a dynamically adaptive LLM-teacher collaboration model where the collaboration strategy should adjust as student proficiency increases to avoid diminishing returns.

Abstract: The double-edged sword of integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) requires an effective triadic collaboration mechanism among LLMs, teachers and students, especially for K-12 education. By developing a triadic collaboration system to support K-12 writing learning, a multidimensional evaluation framework grounded in Systemic Functional Linguistics and the suggestion trajectory tracing pipeline, this paper contributes a large-scale empirical dataset involving $57,954$ essays from $10,195$ students across $120$ schools over two years. Our findings confirm the efficacy of this system in improving writing quality through a strategic labor division: the LLM serves as a generative engine to mitigate teacher burnout, and the teacher acts as a pedagogical gatekeeper and bridge to guarantee feedback quality. While both LLM and teacher are critical for skill improvement, we uncover a ceiling effect where excessive linguistic expansion yields diminishing marginal utility. These suggest a dynamically adaptive LLM-teacher collaboration as student proficiency increases.

</details>


### [129] [When Should Models Change Their Minds? Contextual Belief Management in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30219)
*Haoming Xu,Weihong Xu,Zongrui Li,Mengru Wang,Yunzhi Yao,Chiyu Wu,Jin Shang,Yu Gong,Shumin Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies Contextual Belief Management (CBM) in language models—how they maintain, update, and isolate beliefs over long-horizon interactions. The authors introduce BeliefTrack, a closed-world benchmark with exact evaluation, and find that LLMs suffer from three failure types (Failed Stay, Update, Isolation). While prompting helps little, reinforcement learning with belief-state rewards reduces failures by 70.9%, and representation-level steering cuts failures by 46.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon interactions require language models to manage accumulating information, but existing models struggle with when to update, preserve, or ignore their belief state. The authors aim to systematically measure and address these failures.

Method: The authors introduce BeliefTrack, a closed-world benchmark with Rule Discovery and Circuit Diagnosis tasks, using a finite belief space and symbolic verifiers for turn-level evaluation. They test vanilla LLMs, explicit belief-tracking prompts, reinforcement learning with belief-state rewards, and representation-level steering.

Result: Vanilla LLMs show severe CBM failures; explicit prompting provides limited improvement. Reinforcement learning with belief-state rewards reduces failure rates by 70.9% on average, and representation-level steering reduces failures by 46.1% across two tasks.

Conclusion: CBM is a measurable and addressable challenge in LLMs. Reinforcement learning with belief-state rewards and representation-level steering are effective strategies for improving belief management in long-horizon interactions.

Abstract: Long-horizon interactions require language models to manage accumulating information: when to update their state, when to preserve their state, and what to ignore. We study this challenge as \textbf{Contextual Belief Management (CBM)}: maintaining a predicted belief state aligned with formal evidence while isolating task-irrelevant noise. To make CBM measurable, we introduce BeliefTrack, a closed-world benchmark spanning Rule Discovery and Circuit Diagnosis, where a finite belief space and symbolic verifiers enable exact turn-level evaluation. BeliefTrack diagnoses three failures: Failed Stay, Failed Update, and Failed Isolation. Across multiple LLMs, vanilla models exhibit severe CBM failures, while explicit belief-tracking prompts provide limited gains. In contrast, reinforcement learning with belief-state rewards reduces failure rates by 70.9\% on average. Further probing reveals latent belief-state dynamics behind these failures, and representation-level steering reduces failure rates by 46.1\% across two tasks\footnote{Code is coming soon at https://github.com/zjunlp/CBM.

</details>


### [130] [mcp-proto-okn: Natural-language access to open scientific knowledge graphs through the Model Context Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30283)
*Peter W. Rose,Benjamin M. Good,Amanda M. Saravia-Butler,Charlotte A. Nelson,James P. Balhoff,Yaphet Kebede,Patricia L. Whetzel,Christopher Bizon,Andrew I. Su,Sergio E. Baranzini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCP Server Proto-OKN is a Python-based Model Context Protocol server that enables AI assistants to query and integrate scientific knowledge graphs through natural language, providing graph routing, SPARQL execution, ontology expansion, and multi-graph querying capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To lower the barrier for cross-domain knowledge graph analysis for biomedical and scientific users by enabling natural language interaction with knowledge graphs.

Method: Implemented in Python using the FastMCP framework, providing features including graph routing, schema inspection, SPARQL execution, ontology expansion, multi-graph querying, and transcript generation.

Result: A functional MCP server that allows AI assistants to discover, inspect, query, and integrate scientific knowledge graphs via natural language.

Conclusion: The server successfully lowers barriers to cross-domain knowledge graph analysis for biomedical and scientific users by providing a natural language interface to knowledge graphs.

Abstract: MCP Server Proto-OKN (mcp-proto-okn) is a Python-based Model Context Protocol server that enables AI assistants to discover, inspect, query and integrate scientific knowledge graphs through natural language. The server provides graph routing, schema inspection, SPARQL execution, ontology expansion, multi-graph querying, and transcript generation, lowering the barrier to cross-domain knowledge graph analysis for biomedical and scientific users.
  mcp-proto-okn is implemented in Python using the FastMCP framework and is available at https://github.com/sbl-sdsc/mcp-proto-okn. Documentation, client configuration instructions, and example analysis transcripts are provided in the GitHub repository.

</details>


### [131] [ProjectionBench: Evaluating Scientific Hypothesis Generation in LLMs Under Progressive Information Disclosure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30284)
*A. J. Lew,Y. Cao,M. J. Buehler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark framework that progressively reveals paper details to LLMs, testing their ability to generate hypotheses aligned with ground-truth conclusions. Evaluated on GPT-5, GPT-5.4, Gemini 2.5 pro, and Gemini 3.1 pro across 45 papers, GPT-5.4 achieves 0.7 F1 even with minimal context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
